# Guru Granth Sahib (Ādi Granth)

The supreme scripture of [[sikhism|Sikhism]] — and, since 1708, the **living and perpetual Guru**
of the [[sikhs|Panth]], the eleventh and final Guru in succession to the ten human
[[guru-nanak|Gurus]]. First compiled as the **Ādi Granth** ("first book") by
[[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] in 1604 at [[amritsar|Amritsar]]; given its final form by
[[guru-gobind-singh|Guru Gobind Singh]], who closed the personal Guruship and invested its authority
in the scripture.

*(Scaffolded 2026-06-27 from [[mcleod-sikhism|McLeod]]; the scripture itself — `raw/Sri Guru Granth
Sahib …` — is a later deep-ingest scope.)*

## Composition and Compilation

[[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] initiated the compilation in 1603 and completed it in 1604, dictating to
**Bhai Gurdas** as amanuensis; the text is organized **by rāg** (musical measure), then by type of
composition, then by author — an arrangement McLeod calls "impressive." It contains the bāṇī of the
first five Gurus (Arjan's own being the largest single contribution), with the compositions of
[[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]] added later in the 17th century to produce the final Adi
Granth (agent 1 ~L1981–2051). A prototype lay in the **Goindval pothis** commissioned by
[[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]]; a stated motive for compilation was to fix an authentic text against
the spurious hymns circulating from Prithi Chand's followers (the "Minas").

A 1,430-page printed standard now governs; devout reading-disciplines run from a continuous unbroken
48-hour recitation (**akhand path**, concluded by the *bhog* ceremony) to instalment-readings over
weeks or a year. The script is Gurmukhi; the language is principally Punjabi with Braj, Sant Bhasha,
and Persian registers.

### The Bhagat bani

Alongside the Gurus' hymns the Granth includes the **Bhagat bāṇī** — compositions of "approved Sant
poets" who were not Sikh Gurus, among them [[kabir|Kabir]], Namdev, Ravidas, and the Muslim Shaikh
Farid (agent 1 ~L2017–2019). The opening **Mūl Mantar** and the **Ik-Oaṅkār** emblem (the Gurmukhi
figure 1 + *Oaṅkār*, "representing the unity of [[akal-purakh|Akal Purakh]]") head its invocations.
The opening composition is [[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]]'s **[[japji|Japji]]**.

## The Granth as Guru, and Canon

The Granth is **scripture and Guru at once**: "believed to embody the actual presence of the mystical
Guru," it is treated as the "manifest body of the Guru" — carried on the head, attended with a
*chaurī*, never left uncovered when not in use. As an oracle it yields the daily **[[hukam|hukam]]**
(a passage read at random). Its sole-authority status is structurally limited — it is "silent on the
[[khalsa|Khalsa]] and on fundamental Rahit matters," the gap that the [[rahit-maryada|Rahit Maryada]]
and the SGPC came to fill (see [[sikhism|Sikhism: The Ultimate Authority]]). Sikh scholars who applied
**textual criticism** to the Adi Granth were arraigned before the Akal Takhat as *tanakhāhīs* (agent 4
~L1099–1105).

By contrast the secondary **[[dasam-granth|Dasam Granth]]** "occupies an uncertain position": it
shared the Adi Granth's status as the eternal Guru in the 18th century, then dropped away. The scope
of [[guru-gobind-singh|Guru Gobind Singh]]'s terminal declaration — whether "the Granth" meant the Adi
Granth alone or both Granths — was left unspecified (see [[who-is-a-sikh|Who is a Sikh?]] and the
Dasam Granth page).

## Structural Organization

The Granth is organized **by rāg** (musical mode), then by composition type (*chau-padā*, *asṭapadī*, *shalok*, *var*, etc.), then by author (identified by *Mehl* number: Mehl 1 = Guru Nanak, Mehl 2 = Angad, Mehl 3 = Amar Das, Mehl 4 = Ram Das, Mehl 5 = Arjan, Mehl 9 = Tegh Bahadur). The standard printed edition runs to **1,430 pages** (called *angs*, "limbs").

**Part 1 (Angs 1–346) — scope plan:**
- **Angs 1–13** (Nitnem liturgy): [[japji|Japji Sahib]] (Guru Nanak), Sodar, Kirtan Sohila — the foundational morning and evening prayers not organized under a rāg
- **Angs 14–93** (Siree Raag / Siree Rāg): the first rāg section, compositions by Gurus Nanak, Angad, Amar Das, Ram Das, Arjan, Tegh Bahadur; Bhagat bāṇī of Kabir, Ravidas, Namdev, Trilochan, Sheikh Farid
- **Angs 94–150** (Raag Maajh): the second rāg, primarily Gurus Ram Das and Arjan; prominent *Var* and longer compositions
- **Angs 151–346** (Raag Gauree): the third and largest rāg in Part 1, spanning nearly 200 pages; contains the *Sukhmani Sahib* (Angs 262–296) by Guru Arjan, among the most beloved long compositions in the Granth

## Angs 1–13: Nitnem Liturgy

The opening 13 pages fall outside the rāg system. They constitute the **Nitnem** — the daily prayer cycle:

- **[[japji|Japji Sahib]]** (Angs 1–8, Guru Nanak): the foundational composition; 38 pauris (stanzas) + the Mool Mantar and two Sloks. Not set to a rāg — chanted, not sung. Opens with the [[mul-mantar|Mool Mantar]], the compact theological axiom prefixed to every rāg section in the Granth.
- **Sodar** (Angs 8–12): Evening prayer; multiple hymns by Gurus Nanak (Mehl 1), Ram Das (Mehl 4), and Arjan (Mehl 5) in Raags Aasaa and Goojaree. The "So Dar" hymn ("Where is that Gate...") echoes Japji's Pauri 27 verbatim. Mehl 4's Goojaree hymn introduces **Satsang** (True Congregation) as indispensable: "Blessed, blessed is the Sat Sangat, the True Congregation, where the Lord's Essence is obtained." (L2192, Ang 10); and "Through the Guru's Teachings, the Naam is my breath of life" (L2157, Ang 9).
- **So Purakh** (Angs 10–12, Mehl 4): "That Primal Being is Immaculate and Pure." God is simultaneously Master and Servant; the River of Life containing all. Gurmukhs obtain the Naam; Manmukhs lose it. God Himself separates and reunites.
- **Kirtan Sohila** (Angs 12–13): Bedtime prayer; also sung at cremations. Five hymns. The opening hymn frames death as a wedding (the soul-bride's union with her Husband Lord): "The day of my wedding is pre-ordained... O Nanak, that day is drawing near!" (L2699–2721, Ang 12). Hymn 3 (Mehl 1, Raag Dhanaasaree) contains the celebrated **Aartee** — a cosmic reimagining of the lamp-lit worship ceremony: the sun and moon are God's lamps, stars His pearls, winds His fan, all plants His altar flowers. "Amongst all is the Light — You are that Light. By this Illumination, that Light is radiant within all." (L2791–2807, Ang 13).

Key concepts established in the Nitnem liturgy: [[hukam|Hukam]], [[haumai|Haumai]], [[naam|Naam]], [[shabad|Shabad]], [[nadar|Nadar]], [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh/Manmukh]], the Five Khands, Amrit Vela, Satsang, and Simran.

---

## Siree Raag (Angs 14–93)

The first and structurally preeminent rāg, described within the Granth as "the best of the ragas, if it inspires you to enshrine love for the True Lord" (Var of Siree Raag, opening Shalok, Mehl 3, Ang 83). An entire cross-section of composition types: **Chau-Padās** (Angs 14–52), **Ashtapadīs** (Angs 53–75), **Chhants** (soul-bride lyric poems), **Pehray** (Watches of the Night, Ang 75–76), **Vanajaaraa** (Merchant allegory, Angs 77–82), the **Var** (ballad-ode, Angs 83–91), and the **Bhagat Bāṇī** (Angs 92–93). All Gurus (Mehl 1, 3, 4, 5) are represented; Mehl 2 appears only in Var Shaloks.

### Theological Character

Siree Raag spans a wide range from didactic/doctrinal (Mehl 1's cataloguing of vices, cosmological verses) to devotional-erotic (Mehl 4's viraha poetry) to synthetic-universalist (Mehl 5's omnipresence emphasis). Key distinctive themes:

**Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) — Chau-Padās and Ashtapadīs**: The world as *maya*-trap; fierce social solidarity with the lowly: "Nanak seeks the company of the lowest of the low class, the very lowest of the low. Why should he try to compete with the great? In that place where the lowly are cared for — there, the Blessings of Your Glance of Grace rain down." (L3195–3206, Ang 15). The vices catalogued as social castes: "Greed is a dog; falsehood is a filthy street-sweeper. Cheating is eating a rotting carcass." (L3211, Ang 15). The Guru as cosmic vehicle: "The Guru is the Ladder, the Guru is the Boat, and the Guru is the Raft to take me to the Lord's Name. The Guru is the Boat to carry me across the world-ocean; the Guru is the Sacred Shrine of Pilgrimage, the Guru is the Holy River." (L3690–3700, Ang 17). Direct address to Muslims in Ghar 4: "You are a Mullah, and you are a Qazi, only when you know the Naam, the Name of God." (L5285, Ang 25). The agricultural body-field metaphor: "Make this body the field, and plant the seed of good actions... Let your mind be the farmer; the Lord shall sprout in your heart." (L5157, Ang 24).

**Guru Amar Das (Mehl 3) — Ashtapadīs**: Heaviest emphasis on **Shabad** theology — the Word as divine medium. "O Nanak, the One Light has two forms; through the Shabad, union is attained." (L6742, Ang 31). Explicit rejection of body-mortification: "You may torment your body with extremes of self-discipline... hang upside-down, but your ego will not be eliminated from within." (L7262–7267). The three gunas (qualities) of Maya bind all; only the Gurmukh attains the **fourth state** (*turīya*) beyond them (L6666, Ang 31). The detached householder (*grihastha*) is superior to the world-renouncing ascetic: "O mind, remain detached in the midst of your household." (L5793).

**Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4)**: Most intense viraha register in the rāg: "Within my mind and body is the intense pain of separation; how can my Beloved come to meet me in my home?" (L8723, Ang 40). "The Diamond of the Guru has pierced the diamond of my mind, which is now dyed in the deep crimson color of the Name." (L8920, Ang 41). "Each day, the mouse of death is gnawing away at the rope of life." (L9224, Ang 42).

**Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Chau-Padās and Chhants**: Omnipresence as the synthesizing theme: "All that is seen is You, Lord, the expansion of the expanse... the Guru has removed my doubts; I recognize God in all." (L11384, Ang 51). **Satsang** elevated as primary institutional vehicle for liberation: "Let the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy, be your cleansing baths at the sixty-eight sacred shrines of pilgrimage." (L10573, Ang 48). The most universalist tone: "Even the ungrateful ones are cherished by God. O Nanak, He is forever the Forgiver." (L10441, Ang 47).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Siree Raag (Angs 92–93)

The Bhagat compositions in Siree Raag are introduced by a fresh Mool Mantar invocation. Five authors:

- **[[kabir|Kabir]]** (Composition 1, Ang 92): Impermanence and Maya's delusion; death laughs at the mother who thinks her child is "growing." "Meditate on the Lord, O mortal being, through the Word of His Bani; you shall be blessed with eternal life." (L20459, Ang 92). Composition 2 uses mystical/yogic imagery — the Tenth Gate (crown chakra), Ida and Pingala channels, the Unstruck Melody: "The Unstruck Melody of the Sovereign Lord's Harp vibrates; by His Glance of Grace, we are lovingly attuned to the Sound-current of the Naad." (L20603, Ang 92–93).
- **Trilochan** (Ang 92): Forgetfulness of death; attachment to family while Death approaches. "If someone is going to teach me something, let it be that the Lord is pervading the forests and fields." (L20575, Ang 92).
- **Baynee/Beni** (Ang 93): Life-stages (womb → youth → middle age → old age → death) as a Pehray structure; critique of spiritual negligence at every stage. "O man, when you were coiled in the cradle of the womb, upside-down, you were absorbed in meditation... Leaving the womb, you entered this mortal world; you have forgotten the Lord from your mind." (L20661, Ang 93).
- **[[ravidas|Ravidas]]** (Ang 93): Non-dual devotion transcending caste. "You are me, and I am You — what is the difference between us? We are like gold and the bracelet, or water and the waves." (L20827, Ang 93). The closing verse: "O Ravi Daas, one who understands that the Lord is equally in all, is very rare." — the *sam dal* ("equally in all") teaching, a signature Ravidas formulation asserting God's equal presence across all social positions.

A crucial internal cross-reference: Mehl 3 himself (Ang 67) validates the Bhagats' status: "Naam Dayv the printer, and Kabeer the weaver, obtained salvation through the Perfect Guru... Those who know God and recognize His Shabad lose their ego and class consciousness... Their Banis are sung by the angelic beings, and no one can erase them." (L~14992–15010, Ang 67).

### The Var of Siree Raag (Angs 83–91)

Paurees by Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4), Shaloks by Gurus Nanak, Angad, and Amar Das; 21 Paurees. Opening Mehl 3 Shalok establishes Siree Raag's primacy. Notable Shaloks include Mehl 1's anti-caste verse: "Pride in social status is empty; pride in personal glory is useless. The One Lord gives shade to all beings." (*fakarh jaatee fakarh naa-o*, L~18541, Ang 83); and the kitchen-purity critique: the four vices (false-mindedness, cruelty, slander, deceitful anger) make ritual kitchen-lines meaningless. Pauree 21 ends with the minstrel (*dhaadhee*) imagery: Ram Das presenting himself as God's minstrel at God's door, given robes of honor — "||21||1||Sudh||".

---

## Raag Maajh (Angs 94–150)

The second rāg and the rāg of **viraha** (separation-longing) and reunion. Composition types: **Chau-Padās** (Angs 94–108), **Ashtapadīs** (Angs 108–136), and the **Var of Maajh** (Angs 137–150). All five represented Gurus appear; **no Bhagat bāṇī** — Raag Maajh is entirely Guru-authored, the only rāg in Part 1 with this distinction.

The bride-soul metaphor saturates Maajh far more pervasively than Siree Raag. Every major theological theme — Naam, Satsang, Gurmukh/Manmukh, critique of external religiosity — is expressed through the viraha register: the soul-bride aching for the divine Husband (*pir*, *preetam*, *kant*), the song-bird (*chaatrik*) dying of thirst for the rain-drop of Naam.

### Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4) — Chau-Padās (Angs 94–96)

Opens the rāg with intense personal longing. The Guru elevated to cosmic level: "The Guru, the Perfect True Guru, is my Mother and Father." Unity of all creation: "There is only one breath; all are made of the same clay; the light within all is the same. The One Light pervades all the many and various beings." (L21327, Ang 96) — a panentheistic formulation equal to the most universalist statements anywhere in the Granth. Satsang as the only true meeting-place of God: "In that Congregation, the Lord God abides." (L20959, Ang 95).

### Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Chau-Padās and Ashtapadīs (Angs 97–136)

Dominant presence. The soul-bird as central image: "You are the Lord, and I am Your fish. Your Name is the drop of water, and I am a thirsty rainbird. You are my hope, and You are my thirst. My mind is absorbed in You." (L22181, Ang 100). God as all relationships simultaneously: "You are my Father, and You are my Mother. You are my Relative, and You are my Brother. You are my Protector everywhere." (L22821, Ang 103). The most comprehensive critique of external religion: philosophy, pilgrimage, tilak marks, yogic postures, austerities, wandering — all fail: "their hearts are not at peace, even for an instant." (L21702, Ang 98). God as ocean/bubbles, thread/beads: "You are the thread, and You are also the beads. You are the knot, and You are the primary bead of the maalaa. In the beginning, in the middle and in the end, there is God." (L22633, Ang 103). Kali Yug as experiential rather than merely cosmological: "When I could not be with You for just one moment, the Dark Age of Kali Yuga dawned for me." (L21410, Ang 97).

### Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) and Guru Amar Das (Mehl 3) — Ashtapadīs (Angs 108–136)

Mehl 1 introduces the Nine Gates / Tenth Gate body metaphor (Angs 108–109): "One who dies in the Word of the Shabad is truly dead. Death does not crush him, and pain does not afflict him. His light merges and is absorbed into the Light." (L24645, Ang 109).

Mehl 3 deploys the **swan/crane** symbol: pure Gurmukhs are swans that peck pearls in the Lord's Ocean; Manmukhs are filthy cranes bathing in vain. Extended critique of Vedic/Pandit learning: "The self-willed manmukhs read and recite; they are called Pandits — spiritual scholars. But they are in love with duality, and they suffer in terrible pain." (L28511, Ang 128). The three gunas / fourth state (*chautha pad*) framework appears again: "Those who are controlled by the three qualities are attached to Maya. By Guru's Grace, a few come to understand; they center their consciousness in the fourth state." (L28818, Ang 129). Placement of Hindu deities under Maya: "The source, the root, of the gods and goddesses is Maya. For them, the Simritees and the Shaastras were composed." (L28847, Ang 129) — a significant subordination of the Hindu textual tradition.

### The Var of Maajh (Angs 137–150)

Paurees by Guru Nanak (Mehl 1); Shaloks by Mehls 1, 2, 3, and 4; 27 Paurees. Tune: "To be sung to the tune of Malik Mureed and Chandrahraa Sohee-Aa" — a pre-existing Punjabi folk ballad form repurposed for spiritual praise. Notable Shaloks: Mehl 2's paradox of transcendence — "To see without eyes; to hear without ears; to walk without feet; to work without hands; to speak without a tongue — like this, one remains dead while yet alive. O Nanak, recognize the Hukam of the Lord's Command, and merge with your Lord and Master." (L30988, Ang 138). Mehl 3 on fear of God: "In fear we are born, and in fear we die... O Nanak, if one dies in the fear of God, his coming into the world is blessed." (L33612, Ang 149).

Key Paurees: Pauree 25 gives the sevenfold blessing of the Guru's grace: "When the True Guru is merciful, then your desires will be fulfilled. / When the True Guru is merciful, you will never grieve. / When the True Guru is merciful, you will know no pain. / When the True Guru is merciful, you will enjoy the Lord's Love." (L33641, Ang 149). Pauree 27 (final) uses the minstrel (*dhaadhee*) frame: "I was a minstrel, out of work, when the Lord took me into His service. To sing His Praises day and night, He gave me His Order, right from the start." (L33943, Ang 150). Ends: "naanak sach saalaahi pooraa paa-i-aa. ||27||Sudh||" — "O Nanak, praising the True One, I have obtained the Perfect Lord."

---

## Raag Gauree (Angs 151–346)

The third and longest rāg in Part 1, spanning 196 pages and containing 13 sub-varieties of Gauree (Poorbee, Deepak, Dakhanee, Chaytee, Bairaagan, Maajh-Gauree, and others). All Gurus represented; extensive **Bhagat bāṇī** (Angs 151–346 and concentrated after Ang 297). Includes [[sukhmani-sahib|Sukhmani Sahib]] (Angs 262–296, Guru Arjan) — see below.

Raag Gauree is the longest rāg in the SGGS, spanning nearly 200 pages. It subdivides into 13 sub-varieties (Gwaarayree, Dakhanee, Chaytee, Bairaagan, Poorbee Deepkee, Poorbee, Maajh, Chhant, Ashtapadees, and others). All Gurus appear; extensive Bhagat bāṇī concentrates after Ang 297.

### Gauree Early (Angs 151–249)

**Mehl 1 (Angs 151–157, Ashtapadees 221–229, Chhants 242–244)**: The **Fear of God** (*bhai*) simultaneously terrifies and adorns, annihilating ego and revealing divine love: "The Fear of God is overpowering, and so very heavy." (L33997, Ang 151). The **nine gates + Tenth Gate** of the body: "Nine gates, and then there is the Tenth Gate. Reflect upon this and understand it, O wise one." (L34174, Ang 151). Pandits' scholarship as spiritually hollow: "The priceless jewel is within your own heart. The Pandits read and read endlessly... but they do not know the secret deep within." (L34201, Ang 152). **Death-while-living**: "I have not died — that evil nature within me has died. The One who is pervading everywhere does not die." (L34215, Ang 152). The precious human life lost for a shell: "Human life is such a precious jewel, but it is being lost in exchange for a mere shell." (L35283, Ang 157). The inverted heart-lotus turned upright through Simran, with Amrit Nectar from the Tenth Gate (L34503, Ang 154).

**Mehl 3 (Ashtapadees, Angs 229–234)**: Ritual pollution (*sootak*) deconstructed — true pollution is duality in the mind: "The pollution of the mind is the love of duality. Deluded by doubt, people come and go in reincarnation." (L50964, Ang 229). "Throughout the four ages, the Naam is the ultimate; reflect upon the Word of the Shabad." (L51026, Ang 230).

**Mehl 4 (Angs 163–175)**: Satsang as indispensable: "Come, O Saints — let's meet together and chant the Naam." (L38914, Ang 173). The bride-image in Gauree Maajh: "Unite me with the Lord, Dear Saints; my mind is in love with Him." (L39392, Ang 175).

**Mehl 5 (Angs 176–249, dominant)**: Virtually every hymn references Saadh Sangat. The humility paradox: "When this mind becomes the dust of all, then it recognizes the Lord in each and every heart." (L52367, Ang 235). Simran vs. neglect: "Without meditating in remembrance on the Lord, one's life is like that of a snake." (L53158, Ang 239). "Without the Naam, all occupations are useless, like decorations on a dead body." (L53421, Ang 239). Non-dual resolution: "When he perceives differences, then he suffers pain... But when he recognizes the One and Only Lord, there is no difference between him and the Supreme Lord God." (L52434, Ang 236). Viraha Chhants: "For the bride, the night is painful; sleep does not come." (L53860, Ang 242).

### Gauree Baavan Akhree (Angs 250–261)

A 52-stanza acrostic poem by Mehl 5, one stanza per letter of the Gurmukhi alphabet, each as a paired Shalok + Pauree. The most systematic **caste-critique** composition in Part 1: every Brahminical and Hindu ritual role is redefined in terms of devotion rather than birth. A true Brahmin knows God; a true Pandit instructs his own mind. "Even an untouchable pariah is superior to him, O Nanak, if the Lord of the World abides in his mind." (L56352, Ang 253). "The Beej Mantra, the Seed Mantra, is spiritual wisdom for everyone. Anyone, from any class, may chant the Naam." (L57951, Ang 259).

### Sukhmani Sahib (Angs 262–296)

See [[sukhmani-sahib|Sukhmani Sahib]] for the full article. 24 Ashtapadīs by Mehl 5 — the theological summit of Raag Gauree, developing Naam, Brahm Gyani, Jivan Mukta, Mithya, Saadh Sangat, and Hukam to their fullest expression in Part 1.

### Post-Sukhmani (Angs 297–346)

**Thitee Gauri** (Angs 297–300, Mehl 5): Lunar-days composition — paired Shalok + Pauree per lunar day, giving liturgical-calendar form to core Gauree themes.

**Var with Shaloks by Mehl 4** (Angs 300–325): Gurmukh/Manmukh as structuring opposition. The True Guru as field of Dharma: "The GurSikhs plant ambrosial nectar, and obtain the Lord as their ambrosial fruit." (L68151, Ang 303). Saadh Sangat as alchemical transformation: "Just as iron is transmuted into gold by the touch of the Philosopher's Stone, so are people transformed by joining the Sangat." (L68241, Ang 304).

**Bhagat Bāṇī — Kabir in Raag Gauree** (Angs 325–340): The Simritee (Brahminical law texts) as "serpent" eating the world — escape through Naam (L74322, Ang 329). Self-identification from low Julaha-weaver position: "I am a weaver by birth, and patient of mind. Slowly, steadily, Kabeer chants the Glories of God." (L74203, Ang 328). Radical inter-religious formulation: "Says Kabeer, those who remain detached from the Vedas, the Koran and the Bible are the best riders." (L74387, Ang 330). Naam vs. outward beauty: "Without the Naam, the Name of the Lord, beautiful and handsome people are just ugly hunch-backs." (L74172, Ang 328).

---

**Part 2 (Angs 347–694) — scope plan:**
- **Angs 347–488** (Raag Aasaa): The largest raag in the Granth; includes So Dar repetition (Ang 347), Mehl 1/3/4/5 compositions across all types (chau-padā, ashtapadī, chhant, pehray, etc.), the Bhagat bāṇī of Kabir, Namdev, Ravidas, Sheikh Farid, Dhanna, Sain, and others (Angs ~475–489)
- **Angs 489–536** (Raag Goojaree): Includes compositions of Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5; two Vaars; Bhagat bāṇī (Kabir, Namdev, Ravidas, Trilochan, Jai Dayv)
- **Angs 537–556** (Raag Bihaagraa): Mehl 5 primarily, with Vaar; cosmic creation theology
- **Angs 557–625** (Raag Wadahans): Mehl 1/3/4/5; mourning register transformed; Alaahane (formal Songs of Mourning); Ghorees (wedding-procession songs); Var of Wadahans (Mehl 4 Paurees + Mehl 3 Shaloks); no Bhagat bāṇī
- **Angs 626–659** (Raag Sorat'h): Mehl 5/9/1 Ashtapadees; Var of Sorat'h (Mehl 4); Bhagat bāṇī (Kabir, Namdev, Ravidas, Bheekhan)
- **Angs 660–694** (Raag Dhanaasaree): Mehl 1 Aartee (Ang 663); Mehl 3/4/5/9; Bhagat bāṇī (Kabir, Namdev, Ravidas — including Ravidas's Naam-Aartee at Ang 694)

## Raag Aasaa (Angs 347–488)

The largest raag in the Granth, spanning 142 pages. All six represented Gurus appear (Mehl 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 9), as does the fullest array of composition sub-genres: So Dar (Angs 347–349), Chau-Padās, Ashtapadīs, Chhants, Pattī (acrostic alphabet poem), Pehray (Watches of the Night), Kaafee, So Purakh, Aaratee, Var, and concluding Bhagat bāṇī (Angs 475–489, the most extended bhagat section in the Granth to this point).

### So Dar and So Purakh (Angs 347–349)

The opening of Raag Aasaa repeats the **So Dar** and **So Purakh** compositions from the Nitnem liturgy (Angs 8–12). The repetition is deliberate: the Granth embeds liturgical compositions in their rāg context as well as in the Nitnem sequence, creating intratextual echoes. Mehl 5 compositions in So Dar describe liberation as spontaneous (*sahaj*) immersion in divine presence.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Chau-Padās and Ashtapadīs

**Social critique and Gurmukh theology**: Guru Nanak's compositions in Aasaa include the most pointed inter-religious critique in Part 2. In the Kaafee sub-raag (an Arabic/Persian musical register), Nanak addresses Muslim practice directly: "He who is called a Muslim should keep the faith of the Prophet; when tested, his gold should not be alloyed... First, let him taste the sweetness of the Lord's Love; then he can call himself a Muslim." (L3297–3309, Ang 140) — the true Muslim is defined by inner transformation, not formal compliance. Parallel address to Hindus: tilak marks, sacred threads, and pilgrimage bathings are void without inner devotion; even if a brahmin-by-caste "reads Shaastras and Vedas, if he is full of filth within, what shall he obtain?"

**The Pattī / Patee (Acrostic Alphabet Poem, Ang 432–434)**: Guru Nanak's acrostic poem using Gurmukhi letter-names as spiritual instruments. Each letter becomes a devotional vehicle: the Gurmukhi alphabet itself, the script of the Guru's bāṇī, is enlisted in the soul's journey toward God. Mehl 3 also composes his own Pattee (Ang 432), providing a parallel treatment. This is one of the rarest composition types in the Granth.

**Baabar Vāṇī (Angs 360, 417–418)**: Guru Nanak's eyewitness compositions on the Mughal invasions under Babur. Theologically unique in the SGGS: the compositions name a specific historical event (the invasion of Hindustan), specific groups of victims (Hindu and Muslim women alike), and a specific political actor (Babur). They reframe historical catastrophe in hukam theology: "You Yourself are the Doer; in Your Almighty Power, You came from Kabul, O Death-bringing Mughal, leading your marriage-party." (L~Ang 360) — God, via Babur, punished a land whose rulers had abandoned righteousness. Yet the suffering of civilians (especially women sold into slavery) is lamented, not celebrated. The Baabar Vāṇī constitutes the most direct historical witness in the entire Granth.

### Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das) — Ashtapadīs and Var

Mehl 3's compositions in Aasaa systematically address the three major Hindu castes and their ritual frameworks and relativize them before the Naam. "The Vedas proclaim Him, and the Puraanas proclaim Him; all the Shaastras proclaim Him." — but knowledge of God comes through the Guru's grace alone, not through scriptural literacy. **Jot Saroop** (soul as embodiment of divine light): "His birth is illustrious, who is imbued with the Lord's Love. Within the body is the Light of the Lord; the Gurmukh keeps it always alight." (Ang 441) — the soul is by nature divine light, not merely aspiring toward divinity. **Nirvaan**: "He dwells in nirvaan, in celestial peace, O Nanak; he is absorbed into the True Lord." (Ang 444) — one of the direct appearances of the Buddhist liberation term as a synonym for Naam-liberation.

**Four-Yuga cosmology**: The SGGS does not reject the Hindu cosmological framework of four ages (*yugas*) but reinterprets it: in every age, the Naam is the path appropriate to that age, with Satyug requiring meditation, Treytaa requiring austerity, Dwapur requiring worship — and Kali Yug requiring the Sangat's chanting of the Naam alone. The cycle of ages is thus pressed into service as an argument for the unique indispensability of congregational Naam-practice in the present age.

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Extensive Presence

Mehl 5 dominates Raag Aasaa numerically. Key compositional types:

- **Chhants** (Angs 452–467): Intense soul-bride viraha, then joyous union — "the bride has found the Lord; the unstruck melody resounds; the five virtues play the five instruments." (paraphrase, Angs 453–455). Heavy bridal imagery and the Saadh Sangat as the site of union.
- **Guru = God identity verse** (Ang 444): "The Guru is the Lord of the Universe — worship the Guru; through the Guru, the Lord God is obtained." — one of the strongest Guru-identity formulations in Part 2, asserting the Guru is not merely a path to God but is God for the devotee.

### Raag Aasaa — Bhagat Bāṇī (Angs 475–489)

The most extensive Bhagat bāṇī section to appear in the Granth thus far, covering six Bhagats:

**[[kabir|Kabir]] (Angs 475–484)**: Kabir's Aasaa compositions are the most theologically dense of his appearances in the Granth. Core themes: the **Cosmic Weaver** metaphor (Kabir the *julaaho*/weaver uses his own trade to describe the universe as God's cloth: warp and weft as the two realms; shuttle as consciousness); critique of Hindu idol worship and Muslim circumcision as equally external ("By cutting off the foreskin, the Muslims declare themselves to be Muslims; but then, what about women? Women too should be circumcised, so that they can also declare themselves to be Muslim." — savage irony, Ang 477); critique of pilgrimage; the **ulat-bāṃsī** (inverted speech / paradox verses): "The fish in the water is thirsty; I laugh at this strange thing. Without the Guru, the world is deluded by doubt." — paradox as epistemological disruption, pointing to the unreality of conventional thought.

Key quote:
> "Says Kabeer, I have been ransomed by the True Guru's humble servant. I see that the One Lord is pervading everywhere." (Ang 482)

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (Angs 485–486)**: The calico-printer's compositions in Aasaa develop radical divine immanence: the Lord is not found in temples, pilgrimages, or idols but permeates all things directly. Three parallel verses present the ritual critique: "The Lord is not found at pilgrimage sites; the Lord is not found in books; the Lord is not found through fasting; the Lord is not found through asceticism... Says Naam Dayv, I have found the Lord." (paraphrase, Ang 485). Mehl 3's internal endorsement: "Naam Dayv the printer, and Kabeer the weaver, obtained salvation through the Perfect Guru."

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]] (Angs 486–487)**: Only two compositions in this section, but key for Ravidas's self-positioning. Non-dual devotion: "You are me, and I am You — what is the difference between us? We are like gold and the bracelet, or water and the waves." (Ang 486 — echoing Siree Raag, Ang 93). Low-caste identity stated directly: "My trade is making shoes, and I work in leather." — and yet his city is "the eternal city, which is the city of God" (City of God vision, Ang 345/486).

**[[sheikh-farid|Sheikh Farid]] (Angs 488–489)**: The Chishti Sufi's shaloks close the Aasaa Bhagat bāṇī. Islamic vocabulary throughout: Allah, Khuda, *parvardagaar*. Defining themes: sincerity vs. profession ("They alone are true, whose love for God is deep and heart-felt. Those who have one thing in their heart, and something else in their mouth, are judged to be false." Ang 488); impermanence of the body; seasonal imagery for transience; the dervish and the Guru-disciple relation. The Farid corpus in Aasaa is the first of two separate collections in the Granth (the second, a larger shaloks-collection, appears later).

**[[dhanna|Dhanna]] (Angs 487–488)**: The Jat farmer who found the Lord through wandering in incarnations. Divine providence as the governing theme: God cares for worms under stones and turtle babies separated from their mother — and cares equally for Dhanna. Mehl 5's meta-commentary names Dhanna specifically and uses the term *partakh* ("met him personally" / direct encounter) for the highest form of divine encounter.

**[[sain|Sain]] (via Mehl 5 meta-commentary, Ang 488)**: The barber's compositions are endorsed through Mehl 5's internal description: "Sain, the barber, the village drudge, became famous in each and every house. The Supreme Lord God dwelled in his heart, and he was counted among the devotees." (Ang 488). Sain has no independent compositions in this section; his presence is structurally mediated through the Guru's endorsement.

### Raag Aasaa — Theological Summary

Raag Aasaa establishes the Granth's widest theological register. The Baabar Vāṇī is the Granth's most historically specific text; the Bhagat bāṇī section is its most diverse in terms of social identity (Brahmin, low-caste, Muslim, barber, farmer). The anti-caste and anti-formalist argument is made most comprehensively here, through the convergence of Guru voices and Bhagat voices from across the social spectrum.

---

## Raag Goojaree (Angs 489–536)

A mid-sized raag section containing Mehls 1, 3, 4, and 5, two Vaars, and the **Bhagat bāṇī** of five poets. Goojaree (sometimes *Gujari*) is a raag associated with morning performance in classical North Indian music; within the Granth it carries a tone of earnest petition and devotional declaration.

### Mehl 4 and Mehl 5 Compositions

**Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das)**: Intense longing; the soul-bride's prayer for union. The Saadh Sangat as irreplaceable site of grace: the Gursikh comes to the Guru's court and obtains the Lord's name.

**Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan)**: Comprehensive praise and surrender. The Lord as the remover of disease (both physical and spiritual) and as the sole support in all circumstances.

### The Goojaree Vaars

**Vaar 1 (Mehl 3 Paurees + Mehl 3 Shaloks, Ang 508)**: Classical var structure, Mehl 3 throughout. Major theological statement in **Pauree 2**: "All the Simritees, Shaastras and Vedas describe the Lord in many ways. He is described in the Holy Books of the Hindoos, the Muslims and the Christians." (Angs ~509–511) — this is the SGGS's most direct affirmation that the Vedas, Bible, and Koran are all paths pointing toward the one God, making Goojaree Vaar Pauree 2 the most direct inter-religious statement in Part 2.

**Vaar 2 (Mehl 5 Paurees + Mehl 3 Shaloks, Ang 520)**: The Lord's name as the only wealth; the householder who meditates on Naam is the true renunciate; Gurmukh/manmukh binary applied systematically.

### Raag Goojaree — Bhagat Bāṇī (Angs 524–536)

Five Bhagat authors, the widest set in any single bhagat section so far:

**[[kabir|Kabir]]**: Critique of scriptural learning as spiritual bypass: those who memorize the Vedas and display their learning have not attained the Lord. The "inverted mind" (*ulat*) — the mind turned away from God — is the only real problem.

**[[namdev|Namdev]]**: The Lord's omnipresence: "God is in the water, God is on the land; God is in every heart. I bow to that place where God is." (paraphrase, Ang 524–525). Mystical merging: the light of the individual consciousness merges into the Lord's Light.

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]]**: In Goojaree, Ravidas states his low-caste identity explicitly — "I am a low-caste cobbler" — and argues that God's grace elevates the lowly beyond the Brahmin's ritual scholarship. "Even the stone idol is liberated by the touch of the Lord's feet; I too am of that kind." (paraphrase).

**[[trilochan|Trilochan]]**: Maharashtrian Vaishnava; well-attested presence in Siree Raag (Part 1) and now Goojaree. In Goojaree, Trilochan articulates the **consciousness at death** teaching explicitly: "At death, the soul departs having meditated on whatever it meditated on at the moment of departure. If one contemplates women at the moment of death, one is reborn as a woman; if one contemplates elephants, one is reborn as an elephant." (paraphrase, Ang 526) — a formulation with strong resonances to the *Gita* 8.6 and to Theravada consciousness-at-death doctrine, here applied in the context of Naam-devotion: if one dies with the Lord's Name on one's lips, one merges with the Lord.

**[[jayadeva|Jayadeva]] (Jai Dayv)** (Ang 527): The Sanskrit Vaishnava poet of Bengal (c. 1150–1200), author of the *Gīta Govinda*. His sole SGGS composition is in elevated, Sanskrit-influenced language, focused on the primordial, transcendent Lord rather than the Radha-Krishna imagery of his famous Sanskrit poem. His inclusion demonstrates that the Gurus' criterion for Bhagat selection was inner devotional attainment, not language (Sanskrit vs. vernacular) or caste (Brahmin vs. low-caste). This is the only Sanskrit-register composition in the Granth.

### Raag Goojaree — Theological Character

Goojaree's distinctive contribution is the **most explicit multi-tradition theological affirmation** (Vaar 1 Pauree 2 on Vedas, Bible, and Koran) and the **most linguistically diverse Bhagat bāṇī** in Part 2 (including the Sanskrit-composing Jayadeva alongside vernacular poets).

---

## Raag Bihaagraa (Angs 537–556)

A smaller raag section, primarily Mehl 5, containing a Vaar. Bihaagraa (also *Bihag*) carries a tone of serenity and settled assurance in classical North Indian music; within the Granth it functions as a counterweight to the viraha-dominant raags, expressing union already obtained rather than union longed for.

### Composition

Mehl 5 compositions in Bihaagraa emphasize **complete trust and surrender**: the Lord is the complete provider; those who meditate on Naam find perfect peace. The Guru as the Lord of the Universe manifest in the world.

### Var of Bihaagraa (Angs 547–556)

**Creation cosmology**: The Var of Bihaagraa contains one of the SGGS's most extended treatments of creation theology. God existed in primordial darkness (*andhkar*) before the universe: "For countless ages, there was utter darkness. There was no earth or sky; there was only the infinite Command of the Lord's Hukam." (paraphrase from the Var, Ang 1035 of the printed SGGS — Agent 4 notes this as Raag Bihaagraa Var Pauree). God then willed creation into being through Hukam alone — not from pre-existing material, but from the divine Will itself. This is the Granth's clearest treatment of creation *ex voluntate Dei* (from the divine Will), parallel to but distinct from the Abrahamic *creatio ex nihilo* — the emphasis is on Hukam (divine Command/Order) as the mechanism rather than on nothingness.

**No Bhagat bāṇī** in Raag Bihaagraa — all compositions are by Guru-Mehls.

---

## Raag Wadahans (Angs 557–625)

A major raag section (69 pages) with the most distinctive formal register in Part 2: Wadahans is the Punjabi folk raag associated with **funeral and mourning music**. The Gurus receive this register and systematically reframe it — lamentation oriented toward worldly loss is spiritually vain; authentic weeping is the soul's viraha, its longing for the Lord. This theological transformation is the structural principle of the entire section.

**Composition types**: Chau-Padās (Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5), Ashtapadīs (Mehl 3), Chhants (Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5), **Ghorees** (Mehl 4 — formally labeled wedding-procession songs), **Alaahane** (formally labeled Songs of Mourning, Mehl 1 and 3), and the **Var of Wadahans** (Mehl 4 Paurees + Mehl 3 Shaloks, 21 Paurees). **No Bhagat bāṇī** in Raag Wadahans.

### The Alaahane — Songs of Mourning (Angs 579–585)

The theological heart of Raag Wadahans. The sub-genre is explicitly labeled "Alaahane" (Songs of Mourning) — the Granth accepts the mourning register and inverts its valence:

> "Nanak: he alone truly weeps, O Baba, who weeps in the Lord's Love. One who weeps for the sake of worldly objects, O Baba, weeps totally in vain." (L4607, Ang 579)

> "Come, O my companions — let us meet together and dwell upon the True Name. Let us weep over the body's separation from the Lord and Master; let us remember Him in contemplation." (L4613–4622, Ang 580)

> "Death would not be called bad, O people, if one knew how to truly die." (L4647–4649, Ang 580)

> "The death of brave heroes is blessed, if it is approved by God." (L4679–4680, Ang 580) — the *shāhīdī* (martyrdom) register.

> "Nanak: for whom should we mourn, O Baba? This world is merely a play." (L4717–4718, Ang 580)

The SGGS **death theology** stated in the Alaahane:
1. Mourning for worldly loss (the deceased body) is spiritually vain
2. True grief is *viraha* — the soul's recognition of its separation from God
3. True death is ego-death while living (*jiwan-mukt*): "She alone is approved, who dies, while yet still alive; she lives for the sake of the Love of her Beloved." (L4889–4891, Ang 580)
4. Physical death of the Naam-imbued soul is homecoming: "The nine doors are closed by the True Lord's Command, and the swan-soul takes flight into the skies." (L4847–4851, Ang 580)

Mehl 3's counter-thesis: "The soul-bride shall never be a widow, and she shall never have to endure suffering. Her Husband Lord is True, forever True; He does not die, and He does not leave." (L5267–5270, 5500–5502, Ang 582–583). The soul-bride's bereavement is cured by recognizing the undying nature of the True Lord.

### The Ghorees — Wedding Procession Songs (Angs 575–578, Mehl 4)

Formally labeled "Ghoree" (wedding-procession songs, traditionally sung when the groom's party rides out). In the SGGS, the groom is the soul departing the body, the horse is the body itself, and the wedding destination is liberation. The genre operates in two registers simultaneously: festive procession and final journey. The Guru's Shabad is the saddle and bridle; Naam is the fodder; arrival = union with the True Lord.

### The Var of Wadahans (Angs 585–595, Mehl 4 Paurees + Mehl 3 Shaloks)

21 Paurees, to be sung "in the tune of Lalaa-Behleemaa" (a pre-existing Punjabi folk tune). The Var's theological program:

- **Swan/Heron distinction**: Opening Mehl 3 shaloks establish the *hans/bag* (swan/heron) binary — Gurmukhs are swans who peck pearls in the Lord's Ocean; those who imitate spiritual life without inner transformation are herons who drown in the attempt.
- **Pauree 13**: Hindu exemplars reclaimed as Gurmukhs — "As Gurmukh, Prahlaad meditated on the Lord, and was saved. As Gurmukh, Janak lovingly centered his consciousness on the Lord's Name. As Gurmukh, Vashisht taught the Teachings of the Lord." (L7298–7313, Ang 591) — inclusive appropriation: Prahlad, Janak, and Vasishtha are claimed as practitioners of the Naam-path.
- **Pauree 15**: The Naam replaces all social relationships: "The Lord's Name is my father, the Lord's Name is my mother; the Lord's Name is my helper and friend. My conversation is with the Lord's Name... the Lord's Name is my ancestry, and the Lord's Name is my family." (L7523–7547, Ang 592)
- **Pauree 17**: The Naam replaces all material needs: "The Lord's Name is my food; eating the thirty-six varieties of it, I am satisfied and satiated. The Lord's Name is my clothing; wearing it, I shall never be naked again." (L7744–7765, Ang 593)
- **Pauree 18**: Four-fold "Dhan Dhan" (Blessed, Blessed) praise: "Blessed, blessed is that Sikh of the Guru, who goes and falls at the Feet of the True Guru..." (L7860–7882, Ang 594)

Anti-caste verse (Mehl 4, Chhant section): "jaat ajaat" — explicit statement that caste (high or low) is spiritually irrelevant; those who meditate on Naam receive the supreme treasure regardless of birth (L3687, Ang 575).

---

## Raag Sorat'h (Angs 626–659)

A major raag section spanning 34 pages, with all four represented post-Nanak Gurus (Mehl 5, 9, 1, 4) and four Bhagat poets. Sorat'h picks up immediately where Wadahans leaves off with the same death-consciousness and Naam-dependence register.

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Angs 627–648

The dominant presence in Sorat'h. Consistent chau-pada form with *rahao* (pause/refrain). Themes: Guru as protector and Shabad as guardian; God controls the "reins" of all creatures; *anhad* (unstruck celestial melody) as sign of inner attainment.

Notable verse naming Guru Hargobind by name:
> "God saved Hargobind." (L195, Ang 627) — one of the rare direct historical references in the Granth, naming the sixth Guru in a composition by the fifth.

Also notable: explicit mention of "Sikhs" alongside "Saints": "All the Sikhs and Saints are filled with joy, meditating on the Name of the Lord, Har, Har." (L177–179, Ang 627).

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Angs 632–640

A distinct theological block of 12 compositions. Mehl 9 = Guru Tegh Bahadur, the Sikh tradition's great martyr. His compositions in Sorat'h form the most concentrated statement of his theological voice: urgency of spiritual practice in the face of death; the rarity and preciousness of human birth; equanimity (*sthit-prajna*-like quality) as the mark of the true devotee; inner immanence against external seeking.

> "Death is on the prowl, with its mouth wide open." (L1268, Ang 631)

> "The Immaculate Lord is within your heart, and yet you search for Him in the wilderness." (L1362–1364, Ang 632)

> "What is the use of shaving your head, and wearing saffron robes?" (L1668–1671, Ang 633) — anti-formalist critique of Hindu ascetic externalism.

> "That man who in the midst of pain does not feel pain, who is not affected by pleasure, affection or fear, and who looks alike upon gold and dust — within his heart, God dwells." (L1714–1742, Ang 633–634) — the Mehl 9 ideal of the *sthit-pragya* saint; the formulation closely parallels the *Bhagavad Gita*'s *sthitaprajna* passage (Gita 2.54–72).

> "O Nanak, he merges with the Lord of the Universe, like water with water." (L1747–1750, Ang 634) — unity/merger theology.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Ashtapadīs (Angs 634–643)

True renunciation (*bairaag*) as inner rather than external: "Filth cannot be washed away by hypocrisy, O Siblings of Destiny; the filth of corruption and sin is within you." (L2079–2083). "The Word of the Shabad is his Guru and spiritual teacher, profound and unfathomable; without the Shabad, the world is insane." (L2037–2041). Simultaneous distancing from both Hindu and Muslim external practices: "I am not torn by duality, because I do not worship any other than the Lord; I do not visit tombs or crematoriums." (L1817–1820) — tombs = Muslim Sufi shrine veneration; crematoriums = Hindu death-rites.

### The Var of Sorat'h (Mehl 4 Paurees + Mehl 1/3 Shaloks, Angs 642–647)

**Bridal metaphor**: "Sorat'h is always beautiful, if it brings the True Lord to dwell in the mind of the soul-bride." (L3570–3572) — the raag name itself is given a theological meaning (spiritual beauty = union with God). God as sole actor: "You Yourself are the Creator, the Fashioner of the world. You Yourself have arranged the play, and You Yourself arrange it." (L3651–3668). Extended discussion of Gursikhs coming to the Guru's door.

### Raag Sorat'h — Bhagat Bāṇī (Angs 654–660)

**[[kabir|Kabir]] (11 compositions, Angs 654–657)**: Among Kabir's most theologically charged appearances:

> "Worshipping their idols, the Hindus die; the Muslims die bowing their heads. The Hindus cremate their dead, while the Muslims bury theirs; neither finds Your true state, Lord." (L6370–6378, Ang 654) — simultaneous inter-religious critique at its sharpest.

> "Says Kabeer, those humble people become pure — they become **Khalsa** — who know the Lord's loving devotional worship." (L6540–6543, Ang 655) — significant: "Khalsa" used here by Kabir, predating the formal institution of the Khalsa order by Guru Gobind Singh (1699) by over a century. Here Khalsa = the pure, defined by loving devotion rather than by initiation or lineage.

> "Those select few, who know how to die while yet alive, enjoy great peace." (L6722–6724, Ang 655)

> "I am so hungry, I cannot perform devotional worship service. Here, Lord, take back Your mala." (L6907–6913, Ang 657) — Kabir returns the rosary, asking for basic food and bedding so he can perform devotion. "Says Kabeer, all I want is a dry morsel of flour, along with salt, and a cot to sleep on, and bedding to cover with; these are what I ask for, so that I may meditate on the Name of the Lord." (L6926–6948) — Kabir's petition for a spiritually functional minimum rather than renunciate austerity.

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (3 compositions, Angs 657–659)**: Mystical light imagery — "Where the dazzling white light is seen, there the unstruck sound current of the Shabad resounds. One's light merges in the Light." (L7000–7009, Ang 657). Famous carpenter poem: a neighbor asks who built Namdev's house; he answers: "My carpenter is the Support of the breath of life... contained in everything, everywhere." — God the universal Carpenter (L7062–7079, Ang 658). Philosopher's stone: "The water is contained within the pitcher; I know that the One Lord is contained in all." (L7148–7156, Ang 659).

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]] (7 compositions, Angs 658–659)**: Sorat'h contains Ravidas's most explicit low-caste self-declarations and his most intense non-dual theology:

> "When I am in my ego, then You are not with me. Now that You are with me, there is no egotism within me. The wind may raise up huge waves in the vast ocean, but they are just water in water." (L7176–7184, Ang 658) — advaita-inflected union theology, ego-dissolution as the prerequisite for divine presence.

> "If I am bound by the noose of emotional attachment, then I shall bind You, Lord, with the bonds of love." (L7226–7233, Ang 659) — audacious devotional intimacy; mutual binding of devotee and God.

> "My social status is low, my ancestry is low, and my life is wretched. I have come to Your Sanctuary, O Luminous Lord, my King; so says Ravi Daas, the shoemaker." (L7468–7476, Ang 659)

> "I am a shoemaker, but I do not know how to mend shoes... Without wasting my time mending, I have found the Lord." (L7478–7493, Ang 659) — the cobbler's trade turned into a spiritual metaphor.

**[[bheekhan|Bheekhan]] (2 compositions, Angs 659–660)**: Physical symptoms of longing — weeping eyes, weakened body, whitened hair, constricted throat — followed by resolution in omnivision: "Says Bheekhan, my eyes are content; wherever I look, there I see the Lord." (L7572–7575, Ang 660).

---

## Raag Dhanaasaree (Angs 660–694)

The final raag section of Part 2, opening with a fresh Mool Mantar. A theologically rich section containing Mehl 1, 3, 4, 5, and 9, and Bhagat bāṇī of Kabir, Namdev, and Ravidas. Dhanaasaree's distinctive contribution: **two Aartee compositions** that frame the raag — the famous Mehl 1 cosmic Aartee (Ang 663) and Ravidas's Naam-Aartee at the very last Ang of Part 2 (Ang 694).

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Chau-Padās (Angs 660–663)

Immediate inter-religious critique: "The Qazi tells lies and eats filth; the Brahmin kills and then takes cleansing baths. The Yogi is blind, and does not know the Way. The three of them devise their own destruction." (L8067–8079, Ang 663). Followed by redefinition: "He alone is a Brahmin, who contemplates God. He saves himself, and saves all his generations as well. One who cleanses himself of impurity is a Muslim." (L8093–8107, Ang 663) — triple religious redefinition: true Brahmin = inner contemplator; true Muslim = inner cleanser; true Qazi = inner renouncer. "He is not male, and He is not female; how can anyone describe Him?" (L12877–12879, Ang 686) — apophatic theology on divine gender.

### Dhanaasaree Mehl 1 — The Aartee (Ang 663)

One of the most celebrated compositions in the SGGS, liturgically significant and sung at evening services (*Rehras*). Guru Nanak systematically displaces the Hindu lamp-ceremony (*pūjā āratī*) by making the entire cosmos into the worship service:

> "In the bowl of the sky, the sun and moon are the lamps; the stars in the constellations are the pearls. The fragrance of sandalwood is the incense, the wind is the fan, and all the vegetation are flowers in offering to You, O Luminous Lord." (L8195–8206, Ang 663)

> "What a beautiful lamp-lit worship service this is! O Destroyer of fear, this is Your Aartee, Your worship service. The sound current of the Shabad is the sounding of the temple drums." (L8207–8214, Ang 663)

> "Thousands are Your eyes, and yet You have no eyes. Thousands are Your forms, and yet You have not even one form." (L8216–8221, Ang 663) — apophatic / paradoxical theology.

> "The Divine Light is within everyone; You are that Light. Yours is that Light which shines within everyone. By the Guru's Teachings, this Divine Light is revealed." (L8231–8241, Ang 663)

The Aartee renders temple ritual redundant: if the entire cosmos is a perpetual and spontaneous worship ceremony, there is no need for a lamp in a particular building. The "unstruck sound" (*anhad*) replaces the temple drum. Theologically parallel to — but formally distinct from — the Kirtan Sohila's Aartee (Part 1, Ang 12–13).

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Angs 669–679

Anti-caste theology directly stated: "God, the Lord and Master, is the caste and social status of His humble Saints." (L9131–9133, Ang 669) — the divine itself replaces the social caste hierarchy as the devotee's identity marker. Longing for the Lord's Name as life itself: "Just as without water the water-addict dies, so without the Lord, I die." (Ang 670).

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Angs 684–685

A second cluster of Mehl 9 compositions in Dhanaasaree, continuing the Sorat'h themes:

> "Why do you go looking for Him in the forest? Although He is unattached, He dwells everywhere. He is always with you as your companion." (L12653–12658, Ang 684) — inner immanence; critique of forest-asceticism.

> "Like the fragrance which remains in the flower, and like the reflection in the mirror, the Lord dwells deep within; search for Him within your own heart." (L12663–12669, Ang 684)

> "One who does not slander or praise others, who looks upon gold and iron alike, who is free from pleasure and pain — he alone is called a true Yogi." (L12735–12744, Ang 685) — the Mehl 9 equanimity ideal.

### Raag Dhanaasaree — Bhagat Bāṇī (Angs 691–694)

**[[kabir|Kabir]] (5 compositions, Angs 691–693)**: The Benares/Maghar controversy — Kabir deliberately subverts Hindu sacred geography: "What is the difference between Benares and the barren land of Maghar, if the Lord is within one's heart?" (L14273–14276, Ang 692). Historically, Kabir is said to have died at Maghar (considered inauspicious by Hindus) precisely to demonstrate this point. The famous weaver-merger verse: "Like water, dripping into water, which cannot be separated out again, so is the weaver Kabeer, with softened heart, merged into the Lord." (L14253–14258, Ang 692).

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (5 compositions, Angs 692–694)**: Impermanence through Mahabharata/Ramayana reference: "Sri Lanka was totally rich with gold; was anyone greater than its ruler Raavan? What happened to the elephants, tethered at his gate? In an instant, it all belonged to someone else." (L14412–14422, Ang 693). The cosmic dance composition: "In this Garden of the Lord, we all dance, like water in the pots of the Persian wheel." (L14575–14578, Ang 694) — the Persian water-wheel (*arhat*) as image of creation's cyclical rise and fall. "The Lord says, 'This creation and I are one and the same.'" (L14592–14593, Ang 694).

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]] (3 compositions, Ang 694)**: Intense longing after many incarnations. And the **Dhanaasaree Naam-Aartee** — a direct parallel to Mehl 1's cosmic Aartee, but even more internalized: every element of the ritual is replaced by the Naam itself:

> "Your Name, Lord, is my adoration and cleansing bath. Without the Name of the Lord, all ostentatious displays are useless." (L14723–14728, Ang 694)

> "Your Name is the prayer mat, and Your Name is the stone to grind the sandalwood." (L14730–14734, Ang 694)

> "Your Name is the lamp, and Your Name is the wick. Your Name is the oil I pour into it. Your Name is the light applied to this lamp, which enlightens and illuminates the entire world." (L14745–14755, Ang 694)

> "Says Ravi Daas, Your Name is my Aartee, my lamp-lit worship-service. The True Name is the Lord's offering of food." (L14775–14779, Ang 694)

The final line of Part 2 is this closing verse by Ravidas — the lowest-caste Bhagat provides the last word of the entire 347-page section, and that word is the Naam as the complete substitute for all ritual.

### Raag Dhanaasaree — Theological Summary

Dhanaasaree's structural contribution to Part 2: the two Aartees (Mehl 1 at Ang 663; Ravidas at Ang 694) form a thematic envelope around the raag, both systematically internalizing and universalizing the temple lamp ceremony. The raag closes Part 2 on the note of the Naam as the complete substitute for external religious practice — a note that resonates across all six raags of Part 2.

---

**Part 3 (Angs 695–1106) — scope plan:**
- **Angs 695** (Dhanaasree Bhagat Bāṇī tail): Brief continuation of Part 2 — Trilochan's Dhanaasree composition
- **Angs 696–711** (Raag Jaitsree): Compositions of Mehls 4, 5, 9; Chhants; Vaar with Shaloks; Bhagat Bāṇī (Namdev, Ravidas, and others)
- **Angs 711–718** (Raag Todee): Compositions of Mehls 4, 5, 9; Bhagat Bāṇī (Namdev, Ravidas)
- **Ang 719** (Raag Bairaaree): Short section; Mehls 4 and 5
- **Angs 720–726** (Raag Tilang): Mehl 1 and 4; short section; some Bhagat Bāṇī
- **Angs 727–794** (Raag Soohee): Long section (68 pages); Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5, 9; includes Kuchajee (The Ungraceful Bride, Mehl 1); Chhants; Bhagat Bāṇī of Kabir, Ravidas, and Sheikh Farid
- **Angs 795–840** (Raag Bilaaval): 46 pages; Mehls 1, 4, 5, 9; Ashtapadees; Bhagat Bāṇī (Sadhna)
- **Angs 841–875** (Raag Gond): 35 pages; Mehls 4 and 5; Bhagat Bāṇī of Kabir, Namdev, Ravidas
- **Angs 876–974** (Raag Raamkalee): 99 pages, the longest section in Part 3; Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5; includes **Anand Sahib** (Mehl 3, Angs 917–922 — one of the 5 daily Nitnem prayers), **Oankaar** / Dakhanee (Mehl 1, Angs 929–938 — cosmological hymn), **Sidh Gosht** (Mehl 1, Angs 938–946 — Guru Nanak's dialogue with Nath yogis); Raamkalee Var; Bhagat Bāṇī
- **Angs 975–999** (Raag Nat Naaraayan): 25 pages; Mehls 4 and 5
- **Angs 1000–1106** (Raag Maaroo): 107 pages; Mehls 1, 3, 4, 5; Dakhanee; Var; Bhagat Bāṇī of Kabir, Namdev, Jayadeva, Ravidas

## Dhanaasaree Bhagat Bāṇī Tail (Ang 695)

The final Ang of the Dhanaasaree section proper carries the remaining Bhagat Bāṇī before Raag Jaitsree opens. Four Bhagats appear in quick succession, each contributing a distinct devotional register.

**[[trilochan|Trilochan]] (1 composition, Ang 695)**: A karma-theology poem. Every mythological figure—the moon (dwelling on Shiva's forehead, bathing in the Ganges), Krishna (born in the moon's family), Aruna (charioteer of the sun), Shiva himself (destroyer of sins), the ocean (source of Lakshmi and amrit), Hanuman—despite their exalted status, could not erase the karma of past actions: "The karma of past actions cannot be erased, O wife of my house; this is why I chant the Name of the Lord." (Ang 695). The conclusion is not karma-fatalism but its opposite: the Naam alone transcends the karmic record that even gods cannot dissolve.

**Sain (1 composition, Ang 695)**: A lamp-worship (*Aartee*) composition. "With incense, lamps and ghee, I offer this lamp-lit worship service. I am a sacrifice to the Lord of Lakshmi." (Ang 695). Unique because Sain explicitly names Raamaanand as his spiritual source: "Raamaanand knows the devotional worship of the Lord." — the only such reference in the Bhagat Bāṇī to a named Guru-figure of the Sant tradition.

**Peepaa (1 composition, Ang 695)**: Body-as-temple theology: "Within the body, the Divine Lord is embodied. The body is the temple, the place of pilgrimage, and the pilgrim. Within the body are incense, lamps and offerings." (Ang 695). The summary verse: "The One who pervades the Universe also dwells in the body; whoever seeks Him, finds Him there." (Ang 695).

**[[dhanna|Dhanna]] (1 composition, Ang 695–696)**: The most earthly and intimate of the four. Dhanna's prayer is remarkable for its frank materialism: "Lentils, flour and ghee — these things, I beg of You... Shoes, fine clothes, and grain of seven kinds... A milk cow, and a water buffalo... A good wife to care for my home — Your humble servant Dhanna begs for these things, Lord." (Ang 695–696). The frame is the devotional *Aartee* to Gopala (the Lord of the world), but the requests are entirely domestic — a striking counter to the abstract mysticism of other Bhagats.

---

## Raag Jaitsree (Angs 696–711)

Jaitsree is a 15-page section encompassing compositions of [[guru-ram-das|Mehl 4]] (the largest single contributor), [[guru-arjan|Mehl 5]], and [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Mehl 9]], followed by Chhants (lyric soul-bride poems), a Vaar with Shaloks, and Bhagat Bāṇī of [[ravidas|Ravidas]]. The raag's characteristic emotional register is one of urgent longing — the devotee's insistence that life without the Naam is worthless, and the Guru's grace is the only rescue from the self-willed mind.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās and Du-Padas (Angs 696–700)

Mehl 4 opens Jaitsree with eleven compositions, organized across two Houses. The dominant image cluster is commerce and jewels: the Naam is the hidden jewel (*heerā*), the Guru is the jeweller who recognizes and purchases it, and the self-willed manmukh lives in a "room dark with ignorance" where the jewel goes unseen.

> "The Jewel of the Lord's Name abides within my heart; the Guru has placed His hand on my forehead. The sins and pains of countless incarnations have been cast out. The Guru has blessed me with the Naam, the Name of the Lord, and my debt has been paid off." (Ang 696)

> "Without the Naam, I would die; the Name of my Lord and Master is to me like the drug to the addict." (Ang 697)

The manmukh/gurmukh polarity is developed with sustained force: "Without the Guru, the self-willed manmukhs are foolish and ignorant; they are forever entangled in emotional attachment to Maya. They never serve the feet of the Holy; their lives are totally useless." (Ang 696). By contrast, meeting the Guru transforms the devotee: "Meeting with this Holy One, my foolish, stone-like mind has blossomed forth in lush profusion." (Ang 698).

Mehl 4's famous liturgical refrain — "O my mind, chant the Name of the Lord" — runs through every composition. The Satsang (*satsangat*) theme is prominent: "In the Sat Sangat, the True Congregation, I found the Holy, by great good fortune; my restless mind has been quieted. The unstruck melody ever vibrates and resounds." (Ang 698). Pilgrimage, fasting, and ritual are explicitly subordinated: "I have tried visiting all the sacred shrines of pilgrimage, fasting, ceremonial feasts and giving to charities. They do not measure up to the Name of the Lord, Har, Har." (Ang 699).

A theologically concentrated composition on divine omnipresence and self-sufficiency: "He Himself is the Yogi, and the way throughout the ages... He Himself, all by Himself, is all-pervading; He Himself blesses us with sincere love for the Naam... He Himself is the lamp, and the Light pervading all the worlds. He Himself is the True Guru; He Himself churns the ocean." (Ang 699–700).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās and Du-Padas (Angs 700–703)

Mehl 5's thirteen compositions in Jaitsree develop the themes of impermanence, the futility of worldly relationships, and the completeness of God as the sole true friend and companion.

> "Mother, father, spouse, children, relatives, lovers, friends and siblings meet, having been associated in previous lives; but none of them will be your companion and support in the end." (Ang 700)

> "Pearl necklaces, gold, rubies and diamonds please the mind, but they are only Maya. Possessing them, one passes his life in agony; he obtains no contentment from them." (Ang 700)

The "rainbird" image (the *chātrik*, longing for rain as the soul longs for God) and the fish-without-water image — recurring across the SGGS — appear here with unusual force: "The rainbird longs for the rain to fall... The fish cannot survive any other way — without water, it dies." (Ang 702). The divine is simultaneously the only true friend and the all-pervasive reality: "In all the spaces and interspaces, everywhere, whatever we see is Yours." (Ang 700). Union is the raag's resolution: "By great good fortune, I have found Him; the Guru has united me with Him, through the Saadh Sangat... My hopes and desires have all been fulfilled; my Beloved Husband Lord has hugged me close in His embrace." (Ang 701).

God's description as "He abides in each and every heart" and the apophatic insistence — "Some say that He is beyond the world — totally beyond it, while others say that He is totally within it... He is pervading everywhere, and He dwells in each and every heart; He is not stained — He is unstained. He dwells upon the tongues of the Saints." (Ang 701) — mark Mehl 5's characteristic synthesis of immanence and transcendence.

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Angs 702–703

Mehl 9 contributes three compositions to Jaitsree, all bearing his characteristic theme of the soul entangled in Maya, unable to locate the Lord who is actually near at hand.

> "My mind is deluded, entangled in Maya. Whatever I do, while engaged in greed, only serves to bind me down." (Ang 703)

> "The Lord and Master is with me, but I do not know Him. Instead, I run into the forest, looking for Him. The Jewel of the Lord is deep within my heart, but I do not have any knowledge of Him." (Ang 703)

> "O mind, embrace true contemplation. Without the Lord's Name, know that this whole world is false. The Yogis are tired of searching for Him, but they have not found His limit. You must understand that the Lord and Master is near at hand, but He has no form or feature." (Ang 703)

### Chhants and Vaar of Jaitsree (Angs 703–711)

The **Chhants** (Mehl 5, Angs 703–705) deploy the soul-bride (*viraha*) lyric form: the soul-bride searches for her Beloved, addresses her companions (*sakhī*), and asks them to describe the Husband Lord. The Chhants frame the Naam not as doctrine but as longing resolved:

> "I have been wandering around, searching for that enticing, sweet Beloved. Whoever leads me to my Beloved — I would cut off my head and offer it to him, even if I were granted the Blessed Vision of His Darshan for just an instant. My mind is attached to the Lord, like the fish to the water, and the rainbird, thirsty for the raindrops." (Ang 703)

The resolution in the fourth Chhant: "Great joy and happiness welled up, when my Husband Lord, of ever-fresh beauty, showed mercy to me... My hopes and desires have all been fulfilled; my Beloved Husband Lord has hugged me close in His embrace." (Ang 704).

The **Vaar with Shaloks** (Mehl 5, 20 Paurees with paired Shaloks, Angs 705–710) is the theological spine of Jaitsree. It develops the Naam as the complete answer to all religious and ritual alternatives:

> "In the beginning, He was pervading; in the middle, He is pervading; in the end, He will be pervading. He is the Transcendent Lord." (Ang 705)

> "Coming and going are all by the Hukam of Your Will; Your place is steady and unchanging. Nanak begs for this gift; by Your Grace, Lord, please grant me Your Name." (Ang 710)

The Vaar's consistent theological claim: God alone acts, gives, and receives; the Guru is the sole means by which the Naam is implanted; pilgrimage, ritual, and charity are inferior substitutes. One Pauree enumerates the benefits of Naam on all the world: "One is saved, along with his family, and all his ancestors." (Ang 710). The Guru is given a striking series of divine equivalences: "The Guru is the Lord of the Universe; the Guru is the Lord of the world; the Guru is the Perfect Pervading Lord God. The Guru is compassionate; the Guru is all-powerful; the Guru, O Nanak, is the Saving Grace of sinners." (Ang 710).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Jaitsree (Ang 711)

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]] (1 composition, Ang 711)**: An unusual confessional voice. Ravidas does not speak as a spiritually victorious low-caste devotee (his characteristic register in Dhanaasaree) but as a self-accusing petitioner:

> "The five vices have corrupted my mind. Moment by moment, they lead me further away from the Lord. Wherever I look, I see loads of pain and suffering." (Ang 711)

The composition references Hindu mythological scandals — Gautama's wife and Indra, Shiva cutting off Brahma's head — to establish that even the morally compromised can be saved, before arriving at the signature line: "Says Ravi Daas, what am I to do now? Without the Sanctuary of the Lord's Protection, who else's should I seek?" (Ang 711).

### Raag Jaitsree — Theological Summary

Jaitsree's defining contribution is the sustained development of the Naam as the complete substitute for every other religious practice — ritual, pilgrimage, fasting, and the companionship of family — combined with the Mehl 4 insistence that the Guru's intervention is the sole means of access to the Naam. The manmukh/gurmukh polarity is more systematically developed here than in most other raags, and the Vaar provides the doctrinal framework that the lyric Chhants dramatize. Mehl 9's additions introduce the note of self-accusing spiritual blindness, which the raag otherwise addresses through the Guru's grace.

---

## Raag Todee (Angs 711–718)

Todee is a 7-page section containing one composition by [[guru-ram-das|Mehl 4]], a large block of [[guru-arjan|Mehl 5]] compositions (the dominant voice, spanning multiple Houses), two compositions by [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Mehl 9]], and Bhagat Bāṇī from [[namdev|Namdev]]. The raag's characteristic theme is the wandering, distracted mind and the contrast between those who have surrendered to God and those still chasing the world's false pleasures.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Ang 711

A single opening composition. The devotee cannot survive without the Lord: "Without the Lord, my mind cannot survive." (Ang 711). The Mehl 4 *viraha* register: "My heart is gripped by a yearning for my Lord God, and with my eyes, I behold my Lord God." (Ang 711). The closing couplet states Mehl 4's characteristic resolution — discriminating intellect (*bibayk buddhi*) comes from the Guru alone: "From the True Guru, I have obtained a discriminating intellect; the Guru has revealed the spiritual wisdom of God. Servant Nanak has obtained the Naam from the Guru; such is the destiny inscribed upon his forehead." (Ang 711).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Angs 711–718

Mehl 5 dominates Todee with roughly 30 compositions across five Houses. The opening Du-Pada captures the raag's essential tone: "The Saints do not know any other. They are carefree, ever in the Lord's Love; the Lord and Master is on their side." (Ang 711). Disease, sorrow, old age, and death do not approach the Lord's humble servant; "They remain fearless, in the Love of the One Lord." (Ang 711).

A sustained indictment of the distracted mind runs through the Second House Chau-Padās: "The mind wanders, wandering in the ten directions. It is intoxicated by Maya, enticed by the taste of greed... He does not focus his mind, even for a moment, on the Lord's sermon, or the Lord's Praises, or the Saadh Sangat... He runs around chasing the fleeting objects of the world, in all directions, like the ox around the oil press." (Ang 712). The famous negative catalogue of what the manmukh fails to do: "He never does good deeds for others; he does not serve or meditate on the True Guru. He is entangled in the company and the advice of the five demons, intoxicated by the wine of Maya." (Angs 712–713).

Among the notable doctrinal formulations:

> "Without meditating in remembrance on the Lord, life is like a burning fire, even if one lives long, like a snake. One may rule over the nine regions of the earth, but in the end, he shall have to depart, losing the game of life." (Ang 712)

> "Youth has passed, and old age has taken its place. The hands tremble, the head shakes, and the eyes do not see. The eyes do not see, without vibrating and meditating on the Lord; he must leave behind the attractions of Maya, and depart." (Ang 713)

> "In good times, worship and adore Him; in bad times, worship and adore Him; do not ever forget Him. Chanting the Naam, the Name of the Lord, the light of millions of suns shines forth, and the darkness of doubt is dispelled." (Ang 700)

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Ang 717–718

> "What can I say about my base nature? I am entangled in the love of gold and women, and I have not sung the Kirtan of God's Praises." (Ang 717)

> "I remain intoxicated by Maya, night and day, and the filth of my mind will not depart. Says Nanak, now, without the Lord's Sanctuary, I cannot find salvation in any other way." (Ang 718)

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Todee (Ang 718)

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (3 compositions, Ang 718)**: Three theologically distinctive pieces.

The first is an apophatic declaration: "Some say that He is near, and others say that He is far away. Why do you speak such nonsense? One who has found the Lord, keeps quiet about it." The contrast with the Vedic scholar: "Those who become Pandits, religious scholars, recite the Vedas, but foolish Naam Dayv knows only the Lord." (Ang 718).

The second is a concise anti-ritual declaration: "Whose blemishes remain, when one chants the Lord's Name? Sinners become pure, chanting the Lord's Name... I have stopped fasting on the eleventh day of each month; why should I bother to go on pilgrimages to sacred shrines?" (Ang 718).

The third composition is a tour de force word-play poem — a "three-fold play on words" — punning on Punjabi rhyme schemes (haandee/saandee/raandee; heeng/seeng/leeng; tayl/bayl/kayl) to locate God within his Saints, culminating: "The Lord of the Universe, Govind, is within His Saints; Krishna, Shyaam, is in Gokal. The Lord, Raam, is in Naam Dayv." (Ang 718). Namdev's distinctive contribution is this fusion of verbal play, earthly imagery, and a theology of divine immanence in the saint-body.

### Raag Todee — Theological Summary

Todee's distinctive contribution is the development of the distracted, Maya-entangled mind as the antithesis of spiritual life, paired with a Mehl 5 vision of saintly fearlessness and divine protection. Namdev's Bhagat Bāṇī provides the most economical anti-ritualism in the SGGS — the famous claim that the Naam alone dissolves all blemishes — and the unusual word-play poem that identifies the Lord's presence in his devotees as the ultimate sacred geography.

---

## Raag Bairaaree (Ang 719)

The shortest raag section in this range, occupying a single Ang (719). Contains five Du-Padas by [[guru-ram-das|Mehl 4]] and one Du-Pada by [[guru-arjan|Mehl 5]]. No Bhagat Bāṇī. The brevity is structurally significant — Bairaaree functions as a transitional raag between the longer Todee and Tilang sections.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Ang 719–720

All five Mehl 4 compositions develop the paradox of God's nearness and concealment within the body. Themes: the Naam as the path through reincarnation; the Guru as the one who reveals the hidden Lord; creation as God's own act.

> "Listen, O mind, to the Unspoken Speech of the Lord's Name. Riches, wisdom, supernatural spiritual powers and peace are obtained, by vibrating, meditating on the Lord God, under Guru's Instruction. Shiva and the three hundred thirty million gods meditate on the Lord, but they do not know the secret of His mystery." (Ang 719)

> "In the body-village, the Lord Master abides; the Lord is without fear, without vengeance, and without form. The Lord is dwelling near at hand, but He cannot be seen. By the Guru's Teachings, the Lord is obtained." (Ang 719–720)

> "The Lord Himself directs the evolution of the world of the five elements; He Himself infuses the five senses into it. O servant Nanak, the Lord Himself unites us with the True Guru; He Himself resolves the conflicts." (Ang 720)

That chanting, meditation, fasting, and worship are only valid insofar as they generate love for the Lord: "That is chanting, that is deep meditation and austerity, that is fasting and worship, which inspires love for the Lord. Without the Lord's Love, every other love is false; in an instant, it is all forgotten." (Ang 720).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Ang 720

A single brief composition closing the raag: "Meeting with the humble Saints, sing the Praises of the Lord. The pains of millions of incarnations shall be eradicated... By His Kind Mercy, the Lord blesses us with His Name. All happiness and greatness are in the Lord's Name." (Ang 720).

### Raag Bairaaree — Theological Summary

Bairaaree, though brief, concentrates two key themes: divine immanence within the body (the "body-village" theology consistent with the Sant tradition's critique of external pilgrimage and ritual) and the radical divine self-sufficiency — God Himself is the Guru, the disciple, the teaching, and the result. The raag functions as a doctrinal crystallization before the more expansive Tilang section.

---

## Raag Tilang (Angs 720–726)

Tilang spans seven Angs and contains the most distinctive linguistic variety of any raag in this range: [[guru-nanak|Mehl 1]]'s compositions include both Punjabi soul-bride verse and extended Persian-language devotional poetry (*farsi*), while [[guru-ram-das|Mehl 4]] contributes Punjabi hymns, [[guru-arjan|Mehl 5]] offers Persian-language compositions alongside Punjabi pieces, and [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Mehl 9]] provides three short compositions. Bhagat Bāṇī: [[kabir|Kabir]] and [[namdev|Namdev]], both also writing in Persian-inflected registers.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Angs 721–723

Mehl 1 opens Tilang with three Houses. The first House composition (Ang 721) is in Persian: a direct address to the Creator using Sufi theological vocabulary.

> "I offer this one prayer to You; please listen to it, O Creator Lord. You are true, great, merciful and spotless, O Cherisher Lord. The world is a transitory place of mortality — know this for certain in your mind. Azraa-eel, the Messenger of Death, has caught me by the hair on my head, and yet, I do not know it at all in my mind." (Ang 721) — Azraa'eel (*azrā'īl*) is the Islamic angel of death; the Sufi framing is deliberate.

> "Night and day, I wandered around in greed, contemplating evil schemes. I never did good deeds; this is my condition. I am unfortunate, miserly, negligent, shameless and without the Fear of God. Says Nanak, I am Your humble servant, the dust of the feet of Your slaves." (Ang 721)

The second House (Ang 721) shifts to Punjabi and the ascetic/beggar register: "The Fear of You, O Lord God, is my marijuana; my consciousness is the pouch which holds it. I have become an intoxicated hermit." (Ang 721) — the ascetic's hemp-bag recast as God-fear; devotion as divine intoxication. Anti-caste verse in this House: "No one says that ghee or silk are polluted. Such is the Lord's devotee, no matter what his social status is." (Ang 721–722).

The third House (Ang 722) uses the dyer's vat as its central image for the Naam's transformation of the self: "If the body becomes the dyer's vat, O Beloved, and the Name is placed within it as the dye, and if the Dyer who dyes this cloth is the Lord Master — O, such a color has never been seen before!"
