(Ang 722). The soul-bride imagery: those "whose shawls are so dyed" dwell with their Husband Lord always.

A fourth composition (Angs 722–723) — the *viraha* lyric — addresses the soul-bride's pride and foolishness, counseling submission to [[hukam|Hukam]]: "Whatever the Lord does, accept that as good; do away with your own cleverness and self-will." (Ang 723).

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Angs 723–726

Mehl 4 contributes a Vaar with Shaloks (Angs 723–726) and additional Chau-Padās. The Vaar opens with an assertion of divine sovereignty: "Everyone comes by Command of the Lord and Master. The Hukam of His Command extends to all. True is the Lord and Master, and True is His play." (Ang 723).

The Vaar catalogues the Gurmukh's way of life across 22 verses — combining theological statements with practical community descriptions (those who eat betel nuts but forget God, those who wash the feet of the Saints, those who drink in the Guru's words). Key verses:

> "Those Sikhs of the Guru, who serve the Guru, are the most blessed beings. Servant Nanak is a sacrifice to them; He is forever and ever a sacrifice." (Ang 726)

> "The Lord Himself is pleased with the Gurmukhs, the fellowship of the companions. In the Lord's Court, they are given robes of honor, and the Lord Himself hugs them close in His embrace." (Ang 726)

> "The Lord, Har, Har, is the only real profit in this world." (Ang 723)

The Mehl 4 Chau-Padā immediately preceding the Vaar continues the impermanence theme: "The evil-minded person continually does fruitless deeds, all puffed up with pride... In an instant, all this false play shall perish; O my mind, meditate on the Lord." (Ang 723).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Angs 721–725 (interspersed and additional)

Mehl 5 contributes the most theologically concentrated Persian-language compositions in Tilang. The first (Ang 721) addresses creation ex luce (*nūr*): "The Lord infused His Light into the dust, and created the world, the universe. The sky, the earth, the trees, and the water — all are the Creation of the Lord. O human being, whatever you can see with your eyes, shall perish. The world eats dead carcasses, living by neglect and greed." (Ang 721). This composition uses explicitly Islamic vocabulary — *khudā'ī*, *azrā'īl*, *dojak* (hell) — in an unambiguously Sikh theological framework.

> "The Pure Lord God knows your condition. O Nanak, recite your prayer to the holy people." (Ang 721)

Additional Punjabi compositions:

> "There is no other than You, Lord. You are the Creator; whatever You do, that alone happens... Whatever is seen, is Your form, O treasure of virtue, O Lord of the Universe, O Lord of incomparable beauty." (Angs 723–724)

> "Merciful, the Lord Master is Merciful. He gives His gifts to all beings... Why do you waver, O mortal being? The Creator Lord Himself shall protect you... O human being, meditate on the Lord, as long as there is breath in your body." (Ang 725)

> "O Creator, through Your creative potency, I am in love with You. You alone are my spiritual and temporal Lord; and yet, You are detached from all Your creation. In an instant, You establish and disestablish. Wondrous is Your form! Who can know Your play? You are the Light in the darkness." (Ang 725–726)

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Ang 726

Three short compositions in the *Kaafee* style, all on the theme of life's brevity and the urgency of Naam-meditation.

> "If you are conscious, then be conscious of Him night and day, O mortal. Each and every moment, your life is passing away, like water from a cracked pitcher." (Ang 726)

> "Wake up, O mind! Wake up! Why are you sleeping unaware? That body, which you were born with, shall not go along with you in the end." (Ang 727)

> "Sing the Lord's Praises, O mind; He is your only true companion. Your time is passing away; listen carefully to what I say." (Ang 727)

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Tilang (Angs 726–727)

**[[kabir|Kabir]] (1 composition, Ang 726)**: Written in Persian, addressing the irrelevance of Vedas and Scriptures (*kateb*) for inner peace:

> "The Vedas and the Scriptures are only make-believe, O Siblings of Destiny; they do not relieve the anxiety of the heart. If you will only center yourself on the Lord, even for just a breath, then you shall see the Lord face-to-face, present before you." (Ang 726)

> "O human being, search your own heart every day, and do not wander around in confusion. This world is just a magic-show; no one will be holding your hand." (Ang 726)

> "The True Creator Lord is diffused into His creation; He is not just the dark-skinned Krishna of legends." (Ang 726) — a critique of mythological literalism alongside institutional religion.

> "The Lord is the purest of the pure; only through doubt could there be another. O Kabeer, mercy flows from the Merciful Lord; He alone knows who acts." (Ang 726)

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (2 compositions, Angs 726–727)**: Also in Persian-inflected language. The first is a self-humbling prayer addressing God by Islamic divine names: "I am blind; Your Name, O Creator Lord, is my only anchor and support. I am poor, and I am meek. Your Name is my only support. O beautiful Lord, benevolent and merciful Lord, You are so wealthy and generous... You alone give, and You alone take away." (Ang 726).

The second composition (Ang 727) is a joyous address to Krishna in Dwaarikaa — shifting entirely from Persian to a Braj-inflected lyric: "Hello, my friend, hello my friend. Is there any good news? Where did you come from? Where have You been? And where are You going? Tell me the truth, in the holy city of Dwaarikaa. How handsome is your turban! And how sweet is your speech." (Ang 727). Namdev concludes: "You are my Lord King, like the dark-skinned Krishna" — the Lord of the Universe named through the Vaishnava figure of Krishna.

### Raag Tilang — Theological Summary

Tilang's defining contribution to Part 3 is its linguistic and theological pluralism: the raag deliberately incorporates Persian Sufi vocabulary, Islamic divine names (*Allāh*, *khudā*, *azrā'īl*, *dojak*), and Vaishnava imagery within a single doctrinal framework. Mehl 1's Persian compositions and Kabir and Namdev's Persian-register Bhagat Bāṇī perform an inter-religious theological synthesis — the Sikh Naam understood as the reality beneath Sufi *khudā*, Vaishnava Govind, and the Vedic Brahman simultaneously. The raag also contains the most explicit statement of Hukam as the governing principle of all coming and going, through Mehl 4's Vaar.

---


---

## Raag Soohee (Angs 727–794)

A major raag section of 68 pages. The name *Soohee* (red/crimson) saturates the section's governing metaphor: the red-robed bride (*soohee*) whose color signals either her blessed union with the divine Husband Lord or her futile attachment to worldly things while her true Husband remains estranged. This soul-bride register is the most developed in the entire Granth to this point — Soohee is the raag of viraha transformed into longing, longing transformed into surrender, and surrender into union.

**Composition types**: Chau-Padās (Angs 727–750), Ashtapadīs (Angs 750–762), three unique extended bride-poems (**Kuchajee**, **Suchajee**, **Gunvantee**, Ang 762–763), **Chhants** (Angs 763–784), and the **Var of Soohee** (Angs 785–792), followed by **Bhagat Bāṇī** (Angs 792–794).

**Gurus represented**: Mehl 1 ([[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]]), Mehl 3 ([[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]]), Mehl 4 ([[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]]), Mehl 5 ([[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]]). Note: Mehl 9 ([[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]]) does **not** appear in this raag section.

**Bhagat Bāṇī**: [[kabir|Kabir]], [[ravidas|Ravidas]], [[sheikh-farid|Sheikh Farid]].

---

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Chau-Padās (Angs 727–731)

[[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]]'s Chau-Padās in Soohee open the raag and establish its dominant themes through a series of compressed metaphors drawn from household activity, trade, and social encounter.

**The dairy/churning metaphor** (Ang 727, Ghar 1): One of the most arresting opening hymns in the Granth, recasting spiritual practice as dairy production. The body is the vessel: "Wash the vessel, sit down and anoint it with fragrance; then, go out and get the milk." The refrain: "Chant the Name of the One Lord. All other actions are fruitless." The milk of good deeds is curdled by the rennet of clear consciousness; the churning produces Amrit: "If you chant the Naam, the Name of the Lord, with your tongue, then the curd will be churned. In this way, the Ambrosial Nectar is obtained." — a domestication of the same Naam-as-amrit theology present throughout the Granth, here rendered in the language of the Punjabi household.

**Inner over outer** (Ang 728): "Deep within the self, the Lord abides; do not go outside looking for Him. You have renounced the Ambrosial Nectar — why are you eating poison?" The true slave of the Lord is one who serves; the Lord "is pervading and permeating the water, the land, and the sky." Self-assessment: "I am not good; no one is bad. Prays Nanak, He alone saves us!" — a characteristic Guru Nanak leveling of moral hierarchy under divine grace.

**Bronze and false friends** (Ang 729, Ghar 6): A series of social images for inner corruption: "Bronze is bright and shiny, but when it is rubbed, its blackness appears. Washing it, its impurity is not removed, even if it is washed a hundred times." True friendship: "They alone are my friends, who travel along with me; and in that place, where the accounts are called for, they appear standing with me." The body as the simmal tree — beautiful but bearing useless fruit: "My body is like the simmal tree; seeing me, other people are fooled. Its fruits are useless — just like the qualities of my body."

**True yoga** (Ang 730, Ghar 7): One of the most complete critiques of external asceticism in the Granth. "Yoga is not the patched coat, Yoga is not the walking stick. Yoga is not smearing the body with ashes. Yoga is not the ear-rings, and not the shaven head. Yoga is not the blowing of the horn." The refrain that appears four times: "Remaining unblemished in the midst of the filth of the world — this is the way to attain Yoga." The definition of a true Yogi: "One who looks upon all with a single eye, and knows them to be one and the same — he alone is known as a Yogi." The concluding verse introduces *death-while-living*: "O Nanak, remain dead while yet alive — practice such a Yoga. When the horn is blown without being blown, then you shall attain the state of fearless dignity."

**Cosmic scale** (Ang 731): God's value is immeasurable by any instrument: "What scale, what weights, and what assayer shall I call for You, Lord?... Mind is the scale, consciousness the weights, and the performance of Your service is the appraiser. Deep within my heart, I weigh my Husband Lord; in this way I focus my consciousness." Absolute divine sovereignty: "You Yourself are the balance, the weights and the scale; You Yourself are the weigher. You Yourself see, and You Yourself understand; You Yourself are the trader."

---

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās (Angs 731–736)

[[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]]'s extensive Chau-Padā section opens with an immediate statement of the Guru-Naam synthesis: "My mind worships and adores the Lord's Name, through the Guru, and the Word of the Guru's Shabad. All the desires of my mind and body have been fulfilled; all fear of death has been dispelled." (Ang 731)

**Satsang theology**: The most persistent theme in Mehl 4's Soohee compositions. "The Sat Sangat, the True Congregation of the True Guru, is sublime and exalted. They sing the Glorious Praises of the Lord God. Bless me with Your Mercy, Lord, and unite me with the Sat Sangat; I wash the feet of Your humble servants." (Ang 731) The Satsang is the only site where the Lord's wealth (*har ratan*) is traded: "In the company of the Sat Sangat, the True Congregation, you shall earn the wealth of the Lord; this wealth of the Lord is not obtained anywhere else, by any other means, at all." (Ang 734)

**The color/dye metaphor** (Ang 732): Naam as a permanent dye — "The Lord's Name is the Love of the Lord. The Lord's Love is the permanent color... When the Guru is totally satisfied and pleased, He colors us with the Lord's Love; this color shall never fade away." The Manmukh "is left pale and uncolored. Even if he wishes it a hundred times, he does not obtain the Lord's Love." Only the Gurmukh "is imbued with the deep red color of His Love." — a meta-commentary on the raag's name itself (*Soohee* = crimson).

**Caste inversion** (Ang 733): A key passage where Mehl 4 names historical Bhagats to make the anti-caste argument: "When someone of low social class chants the Lord's Name, he obtains the state of highest dignity. Go and ask Bidar, the son of a maid; Krishna himself stayed in his house." The leather-worker Ravidas and the calico-printer Namdev are named explicitly: "Ravi Daas, the leather-worker, praised the Lord, and sang the Kirtan of His Praises each and every instant. Although he was of low social status, he was exalted and elevated, and people of all four castes came and bowed at his feet... Naam Dayv loved the Lord; the people called him a fabric dyer. The Lord turned His back on the high-class Kh'shaatriyas and Brahmins, and showed His face to Naam Dayv." (Ang 733)

**Sincerity over performance** (Ang 734): "One who chants the Name of the Lord, Har, Har, while constantly practicing deception, shall never become pure of heart... The self-willed manmukh may perform chants, meditations, austere self-discipline, fasts and devotional worship, but his sickness does not go away. Deep within him is the sickness of excessive egotism; in the love of duality he is ruined." Outward appearance: "Outwardly, he wears religious robes and he is very clever, but his mind wanders in the ten directions."

**The wealth of the Naam** (Ang 735): Extended six-stanza hymn (unusual length) on the indestructibility of the Lord's wealth: "The wealth of the Fearless Lord is permanent, forever and ever, and true. This wealth of the Lord cannot be destroyed by fire or water; neither thieves nor the Messenger of Death can take it away." The Amrit Vela as planting time: "The devotees of the Lord plant the seed of the Lord's wealth in the ambrosial hours of the Amrit Vaylaa; they eat it, and spend it, but it is never exhausted."

---

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās (Angs 736–750)

[[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]]'s Chau-Padās in Soohee pursue what may be called a *theatrical non-dualism*: the multiplicity of the world is shown to dissolve back into the One through a series of elegant analogies.

**The actor metaphor** (Ang 736): "The actor stages the play, playing the many characters in different costumes; but when the play ends, he takes off the costumes, and then he is one, and only one." Three parallel analogies follow: (1) "Countless waves rise up from the water" — they are water. (2) "Jewels and ornaments of many different forms are fashioned from gold" — they are gold. (3) "The one sky is reflected in thousands of water jugs, but when the jugs are broken, only the sky remains." All multiplicity is the display (*khel*) of the One. "Doubt comes from greed, emotional attachment and the corruption of Maya" — Maya is what makes the waves look separate from the water.

The existential question framing the whole: "How many forms and images appeared and disappeared? Where have they gone? Where did they come from?" (Ang 736–737)

---

### Ashtapadīs — Mehl 1, 3, 4, 5 (Angs 750–762)

**Mehl 1 (Ang 750)**: Extended soul-bride ashtapadī in which the bride confesses her complete lack of qualification: "I am totally without virtue; I have no virtue at all. How can I meet my Husband Lord?... I have no beauty, no enticing eyes. I do not have a noble family, good manners or a sweet voice." The path to union: "When I get rid of her ego, then she merges in her Husband Lord. Only then can the soul-bride obtain the nine treasures of her Beloved." Finishes with: "Prays Nanak, the Lord is, and shall always be. She alone is ravished and enjoyed, with whom the Beloved Lord is pleased." — This composition prepares the register for the Kuchajee and Suchajee directly below.

**Mehl 3 (Angs 753–757)**: The Ashtapadīs of [[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]] in Soohee develop the Naam-as-foundation and Gurmukh/Manmukh binary with characteristic precision. Opening verse: "Everything comes from the Naam, the Name of the Lord; without the True Guru, the Naam is not experienced. The Word of the Guru's Shabad is the sweetest and most sublime essence, but without tasting it, its flavor cannot be experienced." (Ang 753) The Gurmukh: "The Gurmukh sings the Praises of the Lord; the Gurmukh understands. The Gurmukh contemplates the Word of the Shabad. Body and soul are totally rejuvenated through the Guru." The Manmukh: "The blind self-willed manmukh acts blindly, and earns only poison in this world. Enticed by Maya, he suffers in constant pain, without the most Beloved Guru." The contemplative resolution: "Concentrating on the Word of the Shabad, the soul is illumined and enlightened. I remain absorbed in celestial ecstasy."

---

### The Kuchajee — The Ungraceful Bride (Ang 762)

The most distinctive and structurally unusual composition in Raag Soohee — a unique genre unto itself, labeled *Kuchajee* ("ungraceful" or "ill-mannered" bride), found nowhere else in the Granth. Composed by Mehl 1 ([[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]]), it is a sustained first-person lament in which the soul (figured as a bride) exhaustively catalogues her spiritual failures and begs the divine Husband Lord for a chance at union despite her complete unworthiness.

**Form**: Single extended composition (not divided into numbered stanzas in the usual sense), in the folk *jio* lyric register, with the recurring vocative *jio* ("dear") as a marker of intimate address.

**Opening cry**: "I am ungraceful and ill-mannered, full of endless faults. How can I go to enjoy my Husband Lord? Each of His soul-brides is better than the rest — who even knows my name?" (Ang 762)

**The virtuous brides**: "Those brides who enjoy their Husband Lord are very blessed, resting in the shade of the mango tree. I do not have their virtue — who can I blame for this?" The question of blame is immediately deflected: the fault is entirely the bride's (the soul's), not the Lord's.

**Material entrapment**: The bride confesses having been seduced away from her Husband by the gifts He gave her: "Gold, silver, pearls and rubies are pleasing... My Husband Lord has blessed me with these things, and I have focused my thoughts on them. Palaces of brick and mud are built and decorated with stones; I have been fooled by these decorations, and I do not sit near my Husband Lord." (Ang 762) — The Lord's creation becomes the bride's trap; she is distracted by the gifts from the Giver.

**Existential images**: Cranes shrieking overhead; the bride departing for the in-law's house unprepared; sleeping through the dawn of opportunity: "She kept sleeping as the day dawned; she forgot all about her journey. She separated herself from her Husband Lord, and now she suffers in pain." (Ang 762)

**Final prayer**: "Virtue is in You, O Lord; I am totally without virtue. This is Nanak's only prayer: You give all Your nights to the virtuous soul-brides. I know I am unworthy, but isn't there a night for me as well?" (Ang 762) — The prayer is not for righteousness, but simply for inclusion; the plea of the most wretched soul who cannot even claim the merit of her longing.

The Kuchajee is immediately followed by its dialectical counterpart, the **Suchajee** ("Noble and Graceful Bride," Mehl 1, same Ang), in which the redeemed soul speaks from union: "When I have You, then I have everything. O my Lord and Master, You are my wealth and capital. Within You, I abide in peace; within You, I am congratulated." The Suchajee's theology is complete *Hukam* surrender: "By the Pleasure of Your Will, You bestow thrones and greatness. And by the Pleasure of Your Will, You make us beggars and wanderers... By the Pleasure of Your Will, one crosses over the terrifying world-ocean... Through the Word of the Guru's Teachings, I have found my Husband Lord." (Ang 762)

A third composition in this cluster, the **Gunvantee** ("Virtuous Bride," Mehl 5, Ang 763), adds a communal dimension: the path to the Lord runs through the Sikh community and the instruction of Guru's Sikhs. "When I see a Sikh of the Guru, I humbly bow and fall at his feet... I dedicate this mind to you. Please, show me the Path to God." The instruction received: "Abandon the intellectual pursuits of the mind, and forget the love of duality. In this way, you shall obtain the Blessed Vision of the Lord's Darshan; the hot winds shall not even touch you." (Ang 763)

**Theological significance**: These three compositions constitute a triptych — the unworthy soul's cry (Kuchajee), the redeemed soul's song (Suchajee), and the community-mediated path to union (Gunvantee). Together they articulate the Granth's most concentrated treatment of the soul's interior journey through failure, longing, surrender, and grace, in the soul-bride register.

---

### Chhants (Angs 763–784)

Extended lyric poems by all four Gurus in Soohee, the Chhants sustain the viraha register with particular intensity. The *jio* vocative marker of the Kuchajee continues into the Chhants as the intimate address to God.

**Mehl 1 Chhants (Angs 763–767)**: The dominant image is the bride intoxicated with youth, asleep to spiritual reality: "Intoxicated with the wine of youth, I did not realize that I was only a guest at my parents' home (in this world). My consciousness is polluted with faults and mistakes; without the Guru, virtue does not even enter into me... O father, give me in marriage to the Lord; I am pleased with Him as my Husband. I belong to Him. He is pervading throughout the four ages, and the Word of His Bani permeates the three worlds." (Ang 763) The widowhood without the Lord: "O Nanak, in the prime of my youth, I am a widow; without my Husband Lord, the soul-bride is wasting away." (Ang 763)

**Mehl 3 Chhants (Angs 768–773)**: [[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]]'s Chhants continue his characteristic Shabad-theology within the soul-bride frame.

**Mehl 4 Chhants (Angs 773–777)**: [[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] intensifies the viraha longing.

**Mehl 5 Chhants (Angs 777–784)**: [[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]]'s Chhants bring the section to close with the union register.

---

### The Var of Soohee (Angs 785–792)

Labeled "Vaar Of Soohee, With Shaloks Of The Third Mehl" — all Shaloks are by [[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]] (Mehl 3), with Paurees and additional Shaloks by Mehl 1 appearing in later portions (from Ang 790).

**The opening Shalok** by Mehl 3 frames the entire Var in the red-robed-bride imagery that names the raag: "In her red robes, the discarded bride goes out, seeking enjoyment with another's husband. She leaves the husband of her own home, enticed by her love of duality... She finds it sweet, and eats it up; her excessive sensuality only makes her disease worse. She forsakes the Lord, her sublime Husband, and then later, she suffers the pain of separation from Him." (Ang 785) The contrast: "But she who becomes Gurmukh, turns away from corruption and adorns herself, attuned to the Love of the Lord. She enjoys her celestial Husband Lord, and enshrines the Lord's Name within her heart. She is humble and obedient; she is His virtuous bride forever; the Creator unites her with Himself." (Ang 785)

**The Paurees** begin with a creation/sovereignty hymn: "He Himself established His throne, in the Akaashic ethers and the nether worlds. By the Hukam of His Command, He created the earth, the true home of Dharma... He gives sustenance to all; how wonderful and unique is the Hukam of Your Command! You Yourself are permeating and pervading; You Yourself are the Cherisher." (Pauree 1, Ang 785) — [[hukam|Hukam]] as the creative and sustaining force, framing the entire Var in divine sovereignty.

**Second Mehl 3 Shalok**: "The red-robed woman becomes a happy soul-bride, only when she accepts the True Name. Become pleasing to your True Guru, and you shall be totally beautified... O Nanak, what is the character of the happy soul-bride? Within her, is Truth; her face is bright and radiant, and she is absorbed in her Lord and Master." (Ang 786)

**Mehl 1 Shaloks** (entering from Ang 790 onward): A shift in register. Mehl 1 Shaloks in the Var include pointed social critique: "Thieves, adulterers, prostitutes and pimps, make friendships with the unrighteous... They do not know the value of the Lord's Praises, and Satan is always with them. If a donkey is anointed with sandalwood paste, he still loves to roll in the dirt. O Nanak, by spinning falsehood, a fabric of falsehood is woven." (Ang 790) — the interior moral landscape of those without Naam, described through social types.

---

### Raag Soohee — Bhagat Bāṇī (Angs 792–794)

Three Bhagat authors close Raag Soohee, each deploying the raag's soul-bride and impermanence register in their own distinctive voice.

#### Kabir (Angs 792–793)

[[kabir|Kabir]]'s Soohee compositions are consistently focused on death-readiness, the urgency of Naam-practice before the body fails, and accountability at the divine Court.

**Accountability** (Ang 792, Composition 1): "Since your birth, what have you done? You have never even chanted the Name of the Lord... What preparations are you making for your death, O unfortunate one?... Through pain and pleasure, you have taken care of your family. But at the time of death, you shall have to endure the agony all alone. When you are seized by the neck, then you shall cry out. Says Kabeer, why didn't you remember the Lord before this?" (Ang 792, ||3||1||)

**Aging** (Ang 792, Composition 2): "My innocent soul trembles and shakes. I do not know how my Husband Lord will deal with me. The night of my youth has passed away; will the day of old age also pass away? My dark hairs, like bumble bees, have gone away, and grey hairs, like cranes, have settled upon my head... Water does not remain in the unbaked clay pot; when the soul-swan departs, the body withers away." The bride-persona: "I decorate myself like a young virgin; but how can I enjoy pleasures, without my Husband Lord? My arm is tired, driving away the crows. Says Kabeer, this is the way the story of my life ends." (Ang 792, ||4||2||)

**Impermanence** (Ang 793, Composition 3): "He kept sleeping as the day dawned... The property and wealth he has accumulated passes on to someone else. Says Kabeer, those people are deluded, who forget their Lord and Master, and roll in the dust." (Ang 793, ||4||3||)

**Lallit sub-form** (Ang 793, Composition 4): The *Lallit* is a classical musical form of pathos; Kabir deploys it for the exhaustion of old age: "My eyes are exhausted, and my ears are tired of hearing; my beautiful body is exhausted. Driven forward by old age, all my senses are exhausted; only my attachment to Maya is not exhausted." But the Shabad transcends physical exhaustion: "When the Word of the Shabad abides deep within, thirst and desire are quenched."

**Hukam and the game of chess** (Ang 793, Composition 4 continued): "When one understands the Hukam of the Lord's Command, he plays the game of chess with the Lord; throwing the dice, he conquers his own mind." (||3||) — a striking image in which understanding [[hukam|Hukam]] is figured as a strategic game that transforms the mind.

**Final Kabir composition**: Closes with: "Says Kabeer, I have found that Guru, whose Name is Clear Understanding." (Ang 793, ||4||5||) — a signature closing move: the Guru identified not as a person but as *Clear Understanding* (*sojhee*), a quality of spiritual discernment.

#### Ravidas (Angs 793–794)

[[ravidas|Ravidas]]'s three compositions in Soohee operate in the soul-bride register throughout, with his characteristic emphasis on suffering, thirst, divine mercy, and the impermanence of material existence.

**Soul-bride and compassion** (Ang 793, Composition 1): Opens with the virtuous bride as the model of God-union: "The happy soul-bride knows the worth of her Husband Lord. Renouncing pride, she enjoys peace and pleasure. She surrenders her body and mind to Him, and does not remain separate from Him." The refrain — unusual in the Granth for its ethical directness: "How can anyone know the pain of another, if there is no compassion and sympathy within?" (Ang 793) The discarded bride: "No one will accompany you there; you will have to go all alone." Ravidas's own prayer: "Suffering in pain, I have come to Your Door, O Compassionate Lord. I am so thirsty for You, but You do not answer me. Says Ravi Daas, I seek Your Sanctuary, God; as You know me, so will You save me." (Ang 793, ||3||1||)

**Impermanence** (Ang 794, Composition 2): "That day which comes, that day shall go. You must march on; nothing remains stable. Our companions are leaving, and we must leave as well. We must go far away. Death is hovering over our heads... Why are you asleep? Wake up, you ignorant fool!... The One who gave you life shall also provide you with nourishment. In each and every heart, He runs His shop. Meditate on the Lord, and renounce your egotism and self-conceit." (Ang 794, ||3||2||)

**Body as straw** (Ang 794, Composition 3): "This body is like a house of straw. When it is burnt, it mixes with dust... Even relatives, family and friends begin to say, 'Take his body out, immediately!'... And the wife of his house, who was so attached to his body and heart, runs away, crying out, 'Ghost! Ghost!' Says Ravi Daas, the whole world has been plundered, but I have escaped, chanting the Name of the One Lord." (Ang 794, ||4||3||) — the world as *looted* house (*jag looti-aa*), the only escape the Name of the One Lord.

#### Sheikh Farid (Ang 794)

[[sheikh-farid|Sheikh Farid]]'s two compositions in Soohee deploy the raag's soul-bride imagery with characteristic Chishti Sufi registers of longing, repentance, and the terror of the path.

**The insane bride** (Ang 794, Composition 1): "Burning and burning, writhing in pain, I wring my hands. I have gone insane, seeking my Husband Lord. O my Husband Lord, You are angry with me in Your Mind. The fault is with me, and not with my Husband Lord." The refrain of self-accusation: "O my Lord and Master, I do not know Your excellence and worth. Having wasted my youth, now I come to regret and repent." (Ang 794, ||1||Pause||) The black bird image (the *koel*, cuckoo): "'I have been burnt by separation from my Beloved.' Without her Husband Lord, how can the soul-bride ever find peace?" When God shows mercy: "In His Mercy, God has united me with the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy. And when I look again, then I find God (*Alhu*) as my Helper." — the Islamic divine name (*Alhu* = *Allah*) appears directly. The path's terror: "The path upon which I must walk is very depressing. It is sharper than a two-edged sword, and very narrow. That is where my path lies. O Shaykh Fareed, think of that path early on." (Ang 794, ||4||1||)

**The Lallit sub-form** (Ang 794, Composition 2): "You were not able to make yourself a raft when you should have. When the ocean is churning and over-flowing, then it is very difficult to cross over it." The safflower metaphor for transient worldly beauty: "Do not touch the safflower with your hands; its color will fade away, my dear." Final image: "Says Fareed, O my companions, when our Husband Lord calls, the soul departs, sad at heart, and this body returns to dust." (Ang 794, ||3||2||)

Sheikh Farid's use of *Alhu* (Allah), the Saadh Sangat as the site of divine encounter, and the classic Sufi path-as-razor image root these compositions in Islamic mysticism while fitting them entirely within the soul-bride frame governing the raag. His presence at the close of Soohee confirms the Granth's inclusion of the Islamic devotional tradition within the Sikh understanding of the path to the divine Husband.

---

### Raag Soohee — Theological Summary

Raag Soohee is the Granth's most sustained meditation on the *qualified soul* — not the perfected Gurmukh or the hardened Manmukh, but the soul that knows it is unworthy and cries out nonetheless. The Kuchajee and Suchajee constitute the structural and theological heart of the raag: failure-to-union is the arc, and grace (not merit) is the bridge. The dye-color metaphor (the raag's own name, *Soohee*/crimson) generates the raag's central theological claim: the Naam alone is the permanent color; all other colors — wealth, youth, ritual observance, external appearances — fade. This theology is expressed consistently across all five composition types and all three Bhagat authors. Kabir, Ravidas, and Sheikh Farid each voice the same urgency — death comes, youth passes, the body is straw — and each resolves into the same refuge: the One Name, the Guru's grace, the Saadh Sangat.
---

## Raag Bilaaval (Angs 795–840)

Raag Bilaaval occupies 46 pages and represents one of the more joyful musical registers in the Granth — the name *bilaaval* itself evokes bliss and celebration, a tone Mehl 3 makes explicit when he writes that when God attaches the Gurmukh to His love, "joyful melodies permeate his home... The women come and sing the songs of joy. Meeting with their Beloved, lasting peace is obtained." (Ang 797). All major Gurus are represented: Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak), Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das), Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das), Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan), and Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur). Composition types include Chau-Padās, Partaal, and Ashtapadees. Bhagat bāṇī includes three poets: [[namdev|Namdev]], [[ravidas|Ravidas]], and **Sadhna** — the last of whom has his sole composition in the entire SGGS here.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Chau-Padās (Ang 795–796)

Guru Nanak opens Bilaaval with a cascade of self-negation before divine greatness. The first composition immediately deflates all human speech about God: "You are the Emperor, and I call You a chief — how does this add to Your greatness? As You permit me, I praise You, O Lord and Master; I am ignorant, and I cannot chant Your Praises." (Ang 795). This radical insufficiency of speech before the Indescribable runs through all Mehl 1 compositions here. A second hymn reframes the body as spiritual shrine: "My mind is the temple, and my body is the simple cloth of the humble seeker; deep within my heart, I bathe at the sacred shrine. The One Word of the Shabad abides within my mind; I shall not come to be born again." (Ang 795) — the inward temple metaphor displaces external pilgrimage entirely.

The third Chau-Padā declares divine self-sufficiency in an anaphoric series: "He Himself is the Word of the Shabad, and He Himself is the Insignia. He Himself is the Listener, and He Himself is the Knower. He Himself created the creation, and He Himself beholds His almighty power." (Ang 795–796). The fourth composition contrasts [[haumai|Haumai]] and the liberating Naam: "Love of Maya is like a cursed woman, ugly, dirty and promiscuous. Power and beauty are false, and last for only a few days. But when one is blessed with the Naam, the darkness within is illuminated." (Ang 796). The Shabad-death formula recurs: "One who dies in the Word of the Shabad conquers his mind, through his mind. Keeping his mind restrained, he enshrines the True Lord within his heart." (Ang 796).

The [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh/Manmukh]] polarity appears in viraha language: "Without the Blessed Vision of His Darshan, how can I live, O my mother? Without the Lord, my soul cannot survive, even for an instant." (Ang 796). The manmukh gathers "false wealth" and is separated from the Lord; the Gurmukh attains the Naam and is "celebrated with glory." The closing verse offers the signature self-designation: "The Name of the Lord is the wealth and capital of servant Nanak." (Ang 796).

### Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das) — Chau-Padās (Ang 797–798)

Mehl 3 opens with one of the most intense condemnations of wasted life in the Granth: "Cursed, cursed is the food; cursed, cursed is the sleep; cursed, cursed are the clothes worn on the body. Cursed is the body, along with family and friends, when one does not find his Lord and Master in this life. He misses the step of the ladder, and this opportunity will not come into his hands again; his life is wasted, uselessly." (Ang 797). The vehicle for liberation is singular: "One who becomes Gurmukh is said to be liberated, while the poor self-willed manmukhs are in bondage." (Ang 797). Duality (*doojaa bhaa-o*) is the structural enemy — it prevents the mind from lovingly focusing on God.

The rāg's name surfaces explicitly in Mehl 3's fifth composition: when God attaches the Gurmukh to His love, "joyful melodies (*bilaaval*) permeate his home, and he is embellished with the Word of the Guru's Shabad." (Ang 797) — a musical pun on the rāg's own affective register. The Gurmukhs in this hymn "are dyed in the deep crimson color of the Lord's Love; they look so beautiful. This dye never fades away; they are absorbed in the True Lord." (Ang 797).

Mehl 3 then deploys the four-yuga framework: "Celibacy, self-discipline and pilgrimages were the essence of Dharma in those past ages; but in this Dark Age of Kali Yuga, the Praise of the Lord's Name is the essence of Dharma." (Ang 798) — the same teaching appearing in Raag Gauree and Raag Aasaa, here confirmed in Bilaaval. The True Guru is the means: "One who accepts the wisdom of the True Guru's Teachings, is absorbed into the True Guru. The Lord's Name abides deep within the nucleus of one who realizes the Bani of the Guru's Word within his soul." (Ang 797). The sixth composition achieves the highest union-formula available in Mehl 3: "The Shabad deep within the nucleus of the self dispels the darkness of ignorance. Meeting with my Friend, the True Guru, I have obtained spiritual wisdom. Those who are attuned to the True Lord, do not have to enter the cycle of reincarnation again." (Ang 797).

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās (Ang 799–801)

Mehl 4 opens Bilaaval's fourth house with a striking image of divine sovereignty over all agency: "As the violinist plays upon the strings of the violin, so does the Lord play the living beings." (Ang 799). Nothing escapes divine orchestration — "According to the pre-ordained destiny written upon my forehead, I have found the Guru." (Ang 799). The classic Bhakti narrative of Prahlaad saved from Harnaakhash (the demonic king) is cited as model of divine protection: "as you saved Prahlaad from the clutches of Harnaakash; keep him in Your Sanctuary, Lord." (Ang 799).

Notably, Mehl 4 cites [[ravidas|Ravidas]] by name as an exemplar of divine grace transcending caste: "Ravi Daas, the leather-worker, who worked with hides and carried dead animals was saved, by entering the Lord's Sanctuary." (Ang 799) — one of the rare explicit cross-Bhagat references within the Guru bāṇī itself. The anti-caste teaching extends into a universal Mantra declaration: "Anyone, from any class — Kh'shaatriya, Brahman, Soodra or Vaishya — can chant, and meditate on the Mantra of the Lord's Name." (Ang 800).

Mehl 4 then issues the foundational communal call: "Come, O Saints, and join together, O my Siblings of Destiny; let us tell the Stories of the Lord, Har, Har. The Naam, the Name of the Lord, is the boat in this Dark Age of Kali Yuga; the Word of the Guru's Shabad is the boatman to ferry us across." (Ang 800). The body-village contains the Lord's "supreme, sublime essence" (*raam ras*), accessed through serving the True Guru and drinking in the ambrosial essence of the Naam (Ang 800). The Partaal composition (Thirteenth House, Ang 801) closes with a characteristic Mehl 4 surrender: "Joining the Sangat, the Holy Congregation, I sing the Glorious Praises of the Lord... servant Nanak falls at his feet." (Ang 801).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās and Extended Series (Angs 801–836)

Mehl 5 dominates the Chau-Padā section with the densest concentration of Saadh Sangat theology and non-dual cosmological statements in Raag Bilaaval. The opening Chau-Padā frames the fundamental problem: attachment to what is visible binds; only the Saadh Sangat liberates: "let me be attached to the hem of the robe of the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy." (Ang 801). The soul is Maya-entangled: "Maya, the snake, holds all in her coils. They burn to death in egotism, like the moth lured by seeing the flame." (Ang 801). The priceless jewel within is revealed only through the Guru: "That jewel is within the body, the Temple of the Lord. The Guru has torn away the veil of illusion, and beholding the jewel, I am delighted." (Ang 801–802).

The most theologically dense non-dual composition in Bilaaval comes in the Fourth House:

> "The entire Universe is the form of the One Lord... He Himself is the trade, and He Himself is the trader... He manifests many forms, while still unmanifest and absolute, and yet He has One Form. He Himself is the water, and He Himself is the waves. He Himself is the temple, and He Himself is selfless service. He Himself is the worshipper, and He Himself is the idol. He Himself is the Yoga; He Himself is the Way. Nanak's God is forever liberated." (Ang 802)

This *āpay-āpay* ("He Himself... He Himself") anaphoric sequence represents one of the fullest expressions of Sikh non-dualism in the Granth — God is simultaneously creator and creation, worshipper and worshipped, immanent and transcendent.

Subsequent Mehl 5 Chau-Padās cycle through consistent themes: renounce "the tasteless water of corruption" and "drink in the supreme nectar of the Naam" (Ang 803); Saadh Sangat as the site where "there is no birth or death... no coming or going in reincarnation" (Ang 803); "Without Naam, the body is reduced to ashes... Forgetting the Lord, one becomes subservient to all." (Ang 804). The Guru as indispensable: "He places the one who strays back on the Path; such a Guru is found by great good fortune." (Ang 804). All other rituals are dismissed: "All other rituals are just worldly affairs; joining the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy, one is saved." (Ang 804). Guru identified with the Supreme Lord: "The Guru is the Supreme Lord God, the Transcendent Lord; He is ever-present, forever and ever." (Ang 802).

### Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur) — Du-Padas (Angs 830–831)

[[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]]'s two Du-Padas in Bilaaval are among the most concentrated expressions of Naam theology in his entire bāṇī. The first begins with a pedagogical injunction citing famous Bhakti/Puranic exemplars: "The Name of the Lord is the Dispeller of sorrow — realize this. Remembering Him in meditation, even Ajaamal the robber and Ganikaa the prostitute were liberated; let your soul know this." (Ang 830). Dhruva the child-devotee, saved by Narada's teaching, "obtained the immovable, eternal state of fearlessness, and all the world was amazed." (Ang 830). The teaching is direct: "the Lord is the Saving Grace and the Protector of His devotees; believe it — He is close to you." (Ang 830).

The second Du-Pada delivers the Mehl 9 signature teaching on *jivan mukta* — liberation while alive: "Without the Name of the Lord, you shall only find pain. Without devotional worship, doubt is not dispelled." (Ang 831). External religions are insufficient: "Of what use are sacred shrines of pilgrimage, if one does not enter the Sanctuary of the Lord? Know that Yoga and sacrificial feasts are fruitless, if one forgets the Praises of God." (Ang 831). The path: "One who lays aside both pride and attachment, sings the Glorious Praises of the Lord of the Universe. Says Nanak, the mortal who does this is said to be 'jivan mukta' — liberated while yet alive." (Ang 831). This is the clearest statement of *jivan mukta* in Raag Bilaaval, and one of the most direct in the Granth.

### Ashtapadees (Angs 832–840)

The Ashtapadee section opens with **Mehl 1** (Ang 832), which introduces the epistemological paradox of universal Naam-talk without realization: "Everyone talks about spiritual wisdom and spiritual knowledge. Talking, talking, they argue, and suffer... Without being imbued with the subtle essence, there is no liberation." (Ang 832). The Gurmukh/Manmukh contrast is sharpened: "He alone is a spiritual teacher, who lovingly focuses his consciousness on the Word of the Shabad. The self-willed, egotistical manmukh loses his honor." (Ang 832). Maya is the structural trap: "Maya has caught the mind in the trap of the whirlpool. Each and every heart is trapped by this bait of poison and sin." (Ang 832).

**Mehl 3** (Ang 833) contributes Ashtapadees in the Tenth House, maintaining the Shabad-liberation framework: "Spiritual wisdom and meditation all come from the Guru. Through the lifestyle of Truth, the True Lord comes to dwell in the mind." (Ang 832). **Mehl 4** (Ang 834) follows with compositions organized under the Eleventh House. **Mehl 5** (Angs 836–840) closes the Ashtapadees with the Twelfth House, including a notable set of compositions on the Unstruck Sound-Current: "Those who listen, fascinated, to the Unstruck Sound-Current of the Word, will never have to see the Messenger of Death with their eyes." (Ang 838). The soul-string-of-love (*dor paraym pareet*) ties the devotee to the Formless Lord. An important Mehl 5 Ashtapadee explicitly names the bhagats: "Naam Dayv, Jai Dayv, Kabeer, Trilochan and Ravi Daas the low-caste" are listed among those saved by divine grace, whose bāṇī is endorsed (Ang ~837–838).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Bilaaval (Angs 858–859)

Three Bhagat poets appear in Raag Bilaaval — fewer than in most raags, but each composition carries distinctive weight.

**[[namdev|Namdev]]** (Ang 858): A short but theologically complete composition. The Guru's grace is the sole vehicle: "The Guru has made my life fruitful. My pain is forgotten, and I have found peace deep within myself. The Guru has blessed me with the ointment of spiritual wisdom. Without the Lord's Name, life is mindless." (Ang 858). Namdev closes with the merger-formula: "Meditating in remembrance, Naam Dayv has come to know the Lord. His soul is blended with the Lord, the Life of the World." (Ang 858).

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]]** (Angs 858): Two compositions. The first begins from Ravidas's characteristic low-caste position: "Seeing my poverty, everyone laughed. Such was my condition. Now, I hold the eighteen miraculous spiritual powers in the palm of my hand; everything is by Your Grace." (Ang 858) — total reversal by divine grace. The second composition offers his most sweeping caste-erasure statement in this raag: "Whether he is a Brahmin, a Vaishya, a Soodra, or a Kh'shaatriya; whether he is a poet, an outcaste, or a filthy-minded person, he becomes pure, by meditating on the Lord God. He saves himself, and the families of both his parents." (Ang 858). The conclusion uses his signature water-lily image for detached living: "As the leaves of the water lily float free in the water, says Ravi Daas, so is their life in the world." (Ang 858).

**Sadhna** (Ang 858–859): The sole composition in the entire SGGS by Sadhna, a Muslim butcher-saint (*kasāi*) from Sehwan (Sindh), traditionally identified with the early Bhakti movement. The composition is a dense, urgent petition for divine rescue framed through two parables. The opening parable: a man disguised himself as Vishnu for a king's daughter for selfish motives, but God protected his honor anyway — establishing that divine grace exceeds human unworthiness. The appeal becomes existential: "For the sake of a single rain-drop, the rainbird suffers in pain. When its breath of life is gone, even an ocean is of no use to it." (Ang 858). The poet reaches the desperate argument: "Now, my life has grown weary, and I shall not last much longer... If I drown and die, and then a boat comes along, tell me, how shall I climb aboard?" (Ang 859). The composition closes in total self-abnegation: "I am nothing, I have nothing, and nothing belongs to me. Now, protect my honor; Sadhana is Your humble servant." (Ang 859). The inclusion of a Muslim butcher's sole bāṇī here — preceding Raag Gond — demonstrates the Granth's deliberate breadth of social and religious inclusion in its Bhagat corpus.

### Theological Summary

Raag Bilaaval's defining characteristic is the wedding of joy (*bilaaval*) with self-negation: the soul's bliss is discovered precisely through the erasure of ego. Mehl 1 establishes this via the paradox of speech — only God-enabled praise is possible. Mehl 3 makes the wedding motif explicit, deploying the rāg's own name as a spiritual event: divine union fills the household with joyful song. Mehl 4 grounds the theology in community (Saadh Sangat, the boat across Kali Yuga) and social inclusivity (any caste, Ravidas the leather-worker). Mehl 5's non-dual sequences — "He Himself is the water, and He Himself is the waves" — represent the Granth's most sustained *āpay-āpay* theology in a single rāg. Mehl 9's *jivan mukta* statement and dismissal of external religion mark the raag's austere doctrinal peak. The Bhagat bāṇī (Namdev, Ravidas, Sadhna) extends the social range to calico-printer, cobbler, and Muslim butcher — unified by the single teaching that the Naam alone liberates, without regard to social position.

---

## Raag Gond (Angs 841–875)

Raag Gond spans 35 pages (Angs 841–875) and represents a compositionally unusual raag in the Granth: it contains only **Mehls 4 and 5** among the Guru bāṇī, with no Mehl 1, 3, or 9, and a rich Bhagat bāṇī section (Angs 870–875) featuring [[kabir|Kabir]], [[namdev|Namdev]], and [[ravidas|Ravidas]]. The section opens with the full extended Mool Mantar: "One Universal Creator God. Truth Is The Name. Creative Being Personified. No Fear. No Hatred. Image Of The Undying. Beyond Birth. Self-Existent. By Guru's Grace" — the complete invocatory formula that marks a major new section. In classical North Indian music, Raag Gond is associated with the evening and with a mood of settled devotion; within the Granth it carries a tone of relentless dependence upon God alone and pointed dismissal of worldly supports.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās (Angs 841–862)

[[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] opens Gond with a meditation on hope and its proper object. The foundational verse: "If, in his conscious mind, he places his hopes in the Lord, then he shall obtain the fruits of all the many desires of his mind. The Lord knows everything which happens to the soul. Not even an iota of one's effort goes to waste." (Ang 859). The teaching is sustained across multiple Chau-Padās: hope in *anything other than God* — family, friends, wealth, Maya — is systematically deconstructed.

The most distinctive Mehl 4 contribution to Gond is an extended practical theology of sorrow-sharing. A composition explicitly counsels against confiding one's troubles in other humans: "If you tell your sorrows to another, then he, in return, will tell you of his greater sorrows... So tell your sorrows to the Lord, your Lord and Master, who shall instantly dispel your pain." (Ang 860). The logic is consistent: worldly relationships are entirely self-interested — "all meet with you for their own purposes... that day, when their self-interests are not served, on that day, they shall not come near you." (Ang 860). Only God persists: "Wherever I go, my Lord and Master is there with me. The Lord saves the honor of His humble servants and slaves." (Ang 860). The theological conclusion states divine determinism without exception: "Nothing is in the hands of anyone, O my Lord and Master; such is the understanding the True Guru has given me to understand." (Ang 860).

A closely related teaching on sin and the Lord as purifier: "Serve such a Lord, and ever meditate on Him, who in an instant erases all sins and mistakes." (Ang 860). If one forsakes God and places hope in another, "all his service to the Lord is rendered fruitless." (Ang 860). The Guru's *sangat* (*sangtee*) is again the practical locus of liberation: "Joining the Holy Congregation, I sing the Glorious Praises of the Lord." (Ang 801/860).

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās (Angs 862–870)

[[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]]'s Chau-Padā section in Gond opens with the most compressed non-dual Creator-formula in the raag:

> "He is the Creator of all, He is the Enjoyer of all. The Creator listens, and the Creator sees. The Creator is unseen, and the Creator is seen. The Creator forms, and the Creator destroys. The Creator touches, and the Creator is detached. The Creator is the One who speaks, and the Creator is the One who understands. The Creator comes, and the Creator also goes. The Creator is absolute and without qualities; the Creator is related, with the most excellent qualities. By Guru's Grace, Nanak looks upon all the same." (Ang 862)

The final phrase — *samdristaa*, equal vision — identifies the goal of Guru's grace as non-discriminating sight: all phenomena seen as the One Creator. This is the theological anchor for everything that follows in Gond.

Mehl 5 then deploys extended animalistic entrapment imagery to describe the soul's condition: "You are caught, like the fish and the monkey; you are entangled in the transitory world. Your foot-steps and your breaths are numbered; only by singing the Glorious Praises of the Lord will you be saved." (Ang 862). The five thieves (*panj chor*) framework appears: "You have fallen under the power of the five thieves; escape is only possible in the Sanctuary of the Lord." (Ang 862). Creation theology appears in a tender form: God "fashioned the soul and the breath of life, and infused His Light into the dust" (Ang 863) — then provided everything to sustain the creature. The rhetorical challenge: "how can you forsake that God, you fool! Where else will you go?" (Ang 863). The Name is the supreme treasure: "His Name is the True Primal Being; millions of sins are washed away in an instant, singing His Praises... The Lord God is your best friend, your playmate from earliest childhood." (Ang 863).

Subsequent Mehl 5 Chau-Padās in Gond extend across Angs 863–870, maintaining the consistent theme of radical dependence (*sarnaagat*, taking refuge) and the Naam as the sole support in all circumstances. A notable devotional formula: "Meditate on Mukanday in life, and meditate on Mukanday in death" — the epithet *Mukanday* (Liberator/Lord of Liberation) recurs intensely in the Bhagat bāṇī as well. The Mehl 5 compositions also intensify the grain/cultivation imagery: grain that brings faith in the Lord and Master to the mind is "blessed." (Ang 870).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Raag Gond (Angs 870–875)

The Bhagat bāṇī of Gond contains some of the most narratively dramatic compositions in the entire Granth, drawn from the corpus of [[kabir|Kabir]], [[namdev|Namdev]], and [[ravidas|Ravidas]].

**[[kabir|Kabir]] (Angs 870–872)**: Four compositions.

The *first* narrates the miracle of the elephant: Kabir was "tied up, bundled, and thrown before an elephant" (presumably by a Muslim official hostile to the saint). The elephant driver was ordered by the Qazi to trample him; the elephant "ran away, trumpeting" and bowed repeatedly before the saint. "Three times, he tried to do it... Says Kabeer, such is my Lord and Master. The soul of His humble servant dwells in the fourth state." (Ang 871). The *chautha pad* ("fourth state," *turīya*) — the state of liberation beyond the three *gunas* — appears here as the protector: the saint in the fourth state cannot be destroyed by worldly force.

The *second* composition offers Kabir's metaphysical non-dual paradox on the soul, using a sustained *neti-neti* ("not this, not that") form:

> "It is not human, and it is not a god. It is not called celibate, or a worshipper of Shiva. It is not a Yogi, and it is not a hermit. It is not a mother, or anyone's son. Then what is it, which dwells in this temple of the body? No one can find its limits." (Ang 871–872)

The answer: "By Guru's Grace, I have found the Path. Birth and death have both been erased. Says Kabeer, this is formed of the same essence as the Lord. It is like the ink on the paper which cannot be erased." (Ang 872). The soul is neither destroyed by death nor separable from God — it is *Ram kee ans*, of the same essence as the Lord.

The *third* composition is Kabir's sharpest social critique in Gond. Using his own weaver-identity against religious mendicants, Kabir describes a household whose weaving equipment lies abandoned — "his daughters and sons have nothing to eat" — while "the shaven-headed mendicants night and day eat their fill" (Ang 872). The inversion is deliberate: the self-denying ascetic community is parasitic, yet the poem ends with Kabir taking shelter with them. The tension is unresolved, characteristic of Kabir's *ulat-bāṃsī* (inverted speech) style.

The *fourth* personifies Maya as the universal bride-prostitute: "The world loves only the one bride, Maya. She is the wife of all beings and creatures. With her necklace around her neck, this bride looks beautiful. She is poison to the Saint, but the world is delighted with her." (Ang 872). But Grace breaks the spell: "In His Mercy, the Divine Guru met me. Says Kabeer, now I have thrown her out. She clings to the skirt of the world." (Ang 872).

**[[namdev|Namdev]] (Angs 873–875)**: Seven compositions across two Houses.

The *first house* opens with a sweeping rejection of all Vedic ritual as inferior to Naam-praise: horse sacrifice, giving one's weight in gold, ceremonial baths at Prayag, offering rice at Gaya, living at Benares, reciting the four Vedas — all are enumerated and dismissed: "These are not equal to singing the Praises of the Lord's Name." (Ang 873). Namdev's positive teaching is pure Naam-intensity: "Meditate, meditate in remembrance on the Lord of the Universe. Meditate, O Naam Dayv, and cross over the terrifying world-ocean." (Ang 873).

A signature Namdev composition presents desire-comparison verses: as the deer is lured by the hunter's bell and loses its life without ceasing to fixate on the sound; as the fisherman looks upon the fish; as the goldsmith eyes the gold; as the lustful man gazes at another's wife — "In the same way, wherever Naam Dayv looks, he sees the Lord. Naam Dayv meditates continuously on the Feet of the Lord." (Ang 873). The devotee's gaze mirrors the obsessions of worldly desire but with the Lord as sole object.

The prayer-of-childlike-dependence: "Carry me across, O Lord, carry me across. I am ignorant, and I do not know how to swim. O my Beloved Father, please give me Your arm." (Ang 874). A transformation formula: "I have been transformed from a mortal being into an angel, in an instant; the True Guru has taught me this." (Ang 874). 

A viraha composition of striking simplicity uses animal-longing imagery: "Without her calf, the cow is lonely. Without water, the fish writhes in pain. So is poor Naam Dayv without the Lord's Name." (Ang 874). Then the resolution: "So has Naam Dayv found the Lord. Meeting the Guru, I have seen the Unseen Lord." (Ang 874).

The *second house* opens with one of the most cited anti-caste and anti-ritual statements in the Granth: "Chanting the Name of the Lord, Har, Har, all doubts are dispelled. Chanting the Name of the Lord is the highest religion. Chanting the Name of the Lord, Har, Har, erases social classes and ancestral pedigrees. The Lord is the walking stick of the blind." (Ang 874). A catalogue of miracles follows: Harnaakhash slain; Ajaamal given a place in heaven; the prostitute Ganika saved by teaching a parrot to chant the Lord's Name; Pootna the child-killer saved; Dropadi saved; Gautam's wife turned to stone and saved (Ang 874). Each case extends the logic: if these were saved, all are saveable by Naam alone.

The final inter-religious verse is among the most quoted in the Granth:

> "The Hindu is sightless; the Muslim has only one eye. The spiritual teacher is wiser than both of them. The Hindu worships at the temple, the Muslim at the mosque. Naam Dayv serves that Lord, who is not limited to either the temple or the mosque." (Ang 875)

This verse — simultaneously a critique of both Hindu image-worship and Islamic mosque-centered practice — positions Naam-devotion as the universal *third path* that transcends both institutional religions.

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]] (Ang 875)**: Two compositions.

The *first* centers on the epithet *Mukanday* (Liberator) — Ravidas's preferred divine name in this raag: "Meditate on the Lord Mukanday, the Liberator, O people of the world. Without Mukanday, the body shall be reduced to ashes... Mukanday is my father and mother. Meditate on Mukanday in life, and meditate on Mukanday in death. His servant is blissful forever." (Ang 875). From Ravidas's low-caste cobbler position, the Liberator simultaneously frees from both sin and social bondage: "Erasing my social status, I have entered His Court." (Ang 875). The closing self-characterization: "In His Mercy, the Lord has made this worm His slave. Says Ravi Daas, now my thirst is quenched; I meditate on Mukanday the Liberator, and I serve Him." (Ang 875).

The *second* composition takes an unusual form — a sustained anti-slander poem that catalogs all possible acts of piety and declares them void if accompanied by slander of Saints. Bathing at sixty-eight pilgrimage sites, worshipping twelve Shiva-lingam stones, digging wells and pools, bathing during a solar eclipse at Kurukshetra, offering a decorated wife, listening to all the Simritees, giving countless feasts, donating land — "but if he indulges in slander, then all of this is useless." (Ang 875). The force of the teaching: no outer religiosity compensates for slander; only inner purity matters. "How can the slanderer of the Holy Saints be saved? Know for certain, that he shall go to hell." (Ang 875). Ravidas's conclusion identifies the slanderer's fate definitively: "Says Ravi Daas, he is a sinner; he shall go to hell." (Ang 875). The total composition count closes with the section-ending formula: "||4||2||11||7||2||49|| Total||" — 49 total compositions in Raag Gond.

### Theological Summary

Raag Gond's dominant contribution is a relentless theology of **exclusive dependence**: on God alone, on the Naam alone, on the Guru alone — with every other support (family, friends, worldly allies, external religion) systematically exposed as either self-interested or temporary. Mehl 4 develops this practically through the sorrow-sharing teaching: God is the only confidant who does not burden you in return. Mehl 5 develops it cosmologically through the *samdristaa* (equal-vision) teaching and the Creator-as-all formula. The Bhagat bāṇī then enacts this teaching through three lives: Kabir the weaver (the fourth state protects from elephant and Maya alike), Namdev the calico-printer (devotion erases caste and ritual religion alike), and Ravidas the cobbler (Mukanday the Liberator erases social status at the divine court). The raag closes with Ravidas's warning that no piety substitutes for the inner condition — making Gond a sustained argument that liberation is available to all but only through total inner reorientation toward [[akal-purakh|Akal Purakh]].
---

## Raag Raamkalee (Angs 876–974)

Raag Raamkalee is the longest single section in the SGGS, spanning 99 Angs (pages) and housing the most philosophically ambitious compositions in the entire Granth. Its scope encompasses short Chau-Padās, extended Ashtapadīs, lyric Chhants, a major Var, and three landmark long-form works — the **Anand Sahib** (the "Song of Bliss," the centerpiece of Sikh daily liturgy), **Dakhanee Oankaar** (a cosmological meditation on Ongkaar as the origin of all things), and **Sidh Gosht** (a recorded dialogue between Guru Nanak and the Nath Siddha yogis). The raag also includes substantial Bhagat Bāṇī, prominently from Kabeer. Together these compositions make Raamkalee the theological and philosophical heart of the SGGS.

### Early Compositions (Angs 876–916)

The opening Chau-Padās and Ashtapadīs by Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) and Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das) establish the raag's characteristic preoccupations: the contrast between the Gurmukh (one oriented toward the Guru) and the Manmukh (self-willed ego), the indispensability of the Guru's Shabad, and the pervasive operation of Hukam (Divine Command).

Mehl 1 opens with meditations on the body's transience and the soul's entanglement in Maya. "Without the Guru, the mortal is blind and ignorant; the Name does not come to dwell in the mind" (Ang 876). The early Ashtapadīs develop a sustained critique of ritualism without interiority: external bathing at tīrthas, fasting, and pilgrimage are dismissed as insufficient without Naam. The Gurmukh, by contrast, "bathes in the Pool of Truth at the source of the stream...within his own home" (Ang 879).

Mehl 3's Ashtapadīs elaborate the doctrine that the Guru-disciple relationship is the only path through Maya's three gunas. "One who knows the essence of reality through the Guru's Teachings is emancipated; the Manmukh is caught in the net of the three qualities" (Ang 884). Repeated stanzas describe the rare soul who, by Guru's grace, crosses from the world of duality into the state of sahaj (natural, effortless equipoise): "In the realm of the True Guru, there is intuitive peace and poise — the mind is not afflicted by hope or anxiety" (Ang 888).

Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) and Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) contribute additional Ashtapadīs and Chhants that develop the bridal-mysticism register: the soul as the longing bride awaiting union with the Lord-Husband. "She who is imbued with love for her Husband Lord is the true soul-bride; night and day she remains absorbed in Him" (Ang 912). Mehl 5's Chhants in this section are notable for their imagery of the monsoon season as an analogy for the soul's longing: the rains that revive the parched earth figure the divine grace that revives the soul. "My mind is drenched with the rain of His love; the soul-bride is in bliss meeting her Lord" (Ang 914).

### Anand Sahib — The Song of Bliss (Angs 917–922)

**Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das); 40 stanzas**

The Anand Sahib is the canonical hymn of Sikh joy and is prescribed reading in the Nit Nem (daily prayers). Its 40 stanzas form a complete arc from the opening declaration of ecstasy through the progressive unfolding of divine union, concluding with the Anhad Naad (unstruck celestial sound current) at the Tenth Gate. As a liturgical composition it is recited at all major Sikh rites: at dawn, at the conclusion of Ardās, at Amrit Sanskar, and at death rites. The full 40 stanzas are prescribed on all occasions; in abbreviated form (stanzas 1–5 and 40), it closes most sangat gatherings.

**Opening (Ang 917):** "Anand bha-i-aa mayree maa-ay satguroo mai paa-i-aa. / I am in ecstasy, O my mother, for I have found my True Guru." The stanza announces the discovery of the True Guru as the source of all joy (Anand). The soul's ecstasy is not self-generated but received — the fruit of grace.

**The five demons and the celestial sound (Ang 917–918):** Stanza 5 identifies the five primal sounds (Panch Shabad) resonating in the "blessed house" of the awakened soul: "Through You, we subdue the five demons of desire, and slay Death, the torturer." The five *shabad* are the celestial resonance of the Lord's Name heard inwardly, transcending the five senses corrupted by passion, anger, greed, attachment, and pride.

**Bliss as Guru-given (Ang 917–918):** Stanza 7 corrects a potential misreading: "Bliss, bliss — everyone talks of bliss; bliss is known only through the Guru." Anand is not generic happiness or mystical sensation achievable by technique; it is specifically the fruit of the Guru's Shabad. Without the Guru, talk of bliss is empty.

**The difficult path (Ang 918–919):** Stanza 14 describes the devotee's way as demanding: "The lifestyle of the devotees is unique and distinct; they follow the most difficult path. The path they take is sharper than a two-edged sword, and finer than a hair." The imagery of a razor's edge (echoing the Kaṭha Upaniṣad) insists that authentic devotion is not quietism but a sustained discipline against the ego.

**Progression through interiority:** The middle stanzas trace the soul's movement inward through Naam meditation. Stanza 19: "O my mind, drink in the ambrosial nectar — drink it in forever." Stanza 20 identifies five divine melodies heard in meditation. Stanza 27 addresses the rare ones who have received the complete treasure: "The Gurmukhs know the Unstruck Sound-current of the Shabad."

**The Shabad as the True Guru:** Central to the Anand Sahib is the equation of the Shabad with the living Guru. Stanza 35: "My True Guru is the Primal Lord God; the Word of the Shabad is His Form." This anticipates the transfer of Guruship from the personal Gurus to the Granth itself.

**Closing (Ang 922):** Stanza 40 seals the composition with the image of the Anhad Naad, the unstruck sound current, vibrating at the Tenth Gate: "Prays Nanak, touching the Guru's Feet, the unstruck sound current of the celestial bugles vibrates and resounds. ||40||1||" The Tenth Gate (*dasvā dvār*) is the spiritual center beyond the nine bodily gates (eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, navel, genitals, anus), the locus of divine experience accessed through Naam meditation.

### Raamkalee Sadd — The Call of Death (Angs 923–924)

Immediately following the Anand Sahib is the *Sadd* ("Call" or "Summons"), attributed to Guru Amar Das at the moment of his own death. It frames death not as loss but as the soul's call to merge with the Lord. "The Angel of Death does not come near the Gurmukh; the True Lord Himself calls for him." The composition establishes the theological context for Sikh mortuary practice: the death of a saint is a homecoming, not a tragedy, and Naam remains the soul's passport.

### Chhants and Shaloks Before Oankaar (Angs 924–929)

The interlude between the *Sadd* and Oankaar includes Mehl 4 and Mehl 5 Chhants continuing the themes of yearning and union. Notable among the Shaloks is the figure of the *sunmukh* (face turned toward the Guru): "One who turns to face the Guru, the True Guru, the Lord is revealed to them" (Ang 927). The *baymukh* (one who turns away) is condemned to the cycle of rebirth. This terminology established a crucial soteriological binary for Sikh theology.

### Dakhanee Oankaar — Cosmological Meditation on Ongkaar (Angs 929–938)

**Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak); 54 stanzas**

The *Dakhanee Oankaar* ("Ongkaar of the South," so titled because Guru Nanak composed it during his journey to southern India) is the SGGS's sustained cosmological treatise, tracing the role of Ongkaar (the sacred syllable Ik Ongkaar, "One Universal Creator God") as the primordial generative principle. Each of the 54 stanzas begins with a letter of the Gurmukhi alphabet in sequence, making the composition an acrostic meditation — a formal device communicating that the divine word permeates every syllable and sound of human language.

**Opening stanzas (Ang 929):** "From Ongkaar, Brahma was created. From Ongkaar, the mountains and the ages were created. Ongkaar created the Vedas. Ongkaar saves the world through the Shabad. Ongkaar saves the Gurmukhs." The first stanzas list what Ongkaar originates: cosmic time (the four yugas), cosmic space (the mountains, the earth), sacred text (the Vedas), and salvation itself (via the Shabad). All cosmology reduces to the single primordial syllable.

**Non-dualism (Ang 930):** "There is One Universal Creator Lord; there is no other at all. O Nanak, merge into the One Lord." Stanza 4: "The One Lord is in all actions, colors and forms. He manifests in many shapes through wind, water and fire." The cosmic multiplicity of creation does not compromise divine unity; all forms are expressions of the single Ongkaar.

**Critique of Brahminical ritual (Ang 930–931):** Stanzas address Brahmin pundits who know the Vedas but not their source. "The Pandit reads the scriptures, but his doubts are not dispelled; he is absorbed in the desire for wealth and Maya." Without the Guru's grace, textual knowledge produces pride rather than liberation. The same critique applies to Muslim scholars: "The Mullah reads the Qur'an, but his evil-mindedness is not dispelled." Knowledge becomes hollow without an interior transformation rooted in Naam.

**The Guru and the Shabad (Ang 933–934):** The middle stanzas develop the relationship between the human Guru, the divine Word, and the soul's liberation. "He alone is a true Brahmin, who knows the Lord God. The Gurmukh understands the One Lord who created this creation." The Brahmin of the spirit supersedes the Brahmin of birth; true Vedic knowledge is knowledge of the One. "The Guru has shown me the True Path; I am absorbed in the essence of reality."

**Creation, Maya, and the Fourth State (Ang 935–937):** Oankaar develops an account of the soul's entrapment in the three gunas (the qualities of Maya: rajas, tamas, sattva) and the possibility of rising to the *turiya* (fourth) state beyond the three. "He alone crosses over the terrible ocean of existence, who remains attached to the Guru's Shabad...The world is engrossed in the three qualities; without the fourth state, understanding is not obtained" (Ang 936). Liberation requires not renunciation of the world but transcendence of Maya's three-quality structure through Naam.

**Closing (Ang 937–938):** The final stanza (54) returns to the acrostic's culmination: "He alone is a wise Pandit, who wears the necklace of the Lord's Name. ||54||1||" True wisdom is not Vedic erudition but Naam meditation. The theological trajectory from the opening cosmological sweep to this devotional conclusion encapsulates the movement that Guru Nanak insists is the only valid response to creation: from the recognition of Ongkaar as origin to the wearing of Naam as the only ornament that matters.

### Sidh Gosht — Conversations with the Siddhas (Angs 938–946)

**Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak); 73 stanzas**

The Sidh Gosht ("Discourse with the Siddhas") is the SGGS's most extended philosophical dialogue and its most sustained engagement with a rival tradition. The Nath Siddhas were a pan-Indian ascetic movement combining Shaiva tantra, hatha yoga, breath-control, renunciation, and claims to immortality through yogic power (*siddhi*). Their principal figures — Gorakh (Gorakhnath), Machhindranath (Matsyendranath), and others — were famous across medieval India. The Gosht records a structured dialogue in which the Siddhas question Guru Nanak and he responds, culminating in a comprehensive critique of Nath yoga and an alternative articulation of Gurmukh spirituality rooted in Naam and sahaj.

**Setting and opening challenge (Ang 938–939):** The Siddhas open with an identity challenge. Stanza 2: "Who are you? What is your name? What is your way? What is your goal? We are very pleased to see you; tell us your way of life, O young man." Nanak's reply in stanza 3 refuses self-identification by lineage or sect: "He [the Lord] dwells deep within the nucleus of each and every heart. This is my seat and my home. I walk in harmony with the Will of the True Guru. Nanak is the slave of the Lord." The answer reframes the question: the true "home" is not a monastery or a mountain but the interior space of the divine Naam.

**The core critique of Nath renunciation (Ang 939–941):** The Siddhas ask why Nanak wanders as a householder rather than adopting their ascetic withdrawal. Stanza 7: "O Nanak, how can you cross over the terrifying world-ocean without withdrawing your mind from it?" Nanak's reply (stanza 8) inverts their logic: the householder who practices Naam is more genuinely free than the renunciant who remains inwardly attached. "In the midst of this world, do seva, and you shall be given a place of honor in the Court of the Lord." True liberation is achieved through engagement guided by Guru's grace, not through forest retreat. Stanza 9: "Without the Guru, no one finds the way, even though they may give hundreds of thousands of teachings."

**On Shabad as the True Guru (Ang 941–943):** The dialogue turns to the question of authority. The Siddhas press: who is your Guru? Stanzas 43–44 contain Nanak's most celebrated answer on this question. Siddhas: "What is the root, the source of all? Who is your Guru?" Nanak: "From the air came the beginning...This is the age of the True Guru's Teachings. The Shabad is the Guru, upon whom I lovingly focus my consciousness; I am the chaylaa, the disciple." The Shabad (the divine Word, the living articulation of Ongkaar) is the Guru — not a human teacher bound by time, not a text separated from its source, but the living resonance of the divine Name experienced inwardly.

**The Tenth Gate and the Anhad Naad (Ang 941–942):** Nanak addresses the Nath yogis' system of bodily channels and chakras. The Siddhas work with the three channels (Ida, Pingala, Sushmana) to direct prāna and achieve mystical experience. Nanak accepts the framework but relocates the decisive locus: "Above the three channels [Ida, Pingala, Sushmana] is the Tenth Gate; there, the Anhad Naad [unstruck sound current] vibrates and resounds." The Gurmukh who ascends through Naam reaches beyond the yogic system's ceiling; the Nath methodology cannot reach what Guru's grace opens. Stanza 51: "The absolute Lord is deep within; the absolute Lord is outside us as well...One who knows the Lord in the fourth state, is not subject to virtue or vice."

**On Nath powers and liberation (Ang 942–944):** The Siddhas invoke their *siddhi* powers (supernatural abilities) as evidence of spiritual attainment. Nanak deflects: "The Siddhas sit in their Samaadhi — they feel so proud of themselves. But without meeting the True Guru, the Naam is not obtained, and finally they depart, regretting and repenting." Yogic power (*siddhi*) is a byproduct of practice, not liberation. Liberation requires Naam, and Naam requires the Guru's grace. Stanza 60: "Whoever You forgive, O Lord, is absorbed in the True Name; they are the supreme Siddhas among all the Siddhas."

**Grief, death, and liberation (Ang 944–946):** The dialogue closes with exchanges on how death is confronted. The Gurmukh does not fear the Messenger of Death because the Naam has dissolved the ego's grip. Stanza 65: "The mind of the Gurmukh is in love with the Lord; night and day, he remains in the Guru's service." Stanza 68: "O Nanak, without the Name, Yoga is never attained." The final stanza (73) resolves the entire dialogue with a declaration of divine immanence: "The eternal imperishable Lord God has staged this play; the Gurmukh understands it. O Nanak, He extends Himself throughout the ages; there is no other than Him. ||73||1||" The cosmos is the Lord's play (*khel*); only the Gurmukh, oriented through the Guru's grace, perceives this correctly.

**Sidh Gosht as theological statement:** The Gosht is not a casual exchange. Its formal structure — alternating Siddha challenge and Nanak response, 73 stanzas with refrain — signals deliberate composition as a systematic philosophical treatise. The Nath tradition represented the dominant "spiritual elite" of its era; the Gosht systematically addresses each of their claims (renunciation as necessary, yogic technique as sufficient, physical withdrawal as liberation, supernatural powers as validation, the Guru as human teacher) and replaces each with a Gurmukh counterpart (householder engagement, Naam as sufficient, interior devotion, the Shabad as the true siddhi, the eternal Shabad as the true Guru).

### Var of Raamkalee (Angs 947–964)

**Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das)**
