The Var of Raamkalee, composed by Guru Amar Das, is prescribed to be sung to the tune of "Jodha and Veera Poorbaanee" — a heroic folk ballad-tune, giving the theological content martial energy. Its Paurees (stanzas) and interspersed Shaloks develop the themes of the Manmukh/Gurmukh binary with sustained agricultural imagery.

The Var opens with the image of the Guru as a field of wisdom: "The True Guru is the field of intuitive wisdom...plants the seed of the Name there. The Name sprouts up, and he remains absorbed in the Name. But this egotism is the seed of skepticism; it has been uprooted" (Ang 947). Haumai (ego/egotism) is figured as the competing seed that produces only doubt and suffering: "The self-willed manmukh is the field of sorrow and suffering. He plants sorrow, and eats sorrow. In sorrow he is born, and in sorrow he dies. Acting in egotism, his life passes away" (Ang 947).

Later Shaloks intersperse Mehl 5 compositions. The pattern of alternating voices (Mehl 3 Paurees with Mehl 3 and Mehl 1 Shaloks) characteristic of the Var format allows the collection to build toward cumulative theological depth. Pauree 17 (Ang 965): "One who has such good karma inscribed upon his forehead, is committed to the Lord's service. One whose heart lotus blossoms forth upon meeting the Guru, remains awake and aware, night and day." The image of the lotus blooming (*kamal pargasi-aa*) at the Guru's feet is a recurrent figure for awakening in Raamkalee's Var.

The Var also includes Mehl 5 Shaloks on the spousal mysticism register: "When the Husband Lord is within the heart, then Maya, the bride, goes outside. When one's Husband Lord is outside of oneself, then Maya, the bride, is supreme" (Ang 965). The interior presence of the divine reverses the soul's captivity to Maya; union with the Lord is itself the liberation.

The Var closes with a remarkable Pauree (Ang 968) that encodes succession within the Guru tradition: "Guru Arjun sits on the throne; the royal canopy waves over the True Guru. From east to west, He illuminates the four directions...The four Gurus enlightened the four ages; the Lord Himself assumed the fifth form. ||8||1||" This Pauree was composed to affirm the succession of the Guruship from Guru Nanak through Guru Arjan and encodes a theological claim: each human Guru is not a new light but the same light in a new vessel.

### Bhagat Bāṇī in Raamkalee (Angs 969–974)

The raag's Bhagat section is dominated by Kabeer, whose compositions develop distinctive imagery of intoxication, weaving, and yogic body metaphysics in ways that simultaneously engage and subvert the Nath framework.

**Kabeer's wine-mysticism (Ang 969–970):** Kabeer's first composition is an extended metaphor of spiritual intoxication using the imagery of a distillery. "Make your body the vat, and mix in the yeast. Let the Word of the Guru's Shabad be the molasses...Cut up desire, sexuality, anger, pride and envy, and let them be the fermenting bark" (Ang 969). The five passions that the ego cultivates are recast as the fermenting agents for a superior intoxication. The second composition deepens the image: "Make spiritual wisdom the molasses, meditation the flowers, and the Fear of God the fire enshrined in your mind. The Shushmanaa, the central spinal channel, is intuitively balanced, and the drinker drinks in this wine" (Ang 969–970). Kabeer appropriates the Nath body metaphysics — Sushmana (the central yogic channel), Ida, and Pingala appear — but redirects them toward Naam-intoxication rather than prāna manipulation. This parallels the Sidh Gosht's move: the body's architecture is real, but its activation comes through Shabad rather than technique.

**Kabeer on Magahar and Benares (Ang 970–971):** One of the Bhagat section's most philosophically pointed compositions addresses the superstition that dying at Benares (Kashi) guarantees liberation while dying at Magahar guarantees rebirth as a donkey. Kabeer declares: "Relying upon You, I can live even in the cursed place of Magahar; You have put out the fire of my body...As is Magahar, so is Benares; I see them as one and the same" (Ang 970). This is not merely iconoclasm; it is a direct assertion that liberation is a function of the Lord's grace mediated through interior Naam, not a product of geography. Kabeer historicizes his own position: tradition holds that he died at Magahar precisely to make this point.

**Kabeer on saints and skeptics:** "O Kabeer, no one is mine, and I belong to no one. I am absorbed in the One, who created this creation" (Ang 965, Mehl 5 Shalok quoting Kabeer). The Bhagat Bāṇī section's Kabeer compositions collectively enforce the same non-reliance on external props — holy places, ritual, institutional authority — that characterizes the Guru tradition's engagement with both Nath yoga and Brahminical religion.

---

### Theological Summary: Raamkalee's Distinctive Contributions

Raamkalee is the SGGS's most philosophically ambitious raag. Its distinctive theological contributions include:

1. **The cosmological grounding of Naam**: Oankaar traces the divine Name's role not merely as a devotional practice but as the generative principle of the cosmos. Naam is not a technique for achieving states; it is the structure of reality itself.

2. **The systematic refutation of Nath yoga**: Sidh Gosht is the SGGS's most sustained philosophical argument. It engages the most sophisticated rival spiritual tradition of Guru Nanak's era and systematically replaces each of its central claims (renunciation, yogic technique, teacher-disciple lineage, supernatural powers) with a Gurmukh alternative rooted in Naam and sahaj.

3. **Anand as liturgical theology**: The Anand Sahib does not merely describe joy; its 40-stanza arc enacts the soul's movement from longing to union. Its mandatory liturgical role ensures that Sikh theology is transmitted not primarily as doctrine but as enacted devotional experience.

4. **The Shabad as living Guru**: Across all three major compositions — Anand Sahib, Oankaar, Sidh Gosht — the Shabad (divine Word) replaces the human teacher as the ultimate spiritual authority. This theological move prepares for the transfer of Guruship to the Granth itself at Guru Gobind Singh's death.

5. **Gurmukh householder against Nath renunciant**: Raamkalee systematically counters the claim that liberation requires withdrawal from the world. The Gurmukh serves in the sangat, eats with the community, and practices Naam amid daily life; the Nath yogi's mountain retreat is, in Nanak's account, a more refined form of self-centered attachment.

**Key pages**: [[guru-nanak]], [[guru-amar-das]], [[guru-ram-das]], [[guru-arjan]], [[kabir]], [[haumai]], [[hukam]], [[gurmukh-manmukh]], [[nam-simaran]], [[akal-purakh]], [[nit-nem]], [[guru-granth-sahib]]

## Raag Nat Naaraayan (Angs 975–999)

A 25-page section opening with a fresh Mool Mantar invocation. The raag contains compositions by **Mehl 4** ([[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]]) and **Mehl 5** ([[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]]); no Bhagat Bāṇī appears here. The section includes a notable sub-raag: **Maalee Gauraa** (Angs ~986–999), which is listed under the Nat Naaraayan heading and follows the same two-Mehl structure.

Nat Naaraayan is associated with a tone of intense devotional longing met by divine mercy. The compositions maintain the viraha-and-union oscillation characteristic of Mehl 4 throughout the Granth, while Mehl 5 tilts toward the settled-surrender register. The section contains a **Partaal** (special rhythmic form with complex taal cycles) by both Mehl 4 and Mehl 5, one of the rarer composition sub-types in the Granth.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās and Partaal (Angs 975–985)

The Mehl 4 chau-padās open with the refrain "O my mind, chant the Name of the Lord, day and night" (*mayray man jap ahinis naam haray*), establishing the urgency of constant Simran as the structural theme. Key theological moves:

- **Naam as universal cleanser**: "Millions and millions of sins and mistakes, committed through countless lifetimes, shall all be put aside and sent away." (Ang 975) — the Naam is not merely curative for specific sins but retroactively dissolves the entire karmic inheritance.
- **Merger like water in water**: "Meditating on Him, His humble servant merges with Him, like water with water" (*jan jap mil salal sallay*, Ang 975–976). The same salal-salal (water-in-water) merger image that appears in Siree Raag and Maajh here reaches its most concentrated form in Mehl 4.
- **Lotus-gazing-moon**: "The Lord's Saint loves the Lord in his mind, like the lotus flower gazing at the moon" (Ang 976) — one of the Granth's most beautiful viraha images; the lotus opening toward the moon parallels the soul's yearning for God.
- **Clouds and peacock**: "The clouds hang low, the clouds tremble with thunder, and the mind dances joyfully like the peacock" (Ang 976) — seasonal imagery of monsoon-joy applied to the soul receiving divine grace.
- **Bhagat Namdev protected**: A Mehl 4 chau-padā references Namdev's persecution by "evil villains" and God's protection of his honor (Ang 977) — one of the Granth's internal cross-endorsements of the Bhagats.
- **Unworthy stone carried across**: "O Lord, I am an unworthy stone. The Merciful Lord, in His Mercy, has led me to meet the Guru; through the Word of the Guru's Shabad, this stone is carried across." (Ang 981) — the stone metaphor for the spiritually inert soul rescued by Naam.
- **Thousands of eyes, thousands of forms, One God**: "The thousands of eyes are the eyes of God; the One God, the Primal Being, remains unattached... The One God, our Lord and Master, has thousands of forms; God alone, through the Guru's Teachings, saves us." (Nat Ashtapadees, Ang 981) — apophatic-yet-immanent omnipresence formulation.
- **Breath-by-breath Simran**: "With as many breaths as I have, I chant the Naam, under Guru's Instructions. Each and every breath which escapes me without the Naam — that breath is useless and corrupt." (Nat Ashtapadees, Ang 981) — the most exacting standard of Simran stated in the text so far.

The **Partaal** compositions (labeled *nat naaraa-in mehlaa 4 parh-taal*, Ang ~982) introduce a refrain of pure divine sound: "har ho ho ho nadar nihaal" — "Lord, ho ho ho, beholding You, I am enraptured" — the unusual ho-ho-ho vocalization marking the Partaal as closer to ecstatic utterance than conventional verse structure.

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās, Du-Padas, and Partaal (Angs ~985–985)

Mehl 5 compositions introduce the settled-devotion register characteristic of his voice:

- **Thirst imagery**: "My mind thirsts greatly for the Sight of the Lord" (*man pi-aas bahut darsaavai*, Ang ~985) — longing framed as spiritual thirst rather than viraha-grief.
- **"Whatever You do is sweet to my mind"** (*man meeth tuhaaro kee-o*, Ang ~985) — the signature Mehl 5 surrender formulation: not the pleading of Mehl 4 but acceptance of the divine will as inherently good.
- **Fear of death erased**: "The fear of the Messenger of Death has been erased; I have obtained peace" (*jam kee taraas mitee sukh paa-i-aa niksee ha-umai peer*, Ang ~986) — [[haumai|haumai]] is here described as a "pain" (*peer*) whose exit coincides with liberation from death-fear.
- **"Searching, searching, I became a renunciate"**: "Searching and searching, I became a renunciate; I found it in the Company of the Holy" (*khojat khojat bha-i-o bairaagee saaDhoo sang lahi-o*, Ang ~986) — the Saadh Sangat as the resolution of the seeker's restless quest.

### Maalee Gauraa (Angs ~986–999) — Sub-Raag within Nat Naaraayan

Labeled *raag maalee ga-urhaa* within the Nat Naaraayan section; Mehl 4 compositions dominate the earlier Maalee Gauraa, with Mehl 5 closing the section. The theological program largely continues the Nat Naaraayan register:

**Mehl 4 in Maalee Gauraa**: "I am afraid of the fear of God, and attuned to loving devotion; my heart and mind are immersed in the Guru's Teachings and have awoken" (*bhai bhaa-ay bhagat laago mayraa hee-araa man so-i-o gurmat jaagaa*, Ang ~988). The heart described as "asleep" without Naam and "awakened" through it.

**Mehl 5 in Maalee Gauraa**: 
- **Service to God vs. false service**: "Other service is false; it is performed every day, and Death hovers over one's head" (*avar tahlaa jhoothee-aa nit karai jam sir maar*, Ang ~987) — all worldly service is categorized as "false" (*jhooth*) compared to service of God; death as the constant overseer makes worldly employment spiritually worthless.
- **Cause of causes, ever-present**: "He is the Cause of causes, ever-present here and now" (*karan karaavan haajraa hajoor*, Ang ~988) — a compact statement of simultaneous divine causality and immanent presence.

### Raag Nat Naaraayan — Theological Summary

Nat Naaraayan's contribution to the Granth's Part 3 is the intensification of the Naam-as-breath-of-life theology to its most precise formulation: each breath without Naam is "useless and corrupt." The raag's dominant images — water in water, lotus and moon, peacock and monsoon, unworthy stone carried across — constitute one of the Granth's richest clusters of devotional metaphor for divine mercy operating on an inert but receptive soul. Mehl 4's viraha-petitions and Mehl 5's settled surrender represent the two poles of the Sikh devotional spectrum held in productive tension across the 25 pages.

---

## Raag Maaroo (Angs 1000–1106)

The second-largest raag section in Part 3, spanning 107 pages. Named for the desert in North Indian classical music, Maaroo is the Granth's primary register of **existential urgency, transience, and spiritual death-consciousness**. Where Sorat'h (Part 2) opened the death-urgency register, Maaroo develops it to its fullest extent, making the threat of wasted human life the organizing emotional center. The section contains four Guru-authors (Mehl 1, 3, 4, 5) in standard chau-padā, ashtapadī, chhant, anjulee, and the rare **Dakhanee** (southern composition) sub-genre, a long **Var of Maaroo** (Mehl 3 Paurees), and the most extensive single-raag **Bhagat Bāṇī** collection in Part 3: [[kabir|Kabir]] (largest set), [[namdev|Namdev]], [[jayadeva|Jayadeva]], and [[ravidas|Ravidas]].

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Chau-Padās (Angs ~989–993)

The Mehl 1 chau-padās open Part 3's Maaroo with the desert register immediately established:

- **Separation as the human condition**: "What separation could be worse than separation from the Lord? For those who are united with Him, what other union can there be? / Praise the Lord and Master, who, having created this play, beholds it." (Ang ~989) — the rhetorical question collapses all alternatives into one binary: separation from God or union with God. Every other distinction is dissolved.
- **Pre-ordained calling**: "Those who receive the call in the last hours of the night, chant the Name of their Lord and Master... You send out the call, Lord, to those who meditate on Your Name." (Ang 989) — divine election theology: God's call arrives in the pre-dawn (*amrit vela*) hours; only those who meditate hear it.
- **Joy and pain as fate**: "I have enjoyed the tastes, and now my pains have come to fruition; such is my pre-ordained destiny, O my mother." (Ang 989) — the karmic ledger as inevitability; no complaint, only acknowledgment.
- **Ghar-chhadnay** (leaving the house): "No one remains after leaving the house" — the universal departure from the body-house underscored as the inescapable horizon of all human activity (Ang ~990).

**Mehl 1 in Fifth House** (*ghar 5*, Ang 993): A distinct tonal register, more contemplative. "By the Guru's Teachings, the outer and inner are one; O Nanak, detachment has come about" (*gur bachnee baahar ghar ayko naanak bha-i-aa udaasee*) — the inner/outer duality collapsed through Guru's Word.

### Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das) — Chau-Padās (Angs ~993–994)

Mehl 3's Maaroo compositions continue the Hukam-acceptance theology dominant throughout his voice in the Granth:

- **Complete submission to Hukam**: "Wherever You seat me, there I sit, O my Lord and Master; wherever You send me, there I go." (Ang ~993) — radical Hukam-surrender: not merely acceptance of circumstances but *active contentment* with divine placement.
- **The Creator stages all plays**: "He thinks that good and bad deeds come from himself; this is the source of all evil. Whatever happens in this world is only by the Order of our Lord and Master." (Ang ~993) — a rigorous theological statement: the belief that one is the agent of good and evil is itself the fundamental error.
- **Reincarnation ended by dwelling in the inner home**: "Coming and going in reincarnation no longer exist, when one dwells in the home of the self within." (Ang 994) — the *nij ghar* (inner home) as the condition for ending samsara; not geographic or ritual, but inward.
- **Even the Righteous Judge is afraid**: "Who can call him to account, when the egotistical pride of his mind has been silenced? Even the Righteous Judge of Dharma is intimidated and afraid of him; he has entered the Sanctuary of the True Lord." (Ang ~993) — a bold assertion: Naam-absorption dissolves the entire karmic accountability system.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Chau-Padās (Angs ~995–999)

Mehl 4's Maaroo compositions cycle through familiar viraha-petition themes but with the desert register making the urgency sharper:

- **Five uncontrollable passions in the body**: "Within the body are the five evil and uncontrollable passions" destroyed in an instant by Naam (Angs 995–996) — echoing the Nat Naaraayan treatment but now the "five incurable diseases of the body-village" motif.
- **Siddhas aligned with the Naam**: The sequence includes a chau-padā referencing siddhs (*sidhh samaaN*) who are blended with the devotees of the Lord (Ang ~995) — an unusual acknowledgment that the Nath yogis are subsumed into the Naam community.

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Chau-Padās and Extended Forms (Angs ~999–1009)

Mehl 5's extensive Maaroo compositions carry the surrendered-servant register:

- **Hukam as sweetness**: "When I am under the Lord's Command, I find even hunger pleasurable; I know no difference between sorrow and joy. Whatever the Command of my Lord and Master is, I bow my forehead and accept it." (Ang 1000) — the most complete formulation of the Hukam-equanimity teaching in Part 3; sorrow and joy become epistemically indistinguishable to the Naam-imbued servant.
- **Loading the merchandise of Naam**: "I have purchased the jewel of the Naam and the Glorious Praises of the Lord; loading this merchandise, I have set out on my journey." (Ang 1000) — the merchant-traveler metaphor used to frame death itself as departure on a well-provisioned journey, not loss.
- **Searching ended in Saadh Sangat**: "Good karma has dawned for me... My struggle is ended; I have found peace and tranquility. All my wanderings have ceased." (Ang ~1000) — the conclusion of the seeker's restlessness in the Saadh Sangat's grace.

**Anjulee sub-form** (*maaroo anjulee mehlaa 5 ghar 7*, Ang ~1007): A formal prayer/supplication genre — the palms-joined form of petition. Content continues the trust-and-surrender register.

### Maaroo Ashtapadees — Mehl 1 (Angs ~1009–1033)

The Ashtapadee section of Maaroo opens with a fresh Mool Mantar and represents one of the densest theological passages in the entire Granth. Mehl 1's extended eight-stanza compositions deploy multiple registers simultaneously: critique of external religion, death-consciousness, body-stages theology, and inner-mystical cosmology.

Key compositions and verses:

- **Critique of Vedic religion and pilgrimage**: "Reciting and listening to the Vedas and the Puraanas, countless wise men have grown weary. So many in their various religious robes have grown weary, wandering to the sixty-eight sacred shrines of pilgrimage. The True Lord and Master is immaculate and pure. The mind is satisfied only by the One Lord." (Ang 1009) — the opening of the Ashtapadees states the futility of all textual and ritual religion in the most sweeping terms yet in this section.

- **Life-stages Ashtapadee** (Angs ~1014–1015): An extended body-stages composition cataloguing the entire human lifespan as governed by attachment and ego, rather than Naam. Key verses:
  > "One is born to achieve the four great objectives of life. The spirit has taken up its home in the material world. Driven by hunger, it sees the path of Maya's riches; this emotional attachment takes away the treasure of liberation." (Ang 1014)
  > "Engrossed in sexual desire, anger and egotism, he falls in love with his false relatives." (Ang 1014)
  > "He grows old, his body and youth waste away, and his throat is plugged with mucous; water flows from his eyes. His feet fail him, and his hands shake and tremble; the faithless cynic does not enshrine the Lord in his heart." (Ang 1014–1015)
  > "Without the Word of the Guru's Shabad, he does not understand himself. Without the Lord's Name, death cannot be avoided." (Ang 1015)
  
  This life-stages composition is among the Granth's most systematic accounts of the Manmukh's arc — from birth through the deceptions of youth and middle age to the degradations of old age — set entirely in the death register.

- **Inner-Light mystical Ashtapadee** (Angs ~1015–1016): A counterpoint composition in the Nath-yogic mystical register:
  > "Study the Lord's Name, and understand the Lord's Name; follow the Guru's Teachings, and through the Naam, you shall be saved. Perfect are the Teachings of the Perfect Guru; contemplate the Perfect Word of the Shabad." (Ang 1009)
  
  The Gurmukh's inner experience is described: "The dazzling light glitters, although neither the moon nor the stars are shining; neither the sun's rays nor the lightning flashes across the sky. I describe the indescribable state, which has no sign, where the all-pervading Lord is still pleasing to the mind." (Ang ~1016) — apophatic mystical vision where all physical light is superseded by the inner divine radiance.
  
  > "The rays of Divine Light have spread out their brilliant radiance. Having created the creation, the Merciful Lord Himself gazes upon it. The sweet, melodious, unstruck sound current vibrates continuously in the home of the fearless Lord." (Ang ~1016)

### Maaroo Dakhanee — Mehl 1 (Angs ~1033–1053)

The **Dakhanee** (Southern compositions) of Maaroo is one of the Granth's most concentrated treatments of **inner mystical geography**. *Dakhanee* designates a specific sub-genre associated with a southern musical style or Deccan Sufi-influenced register. In Maaroo, it is labeled *maaroo mehlaa 1 dakh-nee*.

The Dakhanee Maaroo opens with an extended composition on the **body-village** and **Tenth Gate** mystical anatomy — one of the Granth's fullest and most systematic treatments of this theme:

> "Deep within the body-village is the fortress. The dwelling of the True Lord is within the city of the Tenth Gate. This place is permanent and forever immaculate. He Himself created it." (Ang 1033)

> "Within the fortress are balconies and bazaars. He Himself takes care of His merchandise. The hard and heavy doors of the Tenth Gate are closed and locked. Through the Word of the Guru's Shabad, they are thrown open." (Ang 1033)

> "Within the fortress is the cave, the home of the self. He established the nine gates of this house, by His Command and His Will. In the Tenth Gate, the Primal Lord, the unknowable and infinite, dwells; the unseen Lord reveals Himself." (Ang 1033)

The body cosmology continues through multiple stanzas: "Within the body of air, water and fire, the One Lord dwells. He Himself stages His wondrous dramas and plays." (Ang ~1033–1034). The **sun-and-moon as divine lamps** motif (parallel to the Dhanaasaree Aartee) reappears: "The Lord placed the two lamps, the sun and the moon; the sun merges in the house of the moon" (Ang ~1034) — a Nath-yogic formulation where the solar (*soor*) and lunar (*chand*) channels meet in the Tenth Gate.

The Dakhanee Maaroo also contains one of the Granth's clearest creation-theology statements:
> "Creating the earth, He established it as the home of Dharma. Creating and destroying, He remains unattached. He stages the play of the breath everywhere. Withdrawing His power, He lets the beings crumble." (Ang ~1034)

> "The wind blowing around is the chauree, the fly-brush, waving over You. The Lord placed the two lamps, the sun and the moon." (Ang ~1034) — a cosmic Aartee parallel to Dhanaasaree's Aartee (Ang 663), here embedded in the Dakhanee register.

The long Dakhanee composition culminates in extended theological development through approximately 16 stanzas, concluding: "Please bless Nanak with the Truth, and preserve his honor; You are the supreme emperor above the heads of kings." (Ang ~1033) — the closing couplet reasserting the soul's complete dependence on divine authority over all earthly power.

### Var of Maaroo — Mehl 3 Paurees (Angs ~1087–1106)

The **Var of Maaroo** (*maaroo vaar mehlaa 3*) opens with a fresh Mool Mantar and Shaloks by Mehls 1 and 4, with Paurees by Mehl 3. It runs approximately 23 Paurees and represents one of the longer Vaars in the Granth.

**Opening Shaloks** establish the market-of-virtue metaphor:
> "If virtue is sold when there is no buyer, then it is sold very cheap. But if one meets a buyer of virtue, then virtue sells for hundreds of thousands. Meeting with a virtuous person, virtue is obtained, and one is immersed in the True Guru." (Shalok Mehl 1, Ang ~1087)

The Mehl 4 Shaloks introduce the Var's Gurmukh/Manmukh binary:
> "Without the Naam, the Name of the Lord, they wander around, continually coming and going in reincarnation. Some are in bondage, and some are set free; some are happy in the Love of the Lord. O Nanak, believe in the True Lord, and practice Truth, through the lifestyle of Truth." (Shalok Mehl 4, Ang ~1087)

**Paurees of Mehl 3** develop the *sword of spiritual wisdom* motif:
> "From the Guru, I have obtained the supremely powerful sword of spiritual wisdom. I have cut down the fortress of duality and doubt, attachment, greed and egotism. The Name of the Lord abides within my mind; I contemplate the Word of the Guru's Shabad." (Pauree 1, Ang ~1087)

Further Paurees develop: the One Lord as the sole Giver of virtue and vice ("The One Lord is the only Giver of virtue and vice, merits and demerits; how rare are those who, as Gurmukh, understand this," Ang ~1053); the inner Light within the polluted mind and body ("Within the polluted mind and body is the Light of the Infinite Lord," Ang ~1053); and the God who unites through Shabad-contemplation.

**Pauree 13** (Ang ~1091) develops the sovereignty of God's arrangement through a predestination theology applied to the Saadh Sangat:
> "He Himself has embellished the body, and placed the nine treasures of the Naam within it. He confuses some in doubt; fruitless are their actions. Some, as Gurmukh, realize their Lord, the Supreme Soul. Some listen to the Lord, and obey Him; sublime and exalted are their actions. Love for the Lord wells up deep within, singing the Glorious Praises of the Lord's Name." (Ang 1091)

### Raag Maaroo — Bhagat Bāṇī

The Bhagat Bāṇī of Maaroo is the most extensive in Part 3 and arguably the most philosophically dense in the Granth. It contains compositions by four authors, each with a separate Mool Mantar invocation.

#### Kabir in Raag Maaroo (Angs ~1103–1105)

Kabir's Maaroo compositions constitute his largest set in any single raag and display the full range of his theological register: institutional critique, death-urgency, non-dual mysticism, and paradox-speech. Key compositions:

**Against scriptural scholars (Pandits)**:
> "O Pandit, O religious scholar, in what foul thoughts are you engaged? You shall be drowned, along with your family, if you do not meditate on the Lord, you unfortunate person." (Ang 1103)
> "What is the use of reading the Vedas and the Puraanas? It is like loading a donkey with sandalwood. You do not know the exalted state of the Lord's Name; how will you ever cross over?" (Ang 1103)
> "You kill living beings, and call it a righteous action. Tell me, brother, what would you call an unrighteous action? You call yourself the most excellent sage; then who would you call a butcher?" (Ang 1103) — the sharpest anti-Brahminical critique in Kabir's Maaroo set, directly challenging the logic of ritual purity through animal sacrifice.
> "For the sake of Maya and money, you sell knowledge; your life is totally worthless." (Ang 1103)

**Forest asceticism as no solution**:
> "Living in the forest, how will you find Him? Not until you remove corruption from your mind. Those who look alike upon home and forest, are the most perfect people in the world." (Ang ~1103–1104)
> "What is the use of wearing matted hair, smearing the body with ashes, and living in a cave? Conquering the mind, one conquers the world, and then remains detached from corruption." (Ang ~1104)

**Non-dual merger and liberation**:
> "Like drops of water in the water of the ocean, and like waves in the stream, I merge in the Lord. Merging my being into the Absolute Being of God, I have become impartial and transparent, like the air." (Ang ~1104)
> "Coming and going is by the Hukam of His Command; realizing His Hukam, I shall merge in Him." (Ang ~1104) — [[hukam|Hukam]] as the mechanism of both samsara and liberation; accepting Hukam dissolves the cycle rather than opposing it.
> "Giving up the different schools of philosophy, I look upon all equally; I meditate only on the One Name." (Ang ~1104) — renunciation of philosophical doxography in favor of unmediated Naam-practice.

**"Die while yet alive"** (jiwan mukta teaching):
> "Die while yet alive, and by so dying, be alive; thus you shall not be reborn again. Says Kabeer, whoever is absorbed in the Naam remains lovingly absorbed in the Primal, Absolute Lord." (Ang 1104) — the *jiwan-mukta* formulation, nearly identical to its appearances in Raag Sorat'h and Raag Siree Raag, here at its most succinct.

**"Liberation already attained"** — a radical non-dual theology of completed liberation:
> "If You keep me far away from You, then tell me, what is liberation? The One has many forms, and is contained within all; how can I be fooled now?" (Ang ~1104)
> "Tell me where, and what sort of liberation shall You give me? By Your Grace, I have already obtained it." (Ang ~1104) — Kabir's most startling claim in the Granth: liberation is not a future state but a present fact, already received; the conventional question "how do I get saved?" presupposes a separation that does not exist.
> "I have now become pure within my heart, says Kabeer, and my mind is pleased and appeased." (Ang ~1104–1105)

**The body-village allegory**:
> "The body is a village, and the soul is the owner and farmer; the five farm-hands live there. The eyes, nose, ears, tongue and sensory organs of touch do not obey any order." (Ang ~1105)
> "The accountants summoned Chitar and Gupat, the recording scribes of the conscious and the unconscious, to ask for an account of each and every moment." (Ang ~1105) — Chitar (*Chitragupta*) and Gupat as the divine accountants of all human actions; the moment of death as the settling of accounts.
> "Says Kabeer, listen, O Saints: settle your accounts in this farm. O Lord, please forgive Your slave now, in this life, so that he may not have to return again to this terrifying world-ocean." (Ang ~1105)

**Death-urgency compositions**:
> "You fill your belly, and sleep like an animal; you have wasted and lost this human life." (Ang ~1105)
> "Do not take pride in your wealth and youth; you shall crumble apart like dry paper." (Ang ~1105)
> "When the Messenger of Death comes and grabs you by the hair, and knocks you down, on that day, you shall be powerless." (Ang ~1105)
> "Says Kabeer, listen, O Saints: in the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy, you shall be saved." (Ang ~1105)

**The Shalok of Kabir** (Ang ~1105) — among the most celebrated verses in the entire Granth:
> "The battle-drum beats in the sky of the mind; aim is taken, and the wound is inflicted. The spiritual warriors enter the field of battle; now is the time to fight! He alone is known as a spiritual hero, who fights in defense of religion. He may be cut apart, piece by piece, but he never leaves the field of battle." (*gagan damaamaa baaji-o... purjaa purjaa kat marai kabhoo na chhaadai khayt*, Ang ~1105) — the battle-drum shalok; the battlefield is the mind-sky, the fight is for *deen* (faith), and the true warrior's valor is perseverance-unto-death. This is the Granth's most martial spiritual image; its inclusion as a Shalok outside the main composition structure gives it special doctrinal weight.

#### Namdev in Raag Maaroo (Ang 1105)

Namdev's Maaroo composition develops a Vaishnava bhakti register distinct from the Sant-tradition poems around it:

> "I have obtained the four kinds of liberation, and the four miraculous spiritual powers, in the Sanctuary of God, my Husband Lord. I am liberated, and famous throughout the four ages; the canopy of praise and fame waves over my head." (Ang 1105)
> "Meditating on the Sovereign Lord God, who has not been saved? Whoever follows the Guru's Teachings and joins the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy, is called the most devoted of the devotees." (Ang 1105) — the Saadh Sangat-centrism imported into the Vaishnava frame.

Namdev recites a catalogue of divine rescues of devotees: Ambreek (granted fearless dignity), Bhabhikhan (made king), Sudama (given nine treasures), Dhruva (made immovable as the North Star), Prahlad (rescued by Narasimha). The closing verse:
> "Says Naam Dayv, the beautiful-haired Lord is in the power of His devotees; He is standing at Balraja's door, even now!" (Ang 1105) — the devotee's power to hold the Lord (*kaysav bas*) is a Vaishnava bhakti formulation making God subject to the devotee's love.

#### Jayadeva in Raag Maaroo (Ang 1106)

[[jayadeva|Jayadeva]] (Jai Dayv, c. 1150–1200 CE, Bengali Sanskrit poet, author of the *Gīta Govinda*) has a single composition in Maaroo — the most technically yoga-inflected composition in the Bhagat Bāṇī. Unlike his Goojaree composition, this one employs explicit pranayama and Nath-yogic terminology:

> "The breath is drawn in through the left nostril; it is held in the central channel of the Sukhmanaa, and exhaled through the right nostril, repeating the Lord's Name sixteen times." (Ang 1106) — a description of *nadi shodhana* pranayama (Ida/Pingala/Sushumna) with sixteen-count repetition of the Naam.

> "I am powerless; my power has been broken. My unstable mind has been stabilized, and my unadorned soul has been adorned. I drink in the Ambrosial Nectar." (Ang 1106)

The theological resolution is merger-theology stated with the brevity characteristic of Jayadeva's SGGS compositions:
> "I worship the One who is worthy of being worshipped. I trust the One who is worthy of being trusted. Like water merging in water, I merge in the Lord." (Ang 1106)
> "Says Jai Dayv, I meditate and contemplate the Luminous, Triumphant Lord. I am lovingly absorbed in the Nirvaanaa of God." (*barahm nirbaan liv leen paa-i-aa*, Ang 1106) — *nirvān* appears here in a Vaishnava-Sikh syncretic formulation: the Buddhist liberation term is applied to absorption in the Brahman/Lord, consistent with the Granth's cross-tradition theological vocabulary.

Jayadeva's presence here — the only Sanskrit-register Bhagat, deploying yogic breathing and Buddhist liberation terminology in service of theistic devotion — exemplifies the Granth's claim to trans-traditional inclusivity at the level of practice, not merely doctrine.

#### Ravidas in Raag Maaroo (Ang 1106)

[[ravidas|Ravidas]]'s Maaroo compositions include the most explicit naming of the Bhagat community as a collective and the most concentrated statement of the Naam-as-total-sufficiency teaching:

**First composition** — the divine patron of the lowest:
> "O Love, who else but You could do such a thing? O Patron of the poor, Lord of the World, You have put the canopy of Your Grace over my head." (Ang 1106)
> "Only You can grant Mercy to that person whose touch pollutes the world. You exalt and elevate the lowly, O my Lord of the Universe; You are not afraid of anyone." (Ang 1106) — the *chhot* (ritual pollution of touch) acknowledged as a social reality; God's capacity to override it is asserted as divine prerogative against all human hierarchies.

The closing verse of the first Ravidas composition provides the most direct cross-Bhagat solidarity statement in the Granth:
> "Naam Dayv, Kabeer, Trilochan, Sadhana and Sain crossed over. Says Ravi Daas, listen, O Saints, through the Dear Lord, all is accomplished." (Ang 1106) — a list of five Bhagats (Namdev, Kabir, Trilochan, Sadhana, Sain) presented as having already attained liberation; Ravidas aligns himself with this community and endorses the Naam as the path all of them took.

**Second composition** — Naam as the complete substitute for all canonical texts and miraculous powers:
> "The many epics, the Puraanas and the Vedas are all composed out of the letters of the alphabet. After careful thought, Vyaasa spoke the supreme truth, that there is nothing equal to the Lord's Name." (Ang 1106) — Vyasa (compiler of the Vedas/Mahabharata) is claimed as an authority for the *supremacy of Naam over scripture itself*.
> "In intuitive Samaadhi, their troubles are eliminated; the very fortunate ones lovingly focus on the Lord. Says Ravi Daas, the Lord's slave remains detached from the world; the fear of birth and death runs away from his mind." (Ang 1106)

### Raag Maaroo — Theological Summary

Raag Maaroo is the Granth's fullest deployment of the desert/death register. Its theological contributions to the SGGS are:

1. **Death-consciousness as the universal equalizer**: The Mehl 1 Ashtapadees and the Kabir Bhagat Bāṇī together constitute the Granth's most sustained treatment of human mortality as a spiritual argument — not morbidly, but as the clearest reason for prioritizing Naam over all earthly accumulation.

2. **Inner mystical geography** at its most systematic: The Dakhanee Maaroo provides the most detailed mapping of the body-village, nine-gates, and Tenth Gate in the entire Granth — a technical Nath-yogic framework fully integrated into Guru Nanak's theological idiom.

3. **Kabir's Maaroo** is the intellectual apex of the Bhagat Bāṇī: the "liberation already attained" compositions, the "die while yet alive" teaching, and the battle-drum shalok represent Kabir's most distinctive theological voice. His social critique (Pandits, forest asceticism, animal sacrifice) is sharper here than in any other raag.

4. **The Bhagat community named**: Ravidas's closing verse naming five Bhagats as having "crossed over" through the Naam provides the Granth's clearest internal statement of Bhagat solidarity and mutual validation — a community of liberation that crosses caste (cobbler, weaver, calico-printer, barber) and tradition (Sant, Vaishnava, Sanskrit-composing Brahmin).

5. **Jayadeva's yogic-Nirvana synthesis**: His single Maaroo composition is the most technically yoga-inflected text in the Bhagat Bāṇī and the most explicit use of Buddhist liberation terminology in a theistic-devotional context, demonstrating the Granth's capacity to hold apparently disparate systems in a single voice.

6. **Hukam and liberation as two registers of the same reality**: The Maaroo compositions collectively establish that [[hukam|Hukam]] (divine command/order) governs both samsara (coming and going in reincarnation) and liberation (merging in the Lord) — there is no escape *from* Hukam, only the realization that Hukam itself is the path back to the Lord.


---

## Raag Tukhaari (Angs 1107–1117)

Raag Tukhaari spans eleven pages and is the smallest rāg section in Part 4 of this range,
but one of the most poetically concentrated anywhere in the Granth. Two authors appear:
[[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]] (Mehl 1) and [[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] (Mehl 4). The dominant
composition types are the **Chhant** (lyric poem) and what functions structurally as a **Pehray**
(Watches of the Night). No Bhagat bāṇī and no Var appear in Tukhaari. The rāg's distinctive
character is viraha poetry elevated to its most elaborate seasonal structure — the **Baarah Maahaa**
(Twelve Months) — and a secondary set of compositions on impermanence, vigilance, and the
Gurmukh/Manmukh divide.

### Baarah Maahaa — The Twelve Months (Mehl 1, Angs 1107–1110)

The **Baarah Maahaa** (*baarah maahaa*, "twelve months") is the most celebrated composition in
Raag Tukhaari and among the most beloved poems in the entire [[guru-granth-sahib|Guru Granth
Sahib]]. It is a **Chhant** (lyric) by [[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]] (Mehl 1) structured in 17
stanzas: four stanzas of prologue establishing the viraha frame, one stanza for each of the twelve
months of the Indian calendar, and a concluding stanza of union. The composition moves through the
soul-bride (*saaN dhan*, "the noble woman/soul") yearning for her Husband-Lord across the full wheel
of the year, using each month's natural imagery to intensify or modulate the longing, before
arriving in the final month at bridal union within the home of the heart.

**Prologue stanzas (1–4)**: The opening frame establishes the central paradox: the soul acts
according to the karma of past deeds, yet without the Lord she cannot survive even an instant.
The rainbird (*babeehaa*) and the song-bird (*kokil*) cry out for the Beloved. The Tenth Gate
(**nav ghar thaap mahal ghar oocha-o** — "Establishing the nine houses, and the Royal Mansion of
the Tenth Gate above them, the Lord dwells in that home deep within the self," Ang 1107) appears
as the goal of inner search. The Gurmukh drinks the Ambrosial Nectar; the separated soul-bride
has no friend. Rain falls as Gurbani: "O Nanak, the Ambrosial Nectar of Gurbani rains down; the
Lord, in His Grace, has come into the home of my heart." (Ang 1108, stanza 4)

**Month-by-month register** (viraha → longing → guidance → union):

| Stanza | Month (Punjabi / Sanskrit) | Season | Natural imagery | Spiritual register |
|--------|---------------------------|--------|-----------------|--------------------|
| 5 | **Chayt** (Chaitra, March–April) | Spring | Bumble bees hum, forests bloom, mango tree, song-bird | Intense viraha: "If her Husband Lord does not return home, how can the soul-bride find peace? Her body is wasting away with the sorrow of separation." — "O Nanak, in Chayt, peace is easily obtained, if the soul-bride obtains the Lord as her Husband, within the home of her own heart." (Ang 1108) |
| 6 | **Vaisaakh** (Baisakhi, April–May) | Early summer | Branches blossom with new leaves | Yearning at the door: "Please come home, O my Beloved; carry me across the treacherous world-ocean. Without You, I am not worth even a shell." — "O Nanak, finding God in Baisakhi, the consciousness is filled with the Word of the Shabad, and the mind comes to believe." (Ang 1108) |
| 7 | **Jeth** / Jayth (Jyeshtha, May–June) | Hot season | Earth burns like a furnace | Fervent prayer: "The Unattached Lord dwells in His true mansion. If He allows me, then I will come to Him." — "O Nanak, in Jayt'h, she who knows her Lord becomes just like Him; grasping virtue, she meets with the Merciful Lord." (Ang 1108) |
| 8 | **Aasaarh** (Ashadha, June–July) | Peak heat | Sun blazes, earth parched and roasted, crickets in the forest | Complete surrender: "She ties up her bundle of faults and demerits, and suffers in the world hereafter. But dwelling on the True Lord, she finds peace." — "O Nanak, I have given this mind to Him; death and life rest with God." (Ang 1108) |
| 9 | **Saawan** (Shravan, July–August) | Rainy season | Clouds burst into showers, lightning flashes | Sharpest viraha: "My Beloved has not come home, and I am dying of the sorrow of separation. The lightning flashes, and I am scared. My bed is lonely, and I am suffering in agony." — "O Nanak, she alone is a happy soul-bride, who merges in the Being of her Beloved Husband Lord." (Ang 1108–1109) |
| 10 | **Bhaadron** (Bhadrapada, Aug–Sept) | Late monsoon | Dark night of rain, frogs and peacocks call, snakes slither and bite, ponds overflow, mosquitoes bite | Confusion and seeking: "In Bhaadon, the young woman is confused by doubt; later, she regrets and repents." — "O Nanak, I will go and ask my Guru; wherever God is, there I will go." (Ang 1109) |
| 11 | **Asun** / Assu (Ashvina, Sept–Oct) | Autumn transition | Branches green in all ten directions; "that which ripens slowly is sweet" | Grief turning to advocacy: "In Assu, come, my Beloved; the soul-bride is grieving to death." — "O Nanak, in Assu, please meet me, my Beloved. The True Guru has become my Advocate and Friend." (Ang 1109) |
| 12 | **Katak** (Kartika, Oct–Nov) | Cool season | Lamp of intuition burns | Transition: "The lamp of intuition burns, lit by the essence of reality. Love is the oil in the lamp, which unites the soul-bride with her Lord." — "Nanak: please open the shutters of Your Door, O Lord, and meet me. A single moment is like six months to me." (Ang 1109) |
| 13 | **Manghar** (Margashirsha, Nov–Dec) | Winter | Songs and music of poets | Virtuous devotion: "The virtuous wife utters His Glorious Praises; my Beloved Husband Lord is Eternal and Unchanging. The Primal Lord is Unmoving and Unchanging, Clever and Wise; all the world is fickle." — "I have heard the songs and the music, and the poems of the poets; but only the Name of the Lord takes away my pain." (Ang 1109) |
| 14 | **Pokh** (Paush, Dec–Jan) | Deep winter | Snow falls, sap of trees and fields dries up | All-pervading light: "He is permeating and pervading my mind and body; He is the Life of the World. His Light fills all those born of eggs, born from the womb, born of sweat and born of the earth, each and every heart." — "Grant me the Blessed Vision of Your Darshan, O Lord of Mercy and Compassion." (Ang 1109) |
| 15 | **Maagh** (Magha, Jan–Feb) | Mid-winter | Inner sacred pool replaces external tirthas | Purification and arrival: "In Maagh, I become pure; I know that the sacred shrine of pilgrimage is within me. I have met my Friend with intuitive ease; I grasp His Glorious Virtues, and merge in His Being." — "O Nanak, in Maagh, the most sublime essence is meditation on the Lord; this is the cleansing bath of the sixty-eight sacred shrines of pilgrimage." (Ang 1110) |
| 16 | **Phalgun** (Phalguna, Feb–March) | Spring returns | Adorned bride in garlands, pearl necklaces, scented oils, silk robes | Union complete: "In Phalgun, her mind is enraptured, pleased by the Love of her Beloved. Night and day, she is enraptured, and her selfishness is gone." — "O Nanak, the Guru has united me with Him. The soul-bride has found her Husband Lord, within the home of her own heart." (Ang 1110) |

**Concluding stanza (17)**: All twelve months, seasons, weeks, days, hours, minutes, and seconds
are sublime when the True Lord meets her with natural ease: "God, my Beloved, has met me, and my
affairs are all resolved. The Creator Lord knows all ways and means... The bed of my heart becomes
beautiful, when my Husband Lord ravishes me. As Gurmukh, the destiny on my forehead has been
awakened and activated... O Nanak, day and night, my Beloved enjoys me; with the Lord as my
Husband, my Marriage is Eternal." (Ang 1110, stanza 17)

**Theological arc**: The Baarah Maahaa is the most complete expression of **viraha bhakti** in the
Granth structured as a temporal sequence. The poem moves from prologue longing → spring beauty
intensifying absence → summer burning desire → monsoon terror and confusion → autumn's tentative
guidance by the Guru → winter's inner purification → spring's full union. The arc is not merely
emotional but soteriological: the Guru's intervention (stanzas 10–11) is the pivot that converts
confused longing (*bharam*, doubt) into directed seeking, which in turn enables the inner bath
(stanza 15) and the bridal union (stanza 16). The natural world neither allegorizes the spiritual
journey nor is subordinated to it; natural imagery and spiritual condition interpenetrate throughout.
The [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh]] is the soul-bride who has found the Tenth Gate within; the
[[nam-simaran|Naam/Simran]] is the nectar that ends separation.

### Additional Tukhaari Mehl 1 Compositions (Angs 1110–1116)

Following the Baarah Maahaa, [[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]] contributes several Chhant compositions
that elaborate the same viraha-to-union arc through different structural frames:

**Pehray (Watches of the Night) — Ang 1110–1111**: A four-watch composition addressing the
unconscious soul. Each watch brings a different form of spiritual danger: (1) the first watch — "the
dark night; what will become of your honor? The thieves will break into your home and rob you. O
Nanak, the fool never remembers Him; what can he see in the dark of night?"; (2) the second watch —
"Wake up, you unconscious being! Protect your crops, and love the Lord, the Guru. Stay awake and
aware, and the thieves shall not rob you"; (3) the third watch — "sleep has set in. The mortal
suffers in pain, from attachment to Maya, children and spouse... Without the Naam, he suffers";
(4) the fourth watch — the day dawns for those who remained awake; "Those who practice the Word of
the Guru's Shabad are not reincarnated again; the Lord God is their Best Friend." Death is the
fifth stanza's reckoning: "The knot has been untied; rise up — the order has come!" (Ang 1110–1111)
The composition concludes: "O Nanak, the angelic beings are united with the Word of the Shabad;
this is the way God made it." (Ang 1111)

**Chhant on awakening, Ang 1111**: "The Gurmukhs remain awake and aware; their egotistical pride
is eradicated. Night and day, it is dawn for them; they merge in the True Lord." The lamp metaphor:
"The Divine Light is revealed, and in that Light, they achieve realization; the self-willed
manmukhs wander in doubt and confusion." (Ang 1111) Ends with non-dual resolution: "The One Lord
is permeating everywhere; there is no other. The mind comes to believe, from the mind." (Ang 1111)

**Chhant on reincarnation's end, Ang 1111**: "My coming and going in reincarnation have ended;
doubt and hesitation are gone. Conquering my ego, I have met the True Lord, and now I wear the
robe of Truth... My light merges into the Light; I realize and understand my own self." (Ang 1111)

**Chhant on the deluded soul-bride, Ang 1111**: "Deluded by doubt, misled and confused, the
soul-bride later regrets and repents. Abandoning her Husband Lord, she sleeps... Sexual desire,
anger and egotism destroy her." Contrasted with reunion: "The Guru has united me in His Union;
my dependence on people has ended." (Ang 1111–1112)

**Chhant "O my Dear Beloved", Ang 1112**: The Guru revealed as the Invisible Lord; the soul as
"slave of Your slaves." The diamond/diamond imagery: "O Nanak, the diamond of the mind is pierced
through by the diamond of spiritual wisdom. The garland of virtue is strung." — "My light merged
into the Light, when I met my most Darling Beloved." (Ang 1112)

**Chhant "O my ignorant mind", Ang 1113–1114**: Address to the negligent mind: "Without a ladder,
how will you climb up to the Temple of the Lord? Without a boat, you shall not reach the other
shore. Only your awareness of the Guru's Shabad will carry you across. Join the Saadh Sangat,
the Company of the Holy, and you shall enjoy ecstasy; you shall not regret or repent later on."
(Ang 1113–1114)

### Tukhaari Mehl 4 (Angs 1114–1117)

[[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] (Mehl 4) contributes a Chhant sequence of three compositions in
Raag Tukhaari, the most distinctive of which is a historical-devotional narrative of the True Guru's
pilgrimage to Kurukshetra (*kulkhayt*):

**Viraha Chhant, Ang 1114**: "My inner being is filled with love for my Beloved Husband Lord. How
can I live without Him? As long as I do not have the Blessed Vision of His Darshan, how can I
drink in the Ambrosial Nectar?... Night and day, I cry out, 'Pri-o! Pri-o! Beloved! Beloved!',
day and night. Without my Husband Lord, my thirst is not quenched." After the Guru's grace: "Through
the Word of the Guru's Shabad, I have met my Beloved; I am a sacrifice to the True Guru." (Ang 1114)
The viraha is sharply personal; the Darshan of the Guru equals the Darshan of [[akal-purakh|Akal
Purakh]].

**Kurukshetra Pilgrimage Chhant, Ang 1116**: A striking historical composition in which Mehl 4
narrates his own pilgrimage to Kurukshetra during a festival (the *abheej* / Abhaijit astrological
festival): "To receive the Blessed Vision of the Darshan of the Guru, the True Guru, is to truly
bathe at the Abhaijit festival. The filth of evil-mindedness is washed off, and the darkness of
ignorance is dispelled. Blessed by the Guru's Darshan, spiritual ignorance is dispelled, and the
Divine Light illuminates the inner being... The Creator Lord God Himself created the festival, when
the True Guru went to bathe at the festival in Kuruk-shaytra." (Ang 1116) The composition narrates
the Sikhs traveling with the Guru, people arriving from the three worlds to receive Darshan: "Whoever
was blessed with the Darshan of the Guru, the True Guru, the Lord united with Himself." (Ang 1116)
This composition functions as an internal testimonial to the equivalence of Guru-Darshan and
pilgrimage, a position consistent with Sikh teaching on the Guru as living sacred place.

**Final Tukhaari Mehl 4 Chhant, Ang 1116–1117**: Bhakti devotion and divine mercy: "Without the
Fear of God, His Love is not obtained. Without the Fear of God, no one is carried across to the
other side... The treasures of devotional worship to You are countless; he alone is blessed with
them, O my Lord and Master, whom You bless." (Ang 1117)

### Raag Tukhaari — Theological Summary

Raag Tukhaari's dominant theological contribution is the **Baarah Maahaa's soteriological
seasonality**: natural time (the Indian calendar) is pressed into service as a map of the soul's
journey from separation to union. The twelve months are not allegorized into abstract stages but
remain concrete and sensory — the bumble bee in Chayt, the lightning in Saawan, the snow in Pokh
— while simultaneously marking the soul's shifting proximity to the divine. The Guru's mediation is
the pivot of the arc (stanzas 10–11); the inner pilgrimage replaces the external shrine (stanza 15);
the bridal union replaces all forms of external adornment (stanza 16). Key concepts: [[hukam|Hukam]]
(past karma and divine will frame the opening prologue), [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh/Manmukh]]
(the awakened vs. sleeping soul throughout the Pehray compositions), [[nam-simaran|Naam/Simran]]
(the Ambrosial Nectar that quenches separation), and [[akal-purakh|Akal Purakh]] as the Eternal
Husband whose union is the goal of the temporal arc.

---

## Raag Kaydaaraa (Angs 1118–1124)

Raag Kaydaaraa is a compact section of seven pages. The authors are [[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]]
(Mehl 4), [[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] (Mehl 5), [[kabir|Kabir]], and [[ravidas|Ravidas]]. No Mehl
1, 3, or 9 appears; there is no Var. Kaydaaraa is devoted almost entirely to Naam-longing, the
five thieves, and the Satsang as healing remedy; the Bhagat bāṇī (Angs 1123–1124) pivots
sharply to Kabir's merchant/mystical wine imagery and Ravidas's radical low-caste redemption
argument.

### Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4) — Kaydaaraa (Angs 1118–1119)

Two compositions by Mehl 4 open the rāg, each emphasizing the inaccessibility of the Lord without
grace, and the child-father relationship as the primary emotional register:

"O my mind, sing continually the Name of the Lord. The Inaccessible, Unfathomable Lord cannot be
seen; meeting with the Perfect Guru, He is seen." (Ang 1118) — "That person, upon whom my Lord
and Master showers His Mercy — the Lord attunes that one to Himself... Everyone worships the Lord,
but only that person who is pleasing to the Lord is accepted." (Ang 1118)

The child metaphor is Mehl 4's characteristic mode: "I am a child, and You, O my Lord God, are
my Father; please bless me with such understanding, that I may find the Lord. Like the cow, which
is happy upon seeing her calf, O Lord, please hug Nanak close in Your Embrace." (Ang 1118)

The second composition reframes the Guru's feet as the path: "Wash the Feet of the True Guru,
and worship them. In this way, you shall find my Lord God... Stay away from sexual desire, anger,
greed, attachment, egotism and corrupt pleasures... Join the Sat Sangat, the True Congregation,
and speak with the Holy People about the Lord. The Love of the Lord is the healing remedy; the
Name of the Lord is the healing remedy. Chant the Name of the Lord, Raam, Raam." (Ang 1118–1119)
— "Please be kind to servant Nanak, O Lord, my Lord and Master; please make him the dust of the
Feet of the Saints." (Ang 1119)

### Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Kaydaaraa (Angs 1119–1123)

Mehl 5 is the dominant presence in Kaydaaraa numerically, with compositions in multiple *ghar*
(houses) and several thematic registers:

**Darshan-longing and Satsang, Ang 1119**: "O mother, I have awakened in the Society of the
Saints. Seeing the Love of my Beloved, I chant His Name, the greatest treasure." The Guru's grace
as the pivot: "Now, I have found my Peace-giving Guru with ease; seeing His Darshan, my mind
clings to Him... O Nanak, the speech of my Beloved is so sweet!" (Ang 1119)

**The five thieves and three dispositions, Ang 1119**: "Please listen to the prayers of the
humble, O Merciful Lord. The five thieves and the three dispositions torment my mind... I make all
sorts of efforts and go on pilgrimages; I perform the six rituals, and meditate in the right way.
I am so tired of making all these efforts, but the horrible demons still do not leave me." (Ang 1119)
Only sanctuary in the Lord resolves them: "I have been rescued from the ocean of doubt, holding
tight to the feet and the robes of the Saints." (Ang 1119)

**Sanctuary and Naam, Ang 1119**: "I have come to Your Sanctuary, O Lord, O Supreme Treasure.
Love for the Naam, the Name of the Lord, is enshrined within my mind; I beg for the gift of Your
Name." (Ang 1119)

**Yearning for the Darshan, Ang 1120+**: "My mind yearns for the Blessed Vision of the Lord's
Darshan. Please grant Your Grace, and unite me with the Society of the Saints; please bless me
with Your Name." (Ang 1120) — "I serve my True Beloved Lord. Wherever I hear His Praise, there my
mind is in ecstasy." (Ang 1120)

Mehl 5's Kaydaaraa compositions are generally shorter and more concentrated than in rāgs like
Aasaa or Gauree; the emotional tone is consistently one of humble petition and Darshan-thirst.

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Kaydaaraa — Kabir (Angs 1123–1124)

[[kabir|Kabir]]'s six compositions in Raag Kaydaaraa are among his most philosophically dense
appearances in the Granth. The header reads: "Raag Kaydaaraa, The Word Of Kabeer Jee" (Ang 1123).

**Composition 1 — Iron and gold / the fourth state** (Ang 1123): "Those who ignore both praise
and slander, who reject egotistical pride and conceit, who look alike upon iron and gold — they
are the very image of the Lord God." The true devotee is almost impossibly rare: "Hardly anyone
is a humble servant of Yours, O Lord. Ignoring sexual desire, anger, greed and attachment, such
a person becomes aware of the Lord's Feet." The three gunas subordinated: "Raajas, the quality
of energy and activity; Taamas, the quality of darkness and inertia; and Satvas, the quality
of purity and light, are all called the creations of Maya, Your illusion. That man who realizes
the fourth state — he alone obtains the supreme state." Pilgrimage reframed: "Amidst pilgrimages,
fasting, rituals, purification and self-discipline, he remains always without thought of reward.
Thirst and desire for Maya and doubt depart, remembering the Lord, the Supreme Soul." — "The
Fearless Lord is All-pervading. Doubt has run away, says Kabeer, the Lord's humble slave." (Ang 1123)

**Composition 2 — Naam as merchandise** (Ang 1123): The extended merchant metaphor: "Some deal in
bronze and copper, some in cloves and betel nuts. The Saints deal in the Naam, the Name of the
Lord of the Universe. Such is my merchandise as well." — "The priceless diamond has come into
my hands. I have left the world behind." — "My mind is the bull, and meditation is the road;
I have filled my packs with spiritual wisdom, and loaded them on the bull. Says Kabeer, listen,
O Saints: my merchandise has reached its destination!" (Ang 1123)

**Composition 3 — Mystical wine / the Tenth Gate** (Ang 1123–1124): "You barbaric brute, with
your primitive intellect — reverse your breath and turn it inward. Let your mind be intoxicated
with the stream of Ambrosial Nectar which trickles down from the furnace of the Tenth Gate." The
Sufi wine imagery meets Nath body-mysticism: "In the Fear of God, is the Love of God. Only those
few who understand His Love obtain the sublime essence of the Lord." — "There are nine gates to
the one city of the body; restrain your mind from escaping through them. When the knot of the
three qualities is untied, then the Tenth Gate opens up, and the mind is intoxicated, O Siblings
of Destiny." — "When the mortal fully realizes the state of fearless dignity, then his sufferings
vanish; so says Kabeer after careful deliberation. Turning away from the world, I have obtained
this wine, and I am intoxicated with it." (Ang 1123–1124)

**Compositions 4–5 — Death and spiritual blindness** (Ang 1124): Stark physical imagery of the
unconverted ego: "Why do you walk in that crooked, zigzag way? You are nothing more than a
bundle of bones, wrapped in skin, filled with manure; you give off such a rotten smell!" —
"You do not meditate on the Lord. What doubts have confused and deluded you? Death is not far
away from you!" — "You live in a house of sand, but you still puff up your body — you ignorant
fool! Says Kabeer, those who do not remember the Lord may be very clever, but they still drown."
(Ang 1124)

**Composition 6 — Pride and impermanence** (Ang 1124): "Your turban is crooked, and you walk
crooked; and now you have started chewing betel leaves. You have no use at all for loving
devotional worship... In your egotistical pride, you have forgotten the Lord. Gazing upon your
gold, and your very beautiful wife, you believe that they are permanent." — "Says Kabeer, at the
very last moment, death will come and seize you, you fool!" (Ang 1124)

Additional shorter compositions on impermanence and the soul's solitary departure: "The mortal
beats the drum for a few days, and then he must depart... The soul must go home all alone." —
"Says Kabeer, why do you not meditate on the Lord? Your life is uselessly slipping away!" (Ang 1124)

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Kaydaaraa — Ravidas (Ang 1124)

One composition by [[ravidas|Ravidas]], introduced as "Raag Kaydaaraa, The Word Of Ravi Daas Jee."
Its theological core is the radical inversion of caste hierarchy through the argument from sacred
precedent: "One who performs the six religious rituals and comes from a good family, but who does
not have devotion to the Lord in his heart, one who does not appreciate talk of the Lord's Lotus
Feet, is just like an outcaste, a pariah." (Ang 1124) The refrain invokes Valmiki (*Baalmeek*) as
the paradigmatic low-caste figure who transcended social origin through bhakti: "Be conscious, be
conscious, be conscious, O my unconscious mind. Why do you not look at Baalmeek? From such a low
social status, what a high status he obtained! Devotional worship to the Lord is sublime!" (Ang 1124)
The argument extends to multiple canonical figures: "The killer of dogs, the lowest of all, was
lovingly embraced by Krishna. See how the poor people praise him! His praise extends throughout
the three worlds. Ajaamal, Pingulaa, Lodhia and the elephant went to the Lord. Even such
evil-minded beings were emancipated. Why should you not also be saved, O Ravi Daas?" (Ang 1124) The
composition exemplifies Ravidas's characteristic move: establishing low-caste figures as the
proof-texts for divine mercy's unconditional reach, and implicitly positioning himself (*Ravi Daas*)
in the same lineage.

### Raag Kaydaaraa — Theological Summary

Raag Kaydaaraa is a concentrated devotional section structured around the paradox of grace: the
Lord is inaccessible without His own mercy, yet the one who receives mercy is transformed completely.
Mehl 4 and Mehl 5 present the soul's petition from a position of helplessness before the five
thieves and the three gunas; the Satsang and Guru's feet are the only remedy. Kabir deepens this
into a cosmic philosophy: the three gunas as Maya's total domain, the fourth state (*chautha pad*)
as the only escape, the Tenth Gate as the site of mystical intoxication. Ravidas closes with the
most direct social-theological argument in the section: caste status is irrelevant before divine
bhakti; the lowest become the highest. Key linkages: [[haumai|Haumai]] (ego) as the root obstruction
in all three voices; [[nam-simaran|Naam/Simran]] as the universal path; the Saadh Sangat as
institutional vehicle.

---

## Raag Bhairao (Angs 1125–1167)

Raag Bhairao is the largest section in this range, spanning 43 pages. All four represented Gurus
appear — [[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]] (Mehl 1), [[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]] (Mehl 3),
[[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] (Mehl 4), [[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] (Mehl 5) — as well as an
extensive Bhagat bāṇī section (Angs 1157–1167) with compositions by [[kabir|Kabir]] (8+
compositions), [[namdev|Namdev]] (1 composition), and [[ravidas|Ravidas]] (1 composition). The
rāg has no Var. Bhairao spans a wide theological range: body-mortality and Shabad-salvation (Mehl 1),
caste-deconstruction and wakefulness (Mehl 3), Satsang-as-purification (Mehl 4), Guru-supremacy
and divine family metaphysics (Mehl 5), and Kabir's most provocative inter-religious compositions
(Bhagat bāṇī).

### Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) — Bhairao Chau-Padās (Angs 1125–1132)

The Bhairao section opens with the full Mool Mantar: "One Universal Creator God. Truth Is The
Name. Creative Being Personified. No Fear. No Hatred. Image Of The Undying. Beyond Birth.
Self-Existent. By Guru's Grace." (Ang 1125) — the only rāg section in this range to open with
the complete Mool Mantar rather than the abbreviated form.

[[guru-nanak|Guru Nanak]]'s Bhairao compositions are dominated by the theme of bodily mortality,
spiritual blindness, and the Shabad as the sole path of salvation:

**On Hukam and divine sovereignty**: "Without You, nothing happens. You create the creatures, and
gazing on them, you know them... Whatever exists, is by Your Will... You Yourself act, and inspire
all to act; only You Yourself know. Says Nanak, You, Lord, see, establish and disestablish."
(Ang 1125)

**On the world-ocean and Naam**: "Without the Word of the Shabad, how can anyone cross over the
terrifying world-ocean? Without the Naam, the Name of the Lord, the world is entangled in the
disease of duality, and is drowned, drowned, and dies." (Ang 1125–1126) — "The Guru is Divine;
the Guru is Inscrutable and Mysterious. Serving the Guru, the three worlds are known and
understood." (Ang 1125)

**On the mind as king and Yogi**: "The mind is the king; the mind is appeased and satisfied
through the mind itself, and desire is stilled in the mind. The mind is the Yogi, the mind wastes
away in separation from the Lord; singing the Glorious Praises of the Lord, the mind is instructed
and reformed." (Ang 1125–1126)

**On body-decay and spiritual blindness** (second composition series, Angs 1126–1132): Repeated
motifs of aging's approach: "His walk becomes weak and clumsy, his feet and hands shake, and
the skin of his body is withered and wrinkled. His eyes are dim, his ears are deaf, and yet, the
self-willed manmukh does not know the Naam." — "O blind man, what have you obtained by coming
into the world? The Lord is not in your heart, and you do not serve the Guru. After wasting your
capital, you shall have to depart." (Ang 1126) — "Your tongue is not imbued with the Love of the
Lord; whatever you say is tasteless and insipid. You indulge in slander of the Saints; becoming
a beast, you shall never be noble." (Ang 1126)

**On the Gurmukh who conquers the mind**: "He remains with the Guru, day and night, and his
tongue savors the savory taste of the Lord's Love. He does not know any other; he realizes the
Word of the Shabad. He knows and realizes the Lord deep within his own being." — "He conquers
his self-conceit, and is imbued with the Infinite Lord. He serves the Guru." (Ang 1126–1127)
— "Deep within each and every heart, and amidst all, the Embodiment of Truth is permeating and
pervading." (Ang 1127)

**On Shaastras and Vedas**: "The Shaastras and the Vedas keep the mortal bound to the three modes
of Maya, and so he performs his deeds blindly." (Ang 1127) — a subordination of Hindu textual
tradition to inner Shabad-realization consistent with Mehl 1's position throughout the Granth.

### Guru Amar Das (Mehl 3) — Bhairao (Angs 1128–1136)

[[guru-amar-das|Guru Amar Das]] opens his Bhairao compositions with the most direct anti-caste
statement in this range of the Granth:

**Caste-deconstruction** (Ang 1128): "No one should be proud of his social class and status. He
alone is a Brahmin, who knows God." — "Do not be proud of your social class and status, you
ignorant fool! So much sin and corruption comes from this pride." — "Everyone says that there
are four castes, four social classes. They all emanate from the drop of God's Seed." — "The entire
universe is made of the same clay. The Potter has shaped it into all sorts of vessels." — "The
five elements join together to make up the form of the human body. Who can say which is less, and
which is more? Says Nanak, this soul is bound by its actions. Without meeting the True Guru, it
is not liberated." (Ang 1128)

**Wakefulness and the five thieves** (Ang 1128): "The Yogis, the householders, the Pandits, the
religious scholars, and the beggars in religious robes — they are all asleep in egotism. They are
asleep, intoxicated with the wine of Maya. Only those who remain awake and aware are not robbed."
— "One who has met the True Guru, remains awake and aware. Such a person overpowers the five
thieves." — "One who knows the One Lord remains awake and aware. He abandons the service of
others, and realizes the essence of reality. Of the four castes, whoever remains awake and aware,
is released from birth and death. Says Nanak, that humble being remains awake and aware, who
applies the ointment of spiritual wisdom to his eyes." (Ang 1128)

**Hukam theology** (Ang 1129): "Unto whom will you complain? The Hukam of the Lord's Command
is pervasive; by the Hukam of His Command, all things happen." — "This Creation was established
by You. In an instant You destroy it, and You create it again without a moment's delay. By His
Grace, He has staged this Play. By the Guru's Merciful Grace, I have obtained the supreme status.
Says Nanak, He alone kills and revives. Understand this well — do not be confused by doubt."
(Ang 1129)

**Bride-Creator imagery** (Ang 1129): "I am the bride; the Creator is my Husband Lord. As He
inspires me, I adorn myself. When it pleases Him, He enjoys me." — "How can anyone praise or
slander anyone else? The One Lord Himself is pervading and permeating all." (Ang 1129)

**The silent sage / self-inquiry** (Ang 1129): "He alone is a silent sage, who subdues his mind's
duality. Subduing his duality, he contemplates God." — "Let each person examine his own mind, O
Siblings of Destiny. Examine your mind, and you shall obtain the nine treasures of the Naam."
(Ang 1129) — "It is the innate nature of the mind to remain forever detached. The Detached,
Dispassionate Lord dwells within all." — "Says Nanak, one who understands this mystery becomes
the embodiment of the Primal, Immaculate, Divine Lord God." (Ang 1129–1130)

**Naam as world-savior** (Ang 1130): "The world is saved through the Name of the Lord. It carries
the mortal across the terrifying world-ocean. By Guru's Grace, dwell upon the Lord's Name. It
shall stand by you forever." — "Granting His Grace, the Lord unites the mortals with the True
Guru. O Nanak, He enshrines the Naam within the heart." (Ang 1130)

### Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4) — Bhairao (Angs 1133–1136)

[[guru-ram-das|Guru Ram Das]] contributes a set of Chaupadas in Bhairao that emphasize the Lord's
attachment of mortals to the Saints, and Satsang as the site of purification:

"The Lord, in His Mercy, attaches mortals to the feet of the Saints. Through the Word of the
Guru's Shabad, vibrate and meditate on the Lord; let your awareness be absorbed in Him." (Ang 1134)
— "To do good deeds is the best rosary." (Ang 1134) — "Joining with the Sat Sangat, the True
Congregation, I shall cross over the terrifying world-ocean." (Ang 1134) — "In the Sat Sangat,
apply the dust of the feet of the holy to your face; this is how to bathe in the sixty-eight
sacred shrines, and the Ganges." (Ang 1135) — "I am a fool; the Lord has shown mercy to me. The
Savior Lord has saved servant Nanak." (Ang 1135)

### Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Bhairao (Angs 1136–1157)

[[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] dominates Raag Bhairao numerically with over 20 pages of compositions.
Mehl 5's Bhairao register is the most varied theologically: Guru-identity declarations, divine
family metaphysics, mercy and forgiveness, and the complete sufficiency of the Guru.

**Guru-supremacy formulations** (Angs 1141–1143): "There is no deity equal to the Guru.
Whoever has good destiny inscribed on his forehead, applies himself to seva — selfless service."
(Ang 1141) — "My True Guru is totally independent. My True Guru is adorned with Truth. My True
Guru is the Giver of all. My True Guru is the Primal Creator Lord, the Architect of Destiny."
(Ang 1141) — "My True Guru is the Sustainer and Cherisher of all. My True Guru kills and
revives. The glorious greatness of my True Guru has become manifest everywhere. My True Guru is
the power of the powerless. My True Guru is my home and court." (Ang 1141–1142)

**Divine family metaphysics** (Ang 1141): "My Father is Eternal, forever alive. My brothers live
forever as well. My friends are permanent and imperishable. My family abides in the home of the
self within." — "I have found peace, and so all are at peace. The Perfect Guru has united me with
my Father." — "My kingdom is eternally stable. My wealth is inexhaustible and permanent." — "The
Father and son have joined together in partnership. Says Nanak, when my Father is pleased, then
the Father and son are joined together in love, and become one." (Ang 1141–1142)

**Mercy and forgiveness** (Ang 1141): "The True Guru, the Primal Being, is free of revenge and
hate; He is God, the Great Giver. I am a sinner; You are my Forgiver. That sinner, who finds no
protection anywhere — if he comes seeking Your Sanctuary, then he becomes immaculate and pure."
(Ang 1141)

**Hukam as total providence** (Ang 1142): "When it pleases Him, even dry wood becomes green. When
it pleases Him, rivers flow across the desert sands. When it pleases Him, all fruits and rewards
are obtained. Grasping hold of the Guru's feet, my anxiety is dispelled." (Ang 1142) — "I am
unworthy and ungrateful, but He has been merciful to me. My mind and body have been cooled and
soothed; the Ambrosial Nectar rains down in my mind." (Ang 1142)

**On the Guru's face and radiance**: Mehl 5 compositions repeatedly use the image of a veil
being removed: "When the veil of illusion is removed, then I come to see You. You are my Lord and
Master; You are the King of all." (Ang 1142)

**On the blind demon (Andhaa Asur)**: A striking Ang 1135-range composition names a "blind demon"
who "knew nothing of the Lord's Court" — a rare use of demonic imagery in Mehl 5's compositions,
consistent with his broader framework of spiritual blindness as the true adversary.

The concluding Mehl 5 composition before the Bhagat bāṇī (Ang 1157) closes: "Nanak, Nanak,
Nanak, has merged into the Image of God." — the three-fold repetition of Nanak's name as the
signature of complete self-dissolution.

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Bhairao — Kabir (Angs 1157–1166)

The Bhagat bāṇī of Bhairao opens: "Bhairao, The Word Of The Devotees, Kabeer Jee, First House."
[[kabir|Kabir]]'s Bhairao section is his longest concentrated block in the Granth after Raag
Gauree. The compositions range from tender devotion to biting social critique, from Sufi address
to paradox-verse (*ulat bāṃsī*).

**Naam as all wealth** (Ang 1157): "The Name of the Lord — this alone is my wealth. I do not tie
it up to hide it, nor do I sell it to make my living. The Name is my crop, and the Name is my
field. The Name is Maya and wealth for me; the Name is my capital. I do not forsake You; I do not
know any other at all. The Name is my family, the Name is my brother. The Name is my companion,
who will help me in the end." — "One whom the Lord keeps detached from Maya — says Kabeer, I am
his slave." (Ang 1157–1158)

**Impermanence and death** (Ang 1158): "Naked we come, and naked we go. No one, not even the
kings and queens, shall remain." — "What good does it do him, if he has elephants tied up at
his doorway? The fortress of Sri Lanka was made out of gold, but what could the foolish Raawan
take with him when he left? Says Kabeer, think of doing some good deeds. In the end, the gambler
shall depart empty-handed." (Ang 1158)

**Cosmic pollution poem** (Ang 1158): A radical extension of the universal-pollution argument:
"Brahma is polluted, and Indra is polluted. The sun is polluted, and the moon is polluted. This
world is polluted with pollution. Only the One Lord is Immaculate; He has no end or limitation."
— "The rulers of kingdoms are polluted. Nights and days, and the days of the month are polluted."
— "The pearl is polluted, the diamond is polluted. Wind, fire and water are polluted." — "Shiva,
Shankara and Mahaysh are polluted. The Siddhas, seekers and strivers, and those who wear
religious robes, are polluted. The Yogis and wandering hermits with their matted hair are polluted.
The body, along with the swan-soul, is polluted. Says Kabeer, those humble beings are approved and
pure, who know the Lord." (Ang 1158) — the most comprehensive deconstruction of purity categories
anywhere in the Granth: Hindu deities, cosmic elements, religious specialists, and the body itself
are all included in the pollution. Only direct knowledge of the Lord confers purity.

**Sufi-Islamic address** (Ang 1158–1159): "Let your mind be Mecca, and your body the temple of
worship. Let the Supreme Guru be the One who speaks. O Mullah, utter the call to prayer. The one
mosque has ten doors." — "So slaughter your evil nature, doubt and cruelty; consume the five
demons and you shall be blessed with contentment. Hindus and Muslims have the same One Lord and
Master. What can the Mullah do, and what can the Shaykh do? Says Kabeer, I have gone insane.
Slaughtering, slaughtering my mind, I have merged into the Celestial Lord." (Ang 1158–1159)

**Transformation chain** (Ang 1159): Three parallel metaphors for spiritual transformation through
holy company: "When the stream flows into the Ganges, then it becomes the Ganges." — "Associating
with the sandalwood tree, the tree nearby is changed; that tree begins to smell just like the
sandalwood tree." — "Coming into contact with the philosophers' stone, copper is transformed; that
copper is transformed into gold." — "In the Society of the Saints, Kabeer is transformed; that
Kabeer is transformed into the Lord." (Ang 1159)

**Devotion vs. external religion** (Ang 1159): "Some apply ceremonial marks to their foreheads,
