hold malas in their hands, and wear religious robes. Some people think that the Lord is a
play-thing... If I am insane, then I am Yours, O Lord. How can people know my secret? I do not
pick leaves as offerings, and I do not worship idols. Without devotional worship of the Lord,
service is useless. I worship the True Guru; forever and ever, I surrender to Him. By such service,
I find peace in the Court of the Lord. People say that Kabeer has gone insane. Only the Lord
realizes the secret of Kabeer." (Ang 1159)

**Sunn Sahaj / Weaver's mysticism** (Ang 1159): "Turning away from the world, I have forgotten
both my social class and ancestry. My weaving now is in the most profound celestial stillness.
I have no quarrel with anyone. I have abandoned both the Pandits, the Hindu religious scholars,
and the Mullahs, the Muslim priests." — "I weave and weave, and wear what I weave. Where egotism
does not exist, there I sing God's Praises." — "Whatever the Pandits and Mullahs have written,
I reject; I do not accept any of it. My heart is pure, and so I have seen the Lord within.
Searching, searching within the self, Kabeer has met the Lord." (Ang 1159)

**On poverty and wealth** (Ang 1159): A social-economic reflection: "No one respects the poor
man. He may make thousands of efforts, but no one pays any attention to him. When the poor man
goes to the rich man and sits right in front of him, the rich man turns his back on him. But when
the rich man goes to the poor man, the poor man welcomes him with respect." — "Says Kabeer, he
alone is poor, who does not have the Naam, the Name of the Lord, in his heart." (Ang 1159–1160)
— a characteristic Kabir inversion: worldly poverty vs. spiritual poverty, with spiritual poverty
(Naam-lessness) the true destitution.

**Precious human life** (Ang 1160): "Serving the Guru, devotional worship is practiced. Then,
this human body is obtained. Even the gods long for this human body. So vibrate that human body,
and think of serving the Lord." — "Vibrate, and meditate on the Lord of the Universe, and never
forget Him. This is the blessed opportunity of this human incarnation." — "As long as the disease
of old age has not come to the body, and as long as death has not come and seized the body, and
as long as your voice has not lost its power, O mortal being, vibrate and meditate on the Lord
of the World. If you do not vibrate and meditate on Him now, when will you, O Sibling of Destiny?
When the end comes, you will not be able to vibrate and meditate on Him. Whatever you have to do
— now is the best time to do it. Otherwise, you shall regret and repent afterwards, and you shall
not be carried across to the other side." (Ang 1160)

**Guru-Grace as fulfillment of all pilgrimage and ritual** (later Kabir compositions, Angs 1160–1166):
A systematic parallel-structure composition in which each ritual or external practice finds its
true fulfillment through the Guru's grace alone: "When the Divine Guru grants His Grace, one has
bathed at the sixty-eight sacred shrines of pilgrimage. When the Divine Guru grants His Grace,
one's body is stamped with the sacred mark of Vishnu. When the Divine Guru grants His Grace, one
has performed the twelve devotional services. When the Divine Guru grants His Grace, all poison
is transformed into fruit." — "When the Divine Guru grants His Grace, skepticism is shattered...
one escapes from the Messenger of Death... one crosses over the terrifying world-ocean... one is
not subject to the cycle of reincarnation... one understands the rituals of the eighteen Puraanas."
Concludes: "Namdev has entered the Sanctuary of the Guru." (Ang 1166–1167) — this final signature
is [[namdev|Namdev]]'s, suggesting the preceding composition is actually attributed to Namdev (see
below); the transition from Kabir to Namdev in this section is structurally abrupt.

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Bhairao — Namdev (Ang 1166–1167)

[[namdev|Namdev]]'s composition in Bhairao takes a highly unusual form: Krishna in the dress of
a Sufi saint (*kalandar*), described in extravagant cosmic-body imagery:

"Come, O Lord of beautiful hair, wearing the robes of a Sufi Saint." — "Your cap is the realm
of the Akaashic ethers; the seven nether worlds are Your sandals. The body covered with skin is
Your temple; You are so beautiful, O Lord of the World." — "The fifty-six million clouds are
Your gowns, the 16,000 milkmaids are your skirts. The eighteen loads of vegetation is Your stick,
and all the world is Your plate." — "The human body is the mosque, and the mind is the priest, who
peacefully leads the prayer. You are married to Maya, O Formless Lord, and so You have taken form."
— "Performing devotional worship services to You, my cymbals were taken away; unto whom should I
complain? Naam Dayv's Lord and Master, the Inner-knower, the Searcher of hearts, wanders
everywhere; He has no specific home." (Ang 1166–1167)

The composition blends Vaishnava (Krishna, *Kalandar* dressing), Islamic (mosque, prayer, Mullah
imagery), and Sant-bhakti registers in a single vision — one of the most inter-religious individual
compositions in the Granth. The complaint about cymbals being confiscated during worship is
consistent with the broader Namdev hagiography (often depicted as a low-caste calico-printer whose
devotion disrupts established religious hierarchies).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Bhairao — Ravidas (Ang 1167)

[[ravidas|Ravidas]]'s single composition in Bhairao is introduced as "Bhairao, The Word Of Ravi
Daas Jee, Second House." Its philosophical register is more abstract than his Siree Raag or Aasaa
compositions:

"Without seeing something, the yearning for it does not arise. Whatever is seen, shall pass away.
Whoever chants and praises the Naam, the Name of the Lord, is the true Yogi, free of desire." —
"When someone utters the Name of the Lord with love, it is as if he has touched the philosopher's
stone; his sense of duality is eradicated." (Ang 1167) — "He alone is a silent sage, who destroys
the duality of his mind. Keeping the doors of his body closed, he merges in the Lord of the three
worlds." — "Plants blossom forth to produce fruit. When the fruit is produced, the flowers wither
away. For the sake of spiritual wisdom, people act and practice rituals. When spiritual wisdom wells
up, then actions are left behind." — "For the sake of ghee, wise people churn milk. Those who are
Jivan-mukta, liberated while yet alive — are forever in the state of Nirvaanaa. Says Ravi Daas, O
you unfortunate people, why not meditate on the Lord with love in your heart?" (Ang 1167)

The composition's structure is striking: a chain of natural-process analogies (seeing→yearning,
fruit→flowers wither, ghee←milk churning) used to demonstrate that spiritual wisdom supersedes
the ritual acts that produce it. The term *Nirvaanaa* (Pali *nibbāna*, Sanskrit *nirvāṇa*) appears
directly, making this one of the explicit uses of the Buddhist liberation term in the Granth —
used here by a bhakta of low-caste origin as a synonym for the state of *jivan mukta* (liberated
while alive), fully integrated into the Sant devotional framework.

### Raag Bhairao — Theological Summary

Raag Bhairao covers the broadest doctrinal range in this Part 4 segment. Mehl 1 grounds the rāg
in [[hukam|Hukam]] as absolute divine sovereignty and the Shabad as the only crossing of the
world-ocean; the body's decay is the clock that presses spiritual urgency. Mehl 3 adds the most
sustained caste-deconstruction: Brahmin status is redefined as knowledge of God; all four castes
originate from the same divine seed; wakefulness (not birth) determines liberation. Mehl 4 positions
Satsang as the purification mechanism; Mehl 5 elaborates the Guru-God identity to its highest
pitch ("My True Guru is the Primal Creator Lord, the Architect of Destiny"; Father-son as one
love). The Bhagat bāṇī is Bhairao's most distinctive feature: Kabir's cosmic-pollution poem
(dissolving all purity hierarchies), Sufi address (mosque-in-the-body; Hindu and Muslim share
one Lord), transformation-chain mysticism, and the paradox of divine madness; Namdev's
syncretistic Krishna-as-Sufi; Ravidas's philosophical statement that spiritual wisdom supersedes
the ritual acts that seek it, with *Nirvaanaa* as the outcome. Key theological linkages across
all voices: [[haumai|Haumai]] as the root of both caste pride and spiritual blindness; the Saadh
Sangat as the alchemical space of transformation; [[nam-simaran|Naam/Simran]] as the practice
that both crosses the world-ocean and dissolves duality; and the [[akal-purakh|Akal Purakh]]'s
sole immaculate status (Kabir's pollution poem) as the foundation of all bhakti.

---

## Raag Basant (Angs 1168–1196)

The spring rāg, *basant* being the Punjabi word for the season of spring. Raag Basant carries an unusually explicit metaphorical program: the natural blooming of spring becomes the vehicle for expressing spiritual awakening, the blossoming of devotion, and the contrast between mere seasonal renewal and the eternal, divine spring available through Naam. The rāg spans 29 Angs and encompasses a wide array of composition types — Chau-Padās (four-stanza hymns), Du-Tukas (couplet forms), Ik-Tukas (single-stanza forms) — as well as a substantial **Raag Basant Hindol** subsection (a combined Basant-Hindol mode, which adds a swinging, upward melodic quality to the spring associations). Authors represented: Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak), Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das), Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das), Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan), Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur), and Bhagat Bāṇī of Namdev and [[ravidas|Ravidas]].

### Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) — Basant Chau-Padās

Mehl 1 opens Raag Basant with the Mool Mantar and immediately establishes the governing theological metaphor: spring (*basant*) is the season in which the Gurmukh's consciousness blossoms. The very first verse (Ang 1168) encodes the rāg's program: "Among the months, blessed is this month, when spring always comes. / Blossom forth, O my consciousness, contemplating the Lord of the Universe, forever and ever." (*maahaa maah mumaarkhee charhi-aa sadaa basant. / parfarh chit samaal so-ay sadaa sadaa gobind. ||1||*, Ang 1168). The antidote offered to the ignorant (*bholi-aa*) who clings to egotistical intellect is direct: "Subdue your ego, and contemplate Him in your mind; gather in the virtues of the Sublime, Virtuous Lord." (Ang 1168).

A structurally significant verse from the same first hymn introduces a botanical allegory for the spiritual path: "Karma is the tree, the Lord's Name the branches, Dharmic faith the flowers, and spiritual wisdom the fruit. / Realization of the Lord are the leaves, and eradication of the pride of the mind is the shade." (*karam payd saakhaa haree Dharam ful fal gi-aan. / pat paraapat chhaav ghanee chookaa man abhimaan.* ||2||, Ang 1168). This tree-of-Naam metaphor is the rāg's structural image, recurring in later Mehl 3 compositions.

Several Mehl 1 Basant compositions offer pointed critiques of ritual religiosity dressed in spring imagery. One hymn catalogues the most elaborate ritual settings — golden kitchen-squares, Ganges water, sacred firewood, Vedas recited from memory, Qazis and Mullahs and Yogis in their respective garbs — and declares all worthless: "O my mind, these things are worthless, / if you are not drenched with the True Name." (*ray man laykhai kabhoo na paa-ay. / jaam na bheejai saach naa-ay. ||1|| rahaa-o.*, Ang 1169). The judgment falls equally on all traditions: the Pauranic scholar, the four-caste householder, the Qazi, the wandering hermit in saffron — "without understanding the essence of devotional worship, all people are eventually bound and gagged, and driven along by the Messenger of Death." (Ang 1169).

The **Basant Hindol** subsection (Mehl 1, First House, Second House) develops agricultural and hydraulic metaphors for spiritual labor. One striking hymn deploys an irrigation allegory: "Why do you irrigate the barren, alkaline soil? You are wasting your life away! / This wall of mud is crumbling. Why bother to patch it with plaster?" — then reverses the image: "Let your hands be the buckets, strung on the chain, and yoke the mind as the ox to pull it; draw the water up from the well. / Irrigate your fields with the Ambrosial Nectar, and you shall be owned by God the Gardener." (*amrit sinchahu bharahu ki-aaray ta-o maalee kay hovhu.* ||2||, Ang 1171). The two passions — sexual desire and anger (*kaam* and *krodh*) — are reinterpreted as shovels for digging the spiritual field: "Let sexual desire and anger be your two shovels, to dig up the dirt of your farm." (Ang 1171). The bride-soul metaphor appears in the *hindol* sub-raag: "O my Lord and Master, I am deluded by doubt. / I sing the Word which You have written; I do not know any other Word." (Ang 1171).

A philosophically condensed Hindol hymn (Ang 1172) uses paradox: "There is fire within the plants which bloom; the ocean is tied into a bundle. / The sun and the moon dwell in the same home in the sky. You have not obtained this knowledge." — and proposes compassion as the sign of one who knows the All-pervading Lord: "Know that the sign of such a person is that he gathers the wealth of compassion." (*taa kay lakhan jaanee-ahi khimaa Dhan sangar-hay-ay.* ||3||, Ang 1172).

Another Hindol hymn (Ang 1172) articulates the Guru's cosmological role: "Without the Guru, no one is liberated anywhere in the three worlds. The Gurmukh obtains the Lord's Name." And a subsequent one adds the philosopher's stone image that recurs across the Granth: "Touching the philosopher's stone, they themselves become the philosopher's stone; they become the companions of the Lord, the Guru." (*paaras bhayt bha-ay say paaras naanak har gur sang thee-ay.* ||4||4||12||, Ang 1172).

### Guru Amar Das (Mehl 3) — Basant Compositions

Mehl 3 contributes a substantial block of Basant Chau-Padās and Du-Tukas. His characteristic moves — emphasis on Shabad, critique of body-mortification, the Gurmukh/Manmukh opposition, and *jeevan mukta* (living liberation) — are all developed under the spring metaphor. A central formulation: "My God is in bloom, forever in spring. / This mind blossoms forth, singing the Glorious Praises of the Lord of the Universe." (*mayraa parabh ma-oli-aa sad basant. / ih man ma-oli-aa gaa-ay gun gobind.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1174) — here the divine itself is described as perpetually in spring, relativizing the seasonal metaphor: nature blooms once a year, but devotional spring is eternal.

Mehl 3 condemns naked asceticism and yogic body-disciplines as inadequate: "A person may take off his clothes and be naked. / What Yoga does he practice by having matted and tangled hair? / If the mind is not pure, what use is it to hold the breath at the Tenth Gate? / The fool wanders and wanders, entering the cycle of reincarnation again and again." (Ang 1169). The correction is simple: "Meditate on the One Lord, O my foolish mind, and you shall cross over to the other side in an instant." (*ayk Dhi-aavahu moorhH manaa. / paar utar jaahi ik khinaaN.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1169).

**Hukam in Basant Mehl 3**: Several hymns articulate the theology of divine Will (*Hukam*): "What can the created being do? / The Lord Himself moves him. / If the Lord casts His Glance of Grace, then his doubts are dispelled. / If the mortal realizes the Hukam of the Lord's Command, he obtains the True Lord." (*keetaa ho-aa karay ki-aa ho-ay. / jis no aap chalaa-ay so-ay. / nadar karay taaN bharam chukaa-ay. / hukmai boojhai taaN saachaa paa-ay.* ||4||, Ang 1169). This is combined with a critique of inner pollution: if "someone's soul is polluted within, what is the use of his traveling to sacred shrines of pilgrimage all over the world?" — and the cure is the Sat Guru: "O Nanak, when one joins the Society of the True Guru, then the bonds of the terrifying world-ocean are broken." (Ang 1169).

The **dual-fruit** metaphor appears in Mehl 3: "The Creator created both poison and nectar. / He attached these two fruits to the world-plant. / The Creator Himself is the Doer, the Cause of all." (*bikh amrit kartaar upaa-ay. / sansaar birakh ka-o du-ay fal laa-ay.*, Ang 1172).

**True spring as devotional state**: One Mehl 3 hymn crystallizes the governing metaphor of the rāg: "Those who are imbued with Truth, are forever in the bloom of spring. / Their minds and bodies are rejuvenated, uttering the Glorious Praises of the Lord of the Universe. / Without the Lord's Name, the world is dry and parched. / It burns in the fire of desire, over and over again." (*saach ratay tin sad basant. / man tan hari-aa rav gun guvind. / bin naavai sookaa sansaar. / agan tarisnaa jalai vaaro vaar.* ||3||, Ang 1174). This verse is paradigmatic: the world without Naam is the inverse of spring — dry, parched, burning with unquenchable desire.

Mehl 3 also introduces **jeevan mukta** language in Basant: "He remains dead while yet alive, and redeems all his ancestors." (*jeevat marai sabh kul uDhrai.*, Ang 1173). And the Gurmukh as living embodiment of spring: "The Gurmukh blossoms forth, with intuitive ease." (*gurmukh bigsai sahj subhaa-ay.*, Ang 1174). The Basant Hindol sub-section (Mehl 3, Second House) includes a devotional compact: "I shall not renounce Your devotional worship, O Lord; what does it matter if people laugh at me? / The True Name abides within my heart." (*tayree bhagat na chhoda-o ki-aa ko hasai. / saach naam mayrai hirdai vasai.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1173).

### Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4) — Basant Hindol

Mehl 4's Basant compositions appear in the Basant Hindol sub-section. They develop the devotional-erotic register characteristic of Mehl 4, here framed by the spring/blossoming motif. The soul-bride's union with the Husband Lord is expressed through Satsang: the Guru unites the separated soul-bride with her Lord, and the Name is the bond of that reunion. A representative Mehl 4 Hindol line: when the Guru is pleased, he "blesses the mortals with loving devotional worship of the Lord" and grants "a place in the Mansion of the Lord's Presence." (*gur tuthai paa-ee-ai har mahal thaa-o.*, Ang 1172). A keynote verse: "The Guru unites in union with the Lord, and grants the gift of the Name. / The Guru loves His Sikhs, day and night." (*gur mayl milaavai karay daat. / gursikh pi-aaray dinas raat.*, Ang 1172).

The Mehl 4 compositions explicitly mention Kabir, Namdev, and Ravidas as exemplars of devotion within the Basant sequence (Ang 1186): "Kabeer meditated on the One Lord with love. / Naam Dayv lived with the Dear Lord. / Ravi Daas meditated on God, the Incomparably Beautiful. / Guru Nanak Dayv is the Embodiment of the Lord of the Universe." (*kabeer Dhi-aa-i-o ayk rang. / naamdayv har jee-o baseh sang. / ravidaas Dhi-aa-ay parabh anoop. / gur naanak dayv govind roop.*, Ang 1186). This chain of Bhagats and the Guru serves as a thematic bridge to the Bhagat Bāṇī section that follows.

### Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Basant Compositions

Mehl 5's Basant compositions continue the urgency around Naam and the dangers of Maya and egotism. A characteristic verse warns against spiritual neglect: "The mortal wanders in reincarnation through countless lifetimes. / Without meditating in remembrance on the Lord, he falls into hell. / Without devotional worship, he is cut apart into pieces. / Without understanding, he is punished by the Messenger of Death." (*anik janam bharamay jon maahi. / har simran bin narak paahi. / bhagat bihoonaa khand khand. / bin boojhay jam dayt dand.* ||1||, Ang 1187). The counsel offered: "Meditate and vibrate forever on the Lord of the Universe, O my friend. / Love forever the True Word of the Shabad." (*gobind bhajahu mayray sadaa meet. / saach sabad kar sadaa pareet.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1187).

Mehl 5 in Basant also makes an extended critique of Maya's delusions: "All the show of Maya is just a cloud of smoke" (*Dhoomm baadar sabh maa-i-aa saaj.*, Ang 1187) — and maps the wheel of reincarnation driven by egotism, desire, and greed: "Acting in egotism and self-conceit, his corruption only increases. / The world is drowning in attachment and greed. / Sexual desire and anger hold the mind in its power. / Even in his dreams, he does not chant the Lord's Name." (*ha-o ha-o karat baDhay bikaar. / moh lobh doobou sansaar. / kaam kroDh man vas kee-aa. / supnai naam na har lee-aa.* ||3||, Ang 1187).

### Raag Basant Hindol — Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur)

A brief but theologically weighty subsection at the end of Basant Hindol. Mehl 9 contributes his characteristic themes of impermanence, the illusoriness of worldly wealth, and the sufficiency of Naam alone. The opening Hindol composition (Ang 1194) articulates the classic Mehl 9 position: "O Holy Saints, know that this body is false. / The Lord who dwells within it — recognize that He alone is real." (*saaDho ih tan mithi-aa jaan-o. / yaa bheetar jo raam basat hai saacho taahi pachhaano.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1194). And: "The wealth of this world is only a dream; why are you so proud of it? / None of it shall go along with you in the end; why do you cling to it?" (*ih jag hai sampat supnay kee daykh kahaa aidaano. / sang tihaarai kachhoo na chaalai taahi kahaa laptaano.* ||1||, Ang 1194). A final Mehl 9 verse: "Leave behind both praise and slander; enshrine the Kirtan of the Lord's Praises within your heart. / O servant Nanak, the One Primal Being, the Lord God, is totally permeating everywhere." (*ustat nindaa do-oo parhar har keerat ur aano. / jan naanak sabh hee mai pooran ayk purakh bhagvaano.* ||2||1||, Ang 1194).

Basant Mehl 9 proper (following the Hindol subsection) carries the same themes: Yogis, wandering ascetics, and renunciates are all snared in the net of *kaam* (lust): "The Yogis, wandering ascetics and renunciates — this net is cast over them all." (*jogee jangam ar sanni-aas. / sabh hee par daaree ih faas.* ||1||, Ang 1194). Liberation comes only through the Name: "Those who contemplate the Name of the Lord cross over the terrifying world-ocean." (Ang 1194).

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Raag Basant

The Bhagat Bāṇī section of Basant includes compositions by **[[namdev|Namdev]]** and **[[ravidas|Ravidas]]**; the section is preceded by the Mehl 4 ashtapadee that explicitly names Kabir, Namdev, and Ravidas as models of devotion.

**Namdev** (Ang 1196): Namdev's Basant composition operates in the viraha register, with the soul-bride (soul-devotee) seeking the Husband Lord. The Gurmukh seeks the Lord within: "The Gurmukh looks within themselves; their minds are filled with faith." (Ang 1196). Namdev's conclusion affirms divine omnipresence and answers to the Lord alone: "Prays Naam Dayv, O Lord, You are All-pervading. / Please be kind to Your devotee." (*bhanat naamday-o ram rahi-aa. / apnay bhagat par kar da-i-aa.* ||3||3||, Ang 1196).

**[[ravidas|Ravidas]]** (Angs 1196–1197): Ravidas's Basant compositions are the most pointed anti-pride verses in the rāg. The opening: "You know nothing. / Seeing your clothes, you are so proud of yourself. / The proud bride shall not find a place with the Lord. / Above your head, the crow of death is cawing." (*tujheh sujhantaa kachhoo naahi. / pahiraavaa daykhay oobh jaahi. / garabvatee kaa naahee thaa-o. / tayree gardan oopar lavai kaa-o.* ||1||, Ang 1196). In a distinctive image, Ravidas compares the proud mortal to a mushroom: "Why are you so proud? You are insane. / Even the mushrooms of summer live longer than you." (*too kaaN-ay garbeh baavlee. / jaisay bhaada-o khoombraaj too tis tay kharee utaavalee.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1196). The deer-musk image: "The deer does not know the secret; / the musk is within its own body, but it searches for it outside." (*jaisay kurank nahee paa-i-o bhayd. / tan suganDh dhoodhai pardays.*, Ang 1196) — a classic Kabir-tradition formulation of the divine as already present within, used here by Ravidas. The corrective: "If you seek the Support of the Holy, / then all your sins shall be erased." (Ang 1196). Ravidas ends at Ang 1197 just as Raag Saarang begins — making Ravidas's final Basant verse the bridge to the new rāg.

### Theological Summary: Raag Basant

Raag Basant's theological program is built on the season-as-metaphor: natural spring is a shadow of the eternal spring of devotion. The rāg's governing claim — stated explicitly by Mehl 3 (Ang 1174) and embedded in Mehl 1's opening verse — is that those imbued with Truth are "forever in the bloom of spring" (*sad basant*), while those without Naam are "dry and parched" and "burning in the fire of desire." The rāg thus uses a sensory register (the lushness, greenness, and fragrance of spring) to ground a soteriological claim about Naam. Five interlocking themes:

1. **Eternal spring through Naam**: Seasonal spring is temporary; the devotional spring of Naam-absorption is permanent. "Those Gurmukhs who remain merged in the Lord do not wither away; they remain green forever." (Mehl 1, Ang 1168).
2. **Critique of external religiosity**: All formal religious observance — Hindu ritual (golden kitchen, Vedic recitation, pilgrimage), Muslim (Qazi, Mullah, fasting), Yogic (nakedness, matted hair, Tenth Gate breath-holding) — is declared worthless without the Naam.
3. **Hukam and Nadar**: Spiritual blossoming is not an individual achievement but the result of the Lord's gracious will (nadar). "If the Lord casts His Glance of Grace, then his doubts are dispelled." (Mehl 3).
4. **Gurmukh/Manmukh as spring/drought**: The rāg maps its central opposition onto the spring metaphor directly — the Gurmukh blossoms; the Manmukh is parched and wandering.
5. **Impermanence (Mehl 9)**: Guru Tegh Bahadur's compositions in Basant Hindol add the contemplative-renunciant register: body and world are false; only the Lord dwelling within is real. This universalizes the anti-attachment teaching that underlies all other themes.

---

## Raag Saarang (Angs 1197–1252)

One of the major rāgs of the SGGS, spanning 56 pages (Angs 1197–1252). The name *Sārang* is associated in Hindustani classical music with the rainy season (*varsha*), which in the Punjabi seasonal imagination is the season of longing, cooling relief, the cuckoo's and peacock's cries of passion, and the soul-bride awaiting the return of her Husband. Where Basant is the season of blooming, Saarang is the season of thirst and its quenching: the soul as the *chaatrik* bird (*paapiha*, the rain-calling cuckoo), yearning for the single precious rain-drop of the Lord's Name. The rāg is extensive in composition type and authorship: **Chau-Padās** (First–Fifth House), **Ashtapadees**, **Chhants**, the **Vaar of Saarang** (Mehl 4 Paurees, with Shaloks by multiple Mehls), and **Bhagat Bāṇī**. Authors: Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak), Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das), Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das), Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan), Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur), and Bhagat Bāṇī of [[kabir|Kabir]] and [[namdev|Namdev]].

### Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) — Saarang Chau-Padās and Ashtapadees

Mehl 1 opens Raag Saarang with the soul-bride register. The first composition (Ang 1197) articulates the rāg's defining posture — the hand-maiden surrendering to the Lord and killing off her ego: "I am the hand-maiden of my Lord and Master. / I have grasped the Feet of God, the Life of the world. He has killed and eradicated my egotism." (*apunay thaakur kee ha-o chayree. / charan gahay jagjeevan parabh kay ha-umai maar nibayree.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1197). The Manmukh's predicament is inverted: "The worthless self-willed manmukh, with false and shallow understanding — his mind and body are held in pain's grip." (Ang 1197). Union comes through abandoning ego: "Abandoning egotism, I have become detached. And now, I absorb true intuitive understanding. / The mind is pleased and appeased by the Pure, Immaculate Lord; the opinions of other people are irrelevant." (Ang 1197).

The second Mehl 1 Chau-Pada (Ang 1197–1198) introduces the rāg's signature thirst-imagery: "How can I survive without the Lord? I am suffering in pain. / My tongue does not taste — all is bland without the Lord's sublime essence. Without God, I suffer and die." (*har bin ki-o rahee-ai dukh bi-aapai. / jihvaa saad na feekee ras bin bin parabh kaal santaapai.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1197). The vision of the Darshan quenches the thirst: "Gazing upon the Blessed Vision of His Darshan, my mind is pleased and appeased. / The lotus blossoms forth in the water." (*darsan daykhat hee man maani-aa jal ras kamal bigaasee.* ||1||, Ang 1197). The rainy-season imagery follows immediately: "The low-hanging clouds crack with thunder and burst. The cuckoos and the peacocks are filled with passion, / along with the birds in the trees, the bulls and the snakes. The soul-bride is happy when her Husband Lord returns home." (*oonav ghanhar garjai barsai kokil mor bairaagai. / tarvar birakh bihang bhu-i-angam ghar pir Dhan sohaagai.* ||2||, Ang 1197). The soul-bride who lacks divine love is described as "ugly, deformed, and of low character" — the inverse of the one "whose love for the Lord is deep within her heart" (Ang 1198).

Mehl 1's **Ashtapadees in Saarang** (beginning Ang 1232) extend the thirst and longing motifs. A notable Shalok in the Var section critiques all external spiritual methods in a sustained series: "He is not won over by music, songs or the Vedas. / He is not won over by intuitive wisdom, meditation or Yoga. / He is not won over by feeling sad and depressed forever. / He is not won over by beauty, wealth and pleasures. / He is not won over by wandering naked at sacred shrines. / He is not won over by giving donations in charity. / He is not won over by living alone in the wilderness. / He is not won over by fighting and dying as a warrior in battle. / He is not won over by becoming the dust of the masses. // The account is written of the loves of the mind. / O Nanak, the Lord is won over only by His Name." (*na bheejai raagee naadee bayd … naanak bheejai saachai naa-ay.* ||2||, Vaar of Saarang Shalok, Mehl 1). This is among the most comprehensive anti-formalist catalogues in the Granth.

A companion Shalok from the same Var section extends the critique to classical learning: "You may study the nine grammars, the six Shaastras and the six divisions of the Vedas. / You may recite the Mahaabhaarata. / Even these cannot find the limits of the Lord. / Without the Naam, the Name of the Lord, how can anyone be liberated?" (Vaar of Saarang, Mehl 1 Shalok, Ang 1237).

### Guru Ram Das (Mehl 4) — Saarang Chau-Padās

Mehl 4 is the most extensively represented author in Raag Saarang's Chau-Pada section, carrying Angs 1199–1216 and including multiple house-sub-varieties (Du-Padas, Partaal forms). The viraha register is dominant. Key formulations:

**Naam-thirst**: Mehl 4's Saarang compositions continue Mehl 1's thirst imagery but with more personal intensity. The devotee as *chaatrik* (rain-bird): "My inner being is filled with a great thirst, like that of the song-bird for water. When shall I find the Fruitful Vision of His Darshan?" (*antar pi-aas chaatrik ji-o jal kee safal darsan kad paaN-o.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1213 — Mehl 5, but the same *chaatrik* imagery pervades Mehl 4 as well). God as support of the unsupported: "Support of the unsupported, Saving Grace of the unsaved, Home of the homeless." (*niDhri-aa Dhar nigti-aa gat nithaavi-aa too thaa-o.*, Ang 1213). Ubiquity: "Wherever I go in the ten directions, You are there with me." (Ang 1213).

A Mehl 4 formulation of divine transcendence through devotion: "From Your Oneness, You become tens of thousands, and from tens of thousands, You become One. I cannot describe Your state and extent. / You are Infinite — Your value cannot be appraised. Everything I see is Your play." (*aykas tay laakh laakh tay aykaa tayree gat mit kahi na sakaa-o. / too bay-ant tayree mit nahee paa-ee-ai sabh tayro khayl dikhaa-o.* ||3||, Ang 1213).

**Guru as key**: The Vaar of Saarang (Mehl 4 Paurees, beginning Ang 1237) opens with a Shalok by Mehl 2 that provides one of the Granth's most memorable epistemological metaphors: "The key of the Guru opens the lock of attachment, in the house of the mind, under the roof of the body. / O Nanak, without the Guru, the door of the mind cannot be opened. No one else holds the key in hand." (*gur kunjee paahoo nival man kothaa tan chhat. / naanak gur bin man kaa taak na ugh-rhai avar na kunjee hath.* ||1||, Vaar of Saarang, Ang 1237). This Shalok is structurally placed at the head of the Var and serves as the Var's hermeneutical key.

### Guru Arjan (Mehl 5) — Saarang Chau-Padās and Chhants

Mehl 5 is the dominant author in Raag Saarang, occupying the bulk of the Chau-Pada and Chhant sections. Characteristic themes:

**Omnipresence and divine play**: "From Your Oneness, You become tens of thousands, and from tens of thousands, You become One." The creation is the Lord's play (*khayl*): "The Lord Himself created the whole drama of all the world's play." (*aapay khayl rachaa-i-on sabh jagat sabaa-i-aa.*, Pauree of Vaar, Ang 1237). The divine *as* all creation: "Like waves in the water, He created the creation." (*ghat ghat pooran kar bistheeran jal tarang ji-o rachan kee-aa.*, Chhant Mehl 5, Ang 1237).

**Indescribable Lord**: "What praises should I give, and what approval should I offer to Him? / The Perfect Lord is totally pervading and permeating all places." (*ka-un upmaa day-o kavan badaa-ee. / pooran poor rahi-o sarab thaa-ee.*, Chhant Mehl 5, Ang 1237). The devotee's yearning: "I ask the Saints, what is my Lord and Master like? / I offer my heart, to one who brings me news of Him." (*poochhha-o sant mayro thaakur kaisaa. / heeN-o araapa-uN dayh sadaysaa.*, Ang 1237). God is described as permeating "all places, interspaces and countries" (*thaan thaanantar daysaa*, Ang 1237).

**Saadh Sangat**: "I speak with the Company of the Holy; I am in love with the Holy people of the Lord. / Servant Nanak has found the Lord through the Guru's Teachings." (*saaDhan kaa sang saaDh si-o gosat. / jan naanak paa-i-aa hai gurmat.*, Ang 1213). The Chhant section reinforces: "The color of the Lord's Love is the one color of our Lord and Master; in the Saadh Sangat, the Company of the Holy, God is realized." (*har rangee ik rangee thaakur satsang parabh jaataa.*, Ang 1237).

**Detachment and the Guru**: Mehl 5 in Saarang: "Why do you not worship and adore Him? Join together with the Holy Saints; any instant, your time shall come. / All your property and wealth, and all that you see — none of it will go along with you." (*ki-o na aaraaDhahu mil kar saaDhahu gharee muhtak baylaa aa-ee. / arath darab sabh jo kichh deesai sang na kachhoo jaa-ee.*, Ang 1237). The imagery of the all-knowing Lord: "The Dear Lord is the Inner-knower, the Searcher of hearts." (*har jee-o antarjaamee jaan.*, Ang 1213).

### Raag Saarang — Mehl 9 (Guru Tegh Bahadur)

Mehl 9 contributes four short Du-Pada compositions to Raag Saarang. Their content is consistent with his Basant Hindol compositions: impermanence, the illusoriness of worldly bonds, and the sufficiency of the Lord's name.

The first (Ang 1232): "No one will be your help and support, except the Lord. / Who has any mother, father, child or spouse? Who is anyone's brother or sister?" (*har bin tayro ko na sahaa-ee. / kaaN kee maat pitaa sut banitaa ko kaahoo ko bhaa-ee.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1232). The attachment to property is named: "All the wealth, land and property which you consider your own — when you leave your body, none of it shall go along with you. Why do you cling to them?" (Ang 1232). The conclusion: "Says Nanak, the whole world is totally false; it is like a dream in the night." (*naanak kahat jagat sabh mithi-aa ji-o supnaa rainaa-ee.* ||2||1||, Ang 1232).

The second (Ang 1233): "O mortal, why are you engrossed in corruption? / No one is allowed to remain in this world; one comes, and another departs." — and: "Whatever is seen, shall all disappear, like the shade of a passing cloud." (*jo deesai so sagal binaasai ji-o baadar kee chhaahee.* ||1||, Ang 1233). "Abandon egotism, and grasp the Sanctuary of the Saints; you shall be liberated in an instant. / O servant Nanak, without meditating and vibrating on the Lord God, there is no peace, even in dreams." (*taj abhimaan saran santan gahu mukat hohi chhin maahee. / jan naanak bhagvant bhajan bin sukh supnai bhee naahee.* ||2||2||, Ang 1233).

The third (Ang 1233): "O mortal, why have you wasted your life? / Intoxicated with Maya and its riches, involved in corrupt pleasures, you have not sought the Sanctuary of the Lord." (*kahaa nar apno janam gavaavai. / maa-i-aa mad bikhi-aa ras rachi-o raam saran nahee aavai.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1233). "This whole world is just a dream; why does seeing it fill you with greed? / Whatever comes into being, shall all pass away; no one is allowed to remain." (Ang 1233).

### Vaar of Saarang (Mehl 4, Angs 1237–1251)

The Vaar of Saarang is sung "to the tune of Mehma-Hasna" (a pre-existing Punjabi ballad tune). Thirty-six Paurees by Mehl 4; Shaloks by multiple Mehls. The Vaar is structured around the Gurmukh/Manmukh opposition, Satsang as soteriological necessity, and the supremacy of Naam over all other practices. The Vaar's closing Pauree (Pauree 36) affirms divine sovereignty: "The Lord Himself is the Lord and Master of all. He has subdued all, and all are under the Hukam of His Command. / O Nanak, the Lord does whatever He pleases. Everyone walks in harmony with His Will." (*naanak har bhaavai so karay sabh chalai rajaa-ee.* ||36||1|| Sudh||, Ang 1251). The internal theology of the Vaar consistently presents three interlocking themes: (1) God as the original Self-creator who set all creation in motion through the three gunas; (2) human beings bound by Maya and freed only by Naam; (3) the Saadh Sangat as the institutional site of Naam-reception.

### Bhagat Bāṇī of Raag Saarang (Angs 1252)

The Bhagat Bāṇī of Saarang is compact, located at Ang 1252, and includes compositions by [[kabir|Kabir]] and [[namdev|Namdev]].

**[[kabir|Kabir]]** (Ang 1252): Two compositions. The first is a vigorous anti-pride composition: "O mortal, why are you so proud of small things? / With a few pounds of grain and a few coins in your pocket, you are totally puffed up with pride." (*kahaa nar garbas thoree baat. / man das naaj takaa chaar gaaNthee aiNdou taydhou jaat.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1252). The satirical argument moves from small wealth to great kings: "With great pomp and ceremony, you control a hundred villages, with an income of hundreds of thousands of dollars. / The power you exert will last for only a few days, like the green leaves of the forest." (Ang 1252). The historical example — "Emperors, even greater than Raawan, passed away in an instant" — points toward the universality of impermanence. The counsel is Satsang and Naam: "Those who are mercifully blessed by the Lord of the Universe, join the Sat Sangat, the True Congregation." (Ang 1252). The final verse: "Says Kabeer, meditate and vibrate on the Lord, O madman. Your life is uselessly wasting away." (*kahat kabeer raam bhaj ba-uray janam akaarath jaat.* ||4||1||, Ang 1252). The second Kabir composition in Saarang is a paradox composition (*ulat-bāṃsī* register): "The one who goes laughing returns crying, and the one who goes crying returns laughing. / What is inhabited becomes deserted, and what is deserted becomes inhabited." (*hasto jaa-ay so rovat aavai rovat jaa-ay so hasai. / basto ho-ay ho-ay so oojar oojar ho-ay so basai.* ||1||, Ang 1252). This extends to cosmic reversals: water becomes desert, desert becomes well, well becomes mountain, earth rises to sky and falls again; beggar becomes king, king becomes beggar; fool becomes Pandit, Pandit becomes fool; woman becomes man, man becomes woman. "Says Kabeer, God is the Beloved of the Holy Saints. I am a sacrifice to His image." (*kaho kabeer saaDhoo ko pareetam tis moorat balihaaree.* ||4||2||, Ang 1252).

**[[namdev|Namdev]]** (Ang 1252): One composition in the Saarang Bhagat section. The soul is warned against wandering into "the forest of corruption": "O mortal, why are you going into the forest of corruption? / You have been misled into eating the toxic drug." (*kaa-ayN ray man bikhi-aa ban jaa-ay. / bhoolou ray thagmooree khaa-ay.* ||1|| rahaa-o.||, Ang 1252). The fish image: "You are like a fish living in the water; / you do not see the net of death." (*jaisay meen paanee meh rahai. / kaal jaal kee suDh nahee lahai.*, Ang 1252). The allure of pleasure is the tongue's desire: tongue-taste (*jihbaa suaad*) — a recurring Kabir-school image for sensory attachment that Namdev adopts here. The corrective is the Gurmukh's recognition of the Lord's presence, in implicit contrast to the Manmukh's blind wandering.

### Theological Summary: Raag Saarang

Raag Saarang's governing theological mood is the soul's thirst (*piaas*) for the divine and the cooling, quenching relief that comes through the Name. The rainy-season context maps directly onto the rāg's spiritual register:

1. **Thirst and Darshan**: The soul-bride suffers without the vision (*darshan*) of the Lord; the *chaatrik* bird waits for rain. Receiving the Naam is the rain that quenches. "How can I survive without the Lord? I am suffering in pain." (Mehl 1, Ang 1197).
2. **Omnipresence (Mehl 5)**: Saarang carries Mehl 5's most extensive development of divine ubiquity — "From Your Oneness, You become tens of thousands, and from tens of thousands, You become One" — expressed through the *jal tarang* (waves in water) image.
3. **Anti-formalism**: The Vaar of Saarang's Shaloks (Mehl 1 and Mehl 2) constitute one of the Granth's most comprehensive rejections of formal religious practice, from Vedas to yoga to pilgrimage to warfare. "The Lord is won over only by His Name." (Mehl 1, Vaar Shalok).
4. **Impermanence (Mehl 9)**: Guru Tegh Bahadur's four Saarang compositions articulate the most focused meditation on impermanence in the rāg — family bonds, wealth, the body, and the world itself are "false, like a dream in the night." The Sanctuary of the Saints (*santan kee saran*) is the only enduring refuge.
5. **Bhagat Kabir's reversals**: Kabir's *ulat-bāṃsī* compositions in Saarang add a cosmological vertigo — all worldly hierarchies and conditions are unstable and reversible. The only stable reality is the Lord Himself.
6. **Satsang and Naam**: The Vaar of Saarang and Mehl 5's Chau-Padās consistently present the Saadh Sangat as the institutional locus where Naam is received and sustained.

The Granth's Saarang section closes at Ang 1252, with Kabir's final verse acting as a transition out of the rāg and into the next section of the Granth.

## Raag Malaar (Angs 1253–1281)

### Overview

Raag Malaar is the monsoon raag of the Granth, associated with the Indian rainy season (*Saawan*). Its dominant poetic register is that of the soul as a thirsty bride (*sohagan*) or a rain-bird (*baabeehaa*) crying out for rain — figures of aching longing that resolve only when the divine rains down. The file opens with the end of the previous raag (Raag Saarang, including a composition by [[kabir|Kabir]] and one by the Bhagat Parmaanand), and Raag Malaar proper begins at Ang 1254.

Composition types include *Chau-Padas* (four-stanza hymns, the standard form) and *Du-Tukas* (two-stanza forms). Later in the section (approaching Ang 1282) appear *Shaloks* and a *Var* with Paurees, the latter employing the rain imagery in an extended meditative mode. The theological register consistently identifies the divine rain with the [[nam-simaran|Naam]] and with the Guru's grace; outer rainfall is shadow, inner drenching is substance.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak)

Guru Nanak's Malaar compositions open the raag section and are the most numerous among the First Mehl offerings. Several dominant themes recur across them:

**The rain of the Name.** The defining image of the raag: "The rain pours down, and my mind is drenched with His Love. The drop of Ambrosial Nectar pleases my heart; the Guru has fascinated my mind, which is drenched in the sublime essence of the Lord." (*baras ghanaa mayraa man bheenaa. amrit boond suhaanee hee-arai gur mohee man har ras leenaa.* — Ang 1254–55)

**The soul-bride imagery.** The waiting soul as bride, the divine as husband-lord (*pir*): "So rain down, O clouds. My Husband Lord has come home. I am a sacrifice to my Guru, who has led me to meet my Lord God." (*baras ghanaa mayraa pir ghar aa-i-aa. bal jaavaaN gur apnay pareetam jin har parabh aan milaa-i-aa.* — Ang 1255) The bride who has not known union with her husband "weeps and wails with a wretched face" and is "caught in the noose of her own karma" (Ang 1255).

**The Naam as medicine.** A striking extended metaphor: "Pain is the poison. The Lord's Name is the antidote. Grind it up in the mortar of contentment, with the pestle of charitable giving. Take it each and every day, and your body shall not waste away." (*dukh mahuraa maaran har naam. silaa santokh peesan hath daan. nit nit layho na chheejai dayh.* — Ang 1257) A counter-voice from the same hymn chides the mortal: "O foolish doctor, don't give me medicine. The pain persists, and the body continues to suffer" — the point being that no physician or outer remedy reaches the disease of separation from the Lord.

**Critique of ritual religion.** "He does not try to wash away his angry passions by bathing at sacred shrines. He does not love the Name of the Lord." (Ang 1255) Pilgrimage, the Vedas, and outer forms are dismissed when not accompanied by inner transformation through the Naam.

**Seasonal/cosmological reflection.** "As is the season, so is the comfort of the body, and so is the body itself. O Nanak, that season is beautiful; without the Name, what season is it?" (*jayhee rut kaa-i-aa sukh tayhaa tayho jayhee dayhee. naanak rut suhaavee saa-ee bin naavai rut kayhee.* — Ang 1254)

**The body as mansion.** "The body is a mansion, a temple, the home of the Lord; He has infused His Infinite Light into it. O Nanak, the Gurmukh is invited to the Mansion of the Lord's Presence; the Lord unites him in His Union." (Ang 1256)

**The Unseen Lord.** "The Unknown cannot be known; O Inaccessible, Unborn Lord God, You are the Lord and Master of masters. How can I compare You to anyone else I see? All are beggars - You are the Great Giver." (Ang 1255)

### Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das)

Guru Amar Das's Malaar compositions develop the [[hukam|Hukam]] theme extensively and deploy the [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh/Manmukh]] contrast with sustained intensity.

**Hukam as the key.** "Those who realize the Hukam of the Lord's Command are united with Him; through the Word of His Shabad, their egotism is burnt away." (*jinee hukam pachhaani-aa say maylay ha-umai sabad jalaa-ay.* — Ang 1258) "O mortal, accept His Will and find peace." The creation itself is a function of Hukam: "The Creator Himself beholds it; He enjoins us as He pleases." (Ang 1258)

**The rain-bird.** "Each and every instant, the rain-bird cries and calls. Without seeing her Beloved, she does not sleep at all. She cannot endure this separation. When she meets the True Guru, then she intuitively meets her Beloved." (Ang 1260) The *baabeehaa* (Indian pied cuckoo, believed to cry for rain) as figure of the longing soul. Two Shaloks at this location (Ang 1282 area) elaborate: "O rainbird, do not cry out. Do not let this mind of yours be so thirsty for a drop of water. Obey the Hukam, the Command of your Lord and Master, and your thirst shall be quenched." (Mehl 3 Shalok) And: "O rainbird, your place is in the water; you move around in the water. But you do not appreciate the water, and so you cry out." (Mehl 3 Shalok)

**The Manmukh.** "The self-willed manmukhs are forever separated from the Lord; no one is with them. They are stricken with the great disease of egotism; they are hit on the head by the Messenger of Death." (Ang 1258) "The self-willed manmukhs never give up their hypocrisy; in the love of duality, they suffer in pain." (Ang 1260)

**The Jivan-mukta.** "Those who are attached to the Guru's Teachings, are Jivan-mukta, liberated while yet alive. They remain forever awake and aware night and day, in devotional worship of the Lord." (*jeevat mukat gurmatee laagay. har kee bhagat an-din sad jaagay.* — Ang 1262)

**The fourth state.** "He comes to dwell in the fourth state (*chautha pad*); he remains merged in the True Lord." (*cha-uthai pad vaasaa ho-i-aa sachai rahai samaa-ay.* — Ang 1257) The fourth state (*turiyaa*) is the state of liberation beyond the three qualities (gunas) and waking/dream/deep-sleep consciousness; found in Sat Sangat by grace.

**The pandit redefined.** A sustained address to the Brahmanical *pandit*: "He alone is a Pandit, who sheds the load of the three qualities. Night and day, he chants the Name of the One Lord... He accepts the Teachings of the True Guru... He remains forever unattached in the state of Nirvaanaa." (Ang 1260)

**World as foam.** "Like foam bubbling up on the surface of the water, so is this world. It shall eventually merge back into that from which it came, and all its expanse shall be gone." (Ang 1258)

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das)

Guru Ram Das's Malaar compositions are marked by heightened devotional intensity and the imagery of rivers seeking the dust of the feet of the Holy.

**Dust of the saints as purifier.** "The Ganges, the Jamunaa, the Godaavari and the Saraswati — these rivers strive for the dust of the feet of the Holy." Pilgrims bring their filth to bathe in the rivers, and the rivers' own pollution is removed by the dust of the saints (Ang 1263). "Instead of bathing at the sixty-eight sacred shrines of pilgrimage, take your cleansing bath in the Name. When the dust of the feet of the Sat Sangat rises up into the eyes, all filthy evil-mindedness is removed." (Ang 1263)

**The wall of egotism.** "The soul-bride and the Husband Lord live together as one, but the hard wall of egotism has come between them. The Perfect Guru demolishes the wall of egotism; servant Nanak has met the Lord, the Lord of the World." (Ang 1263)

**The Guru as True God.** "The Divine True Guru is the Embodiment, the Image of the Lord; He utters the Ambrosial Word. O Nanak, blessed and good is the destiny of that humble being, who focuses his consciousness on the Feet of the Lord." (Ang 1264)

**The saint compared to the swan.** "As the swan separates the milk from the water, the Holy Saint removes the fire of egotism from the body." (Ang 1265)

**Meditative intensity.** "My eyes gaze eternally on the Lord, Har, Har. Gazing on the True Guru, my mind blossoms forth. I have met with the Lord, the Lord of the World." (Ang 1263)

### Bhagat Bāṇī: Namdev

The Malaar section of [[namdev|Namdev]]'s bāṇī begins at approximately Ang 1280 ("Raag Malaar, The Word Of The Devotee Naam Dayv Jee"). It is a single extended composition in cosmic-royal imagery, presenting the divine as the sovereign king whose household encompasses all of creation:

"His Home is the pavilion seen in all directions; His ornamental heavenly realms fill the seven worlds alike. In His Home, the virgin Lakshmi dwells. The moon and the sun are His two lamps; the wretched Messenger of Death stages his dramas, and levies taxes on all. Such is my Sovereign Lord King, the Supreme Lord of all." (Ang 1280)

The poem proceeds through description of the cosmic household: Brahma as the four-faced potter who created the universe; Shiva as the "insane Guru of the World"; sin and virtue as standard-bearers at the door; Death as doorman; heavenly heralds, celestial singers, and Rishis as minstrels; Maya as hand-maiden. The composition moves in a refrain structure, each stanza closing with "Such is the Sovereign Lord King of the three worlds" (*so aisaa raajaa taribhavan patee*). The tone is cosmic wonder at divine sovereignty, voiced in the Bhakti idiom of the Sant tradition.

### Theological Summary

Raag Malaar deploys the monsoon season as a sustained theological metaphor: the soul longs for the divine rain of the Naam as parched earth longs for water, as the rain-bird longs for a single drop. Acceptance of [[hukam|Hukam]] quenches the thirst; [[haumai|Haumai]] is the obstacle preventing the union. The [[gurmukh-manmukh|Gurmukh]] receives the rain and blooms; the manmukh remains caught in the cycle of birth and death. The Guru is both the cloud that brings the rain and the True Teacher who directs the soul toward divine union. Throughout, the raag resists reducing the divine to ritual or pilgrimage — the only purification is the Naam, the only medicine is the Name of the Lord.

---

## Raag Kaanraa (Angs 1282–1309)

### Overview

Raag Kaanraa occupies Angs 1282–1309. The transition from the Malaar Var (with its Shaloks and Paurees) to Kaanraa proper occurs within this span; the Kaanraa Chau-Padas begin at approximately Ang 1294–95 ("Raag Kaanraa, Chau-Padas, Fourth Mehl, First House"). The raag is strongly associated with the Fourth and Fifth Mehls. Composition types include Chau-Padas in multiple houses, Partaal (a complex rhythmic form, in the Fifth House), and shorter forms; the Bhagat Bāṇī of Namdev concludes the section.

The dominant mood of Kaanraa is longing combined with the joy of meeting the Lord's servants (the *sant jan*, Holy people). The raag is less explicitly seasonal than Malaar; its imagery is more courtly and mercantile — the Lord as king, the Naam as precious merchandise, the soul as a trader in the market of devotion.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das)

Guru Ram Das is the predominant voice in Kaanraa. A series of Chau-Padas elaborate a central dynamic: encounter with the Holy (*sant jan*) as the mechanism of transformation.

**Meeting the saints.** "Meeting with the Holy people, my mind blossoms forth. I am a sacrifice, a sacrifice, a sacrifice, a sacrifice to those Holy beings; joining the Sangat, the Congregation, I am carried across to the other side." (*mayraa man saaDh janaaN mil hari-aa. ha-o bal bal bal bal saaDh janaaN ka-o mil sangat paar utri-aa.* — Ang 1294) The saint functions as intermediary between the devotee and the Lord: "Blessed, blessed are the Holy, who know the Lord God. Meeting with the Holy, even sinners are saved." (Ang 1294)

**The mind and the saint.** "The mind roams and rambles all around in all directions. Meeting with the Holy, it is overpowered and brought under control, just as when the fisherman spreads his net over the water, he catches and overpowers the fish." (Ang 1295)

**Critique of idol worship.** "The ignorant and the blind wander deluded by doubt; deluded and confused, they pick flowers to offer to their idols. They worship lifeless stones and serve the tombs of the dead; all their efforts are useless and fruitless." (*nirjee-o poojeh marhaa sarayveh sabh birthee ghaal gavaavai.* — Ang 1297) Against this, "He alone is said to be the True Guru, who realizes God, and proclaims the Sermon of the Lord, Har, Har." (Ang 1297)

**The fortress of Maya.** "The commodity of the Lord, Har, Har, is locked in the fortress of Maya; through the Word of the Guru's Shabad, I have conquered the fortress." (*har har vasat maa-i-aa garheh vayrhHee gur kai sabad lee-o garh jeet.* — Ang 1296) False attachment to family is the shadow of a passing tree: "just like the passing shade of the tree, your body-wall shall crumble in an instant." (Ang 1296)

**Slave of the slave.** The closing of one Chau-Pada achieves intense humility: "Servant Nanak is the slave of the slave of Your slaves. O God, please bless me with Your Grace; please protect me and keep me with Your humble servants." (*jan naanak daas daas daasan ko parabh karahu kirpaa raakho jan saath.* — Ang 1298)

**The Partaal form.** The Fifth House includes a Partaal composition (a complex rhythmic setting with multiple time signatures). Its language becomes more rapturous and repetitive: "O mind, meditate on the Lord, the Lord of the World. The Lord is the Jewel, the Diamond, the Ruby. The Lord fashions the Gurmukhs in His Mint... I have become enraptured, enraptured, enraptured, meditating on the Lord." (*har jap bha-ee nihaal nihaal nihaal.* — Ang 1299)

**Predestination and grace.** "She alone obtains her Husband Lord, who is so pre-destined. Through the Guru's Teachings, she sings the Glorious Praises of the Lord." The recurring motif of *dhur likhi-aa* (what is inscribed from the beginning, predestined) appears across multiple compositions: "According to that pre-ordained destiny inscribed on my forehead by my Lord and Master, I have enshrined the Feet of the Guru, the True Guru, within my heart." (Ang 1295)

**The Lord like a calf and cow.** "Meeting with the Lord's Saints, I meet with the Lord, just as when the calf is seen — the cow is there as well." (*har kay sant milay har mili-aa jaisay ga-oo daykh bachhraak.* — Ang 1296)

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan)

Guru Arjan's Kaanraa compositions (from Ang 1299 onward) are often shorter in individual unit length but appear in many houses. They emphasize the sweetness of the Naam and the community of the Lord's servants.

**The sweetness of the Lord.** "The Lord seems sweet to that humble being who is blessed by the Grace of the Lord. His hunger and pain are totally taken away; he chants the Glorious Praises of the Lord, Har, Har." (Ang 1300)

**The servant as fish.** "The humble servant of the Lord is a fish in the Water of the Lord. Forgetting the Lord, he would dry up and die." (*har kaa jan har jal kaa meenaa har bisrat foot marai.* — Ang 1301) This image echoes and intensifies the soul-in-God's-ocean image from Kalyaan.

**One tongue, many praises.** "Sing the Glorious Praises of the Lord, the Lord of the Universe. Let my one tongue become two hundred thousand — with them all, I will meditate on the Lord, Har, Har, and chant the Word of the Shabad." (*har gun gaavhu jagdees. aykaa jeeh keechai lakh bees.* — Ang 1302) This longing for multiple tongues to praise the infinite God appears across several Kaanraa and Kalyaan compositions.

**Inner vs. outer purity.** "Those who do not love the Lord in their hearts are deceitful; they continually practice deception. What can anyone give them to eat? Whatever they themselves plant, they must eat." (Ang 1302)

**Later compositions.** Several later houses in Kaanraa (houses 5–11, Angs 1305–1309) are very short, some only two stanzas, with intensely condensed theological statements. One composition captures the essential Sikh hermeneutical stance toward extra-scriptural authority: "Prabhoo says, the Lord is the Destroyer of sins, the Cleanser of stains. The Guru unites the devotee with God; there is no other way." (*parabh kahan malan dahan lahan gur milay aan nahee upaa-o.* — pause verse, Ang 1306)

### Bhagat Bāṇī: Namdev

The Kaanraa section closes with a single composition by [[namdev|Namdev]] (Ang 1318–19): "Such is the Sovereign Lord, the Inner-knower, the Searcher of Hearts; He sees everything as clearly as one's face reflected in a mirror. He dwells in each and every heart; no stain or stigma sticks to Him. He is liberated from bondage; He does not belong to any social class... As one's face is reflected in the water, so does Naam Dayv's Beloved Lord and Master appear." (*aiso raam raa-ay antarjaamee. jaisay darpan maahi badan parvaanee.* — Ang 1318) The mirror and water-reflection imagery conveys divine omnipresence and immanence in distinctly Bhakti terms.

### Theological Summary

Raag Kaanraa grounds liberation in the community of the Holy (*sant jan*, *satsangat*) as the living embodiment of grace. The individual cannot reach God alone; the meeting with saints is the mechanism by which the wall of haumai is broken and the Naam enters the heart. Maya is a fortress, but the Guru's Shabad is the key that conquers it. Worship of images and tombs is empty. The divine is simultaneously the ocean in which the soul is a fish, and the merchant-banker whose commodity (the Naam) is the highest profit.

---

## Raag Kalyaan (Angs 1310–1327)

### Overview

Raag Kalyaan covers Angs 1310–1327. It opens with compositions of the Fourth Mehl and then introduces the Fifth Mehl, with a sub-raag variant *Kalyaan Bhopaalee* appearing within the Fourth Mehl section (Ang 1318). The Kalyaan section also contains *Ashtapadees* (eight-stanza hymns) by the Fourth Mehl, which are among the most extended compositions in this range of the Granth. There is no Bhagat Bāṇī in Kalyaan.

Kalyaan as a musical raag is associated with the evening hours and a mood of elevation and wonder. Theologically, the dominant registers are transcendence and immanence simultaneously: the Lord is infinitely beyond knowing, yet is Mother and Father, ocean and fish, flower and bee.

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das)

Guru Ram Das opens Kalyaan with a declaration of divine incomprehensibility: "The Lord, the Beauteous Lord — no one has found His limits. The Names of the Lord are Countless and Unfathomable. My Sovereign Lord is Unfathomable and Incomprehensible. The virtuous and the spiritual teachers have given it great thought, but they have not found even an iota of His Value." (*har kay naam asaNkh agam heh agam agam har raa-i-aa. gunee gi-aanee surat baho keenee ik til nahee keemat paa-i-aa.* — Ang 1319)

**Parent-child relationship.** "I am a child — You cherish and sustain me. You are the Great Primal Being, my Mother and Father." (*ham baarik partipaaray tumray too bad purakh pitaa mayraa maa-i-aa.* — Ang 1319) This image of God as parental guardian recurs: "I am a foolish and ignorant child; Father, please teach me! Your child makes mistakes again and again, but still, You are pleased with him, O Father of the Universe." (Ang 1320)

**Four goals of life transformed.** "Righteousness and Dharmic faith, success and prosperity, pleasure, the fulfillment of desires and liberation — all follow the humble servant of the Lord like a shadow." (*Dharam arath sabh kaam mokh hai jan peechai lag firtha-ee.* — Ang 1320) The classical Hindu four aims of life (*purusharthas*: dharma, artha, kama, moksha) are here made secondary: they follow the servant of the Lord automatically, not as things to be pursued independently.

**God distinguishes genuine from counterfeit.** "So behold the Wondrous and Amazing Play of the Lord. In an instant, He distinguishes the genuine from the counterfeit." (Ang 1321)

**Single petition.** "Lord, You are the Great Giver, our All-powerful Lord and Master; O Lord, I beg for only one gift from You... Lord, please bless servant Nanak with Your Grace, that Your Feet may abide forever within my heart." (Ang 1321)

**The Ashtapadees.** These extended eight-stanza hymns (Angs 1322–1327) develop the theology of the Lord-servant relationship in greater detail. Themes include: the danger of slander aimed at the Lord's servants (the slanderer as snake-stung), the protection God extends to His own across the ages, the absorption of individual light into divine Light (*jotee jot milaa-ay*).

### Kalyaan Bhopaalee (Mehl 4)

The sub-raag *Kalyaan Bhopaalee* contains a single composition (Ang 1318) addressed to the divine in a cascade of names: "O Supreme Lord God, Transcendent Lord and Master, Destroyer of pain, Transcendental Lord God. All Your devotees beg of You. Ocean of peace, carry us across the terrifying world-ocean; You are the Wish-fulfilling Jewel." (*saat-gal bhagat jaacheh sukh saagar bhav niDh taran har chintaamanay.* — Ang 1318) The composition closes with: "The Lord preserves the honor of His humble devotees; O servant Nanak, the Lord Himself showers them with His Grace." (Ang 1318)

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan)

Guru Arjan's Kalyaan compositions (two houses, Angs 1319–1322) are among the most lyrical in this range.

**The bumble-bee and lotus feet.** "Please grant me this blessing: May the bumble-bee of my mind be immersed again and again in the Honey of Your Lotus Feet. I am not concerned with any other water; please bless this songbird with a Drop of Your Water, Lord. Unless I meet my Lord, I am not satisfied. Nanak lives, gazing upon the Blessed Vision of His Darshan." (*al makrand charan kamal si-o man fayr fayr reejhai. aan jalaa si-o kaaj na kachhoo-ai har boond chaatrik ka-o deejai.* — Ang 1319) The *chaatrik* (pied cuckoo / papeeha) reappears, its longing for rain (as in Malaar) transposed to longing for the divine darshan.

**This beggar begs.** "This beggar begs and begs for Your Name, Lord. You are the Support of all, the Master of all, the Giver of absolute peace." (Ang 1319)

**The Lord's glory.** "O, the Wondrous Glory of my Beloved! My mind is rejuvenated forever by His Wondrous Love. Brahma, Shiva, the Siddhas, the silent sages and Indra beg for the charity of His Praise and devotion to Him." (*mayray laalan kee sobhaa. sad navtan man rangee sobhaa.* — Ang 1322)

**Diamond meets diamond.** "Fruitful, fruitful, fruitful is the Blessed Vision of His Darshan; touching His Touch, I sing His Glorious Praises. O Nanak, one's essence is blended into the Essence; the diamond of the mind is pierced through by the Diamond of the Lord." (*naanak tat tat si-o milee-ai heerai heer biDhaa-ee-ai.* — Ang 1322)

### Theological Summary

Raag Kalyaan articulates the divine relationship in terms of wonder, incomprehensibility, and reciprocal love. The Lord cannot be measured, yet is intimately close — parent, ocean, diamond that pierces the mind's diamond. All four goals of human life (dharma, artha, kama, moksha) come as byproducts of devotion rather than as independent pursuits. The saint is protected by God against slanders and adversaries; light merges into Light at the moment of liberation. The section has no Bhagat Bāṇī, keeping its focus on the Gurus' direct address to the divine.

---

## Raag Prabhaatee / Parbhaatee (Angs 1328–1351)

### Overview

Raag Prabhaatee (also spelled Parbhaatee; the two names appear interchangeably in the source) is the dawn raag — *prabhaatee* means "of the dawn" (*prabhaat*). It covers Angs 1328–1351, making it the final raag in this range, and one of the most theologically concentrated sections of the Granth's later portions.

The raag encompasses multiple sub-forms: *Parbhaatee Bibhaas* (a variant form associated with the *bibhaas* melodic shade), and simple *Prabhaatee*. Composition types include Chau-Padas, Ashtapadees (Mehl 1), Partaal forms, and shorter compositions. Authors across this span include all three prominent Mehls of the classical period: Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak, the dominant voice), Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das), Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das), and Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan). Bhagat Bāṇī appears at the end from both Namdev and Baynee.

Theologically, the raag carries the pre-dawn urgency of *amrit vela* — the devotional hours before sunrise — when the Naam is to be taken up before the busyness of the world reasserts itself.

### Mehl 1 (Guru Nanak) — Bibhaas and Standard Prabhaatee

Guru Nanak's Prabhaatee compositions are among the most philosophically wide-ranging he composed in any raag.

**The Naam as everything.** An extended meditation (opening the raag) on the Naam as the totality of strength, honor, army, king, and support: "Your Name carries us across; Your Name brings respect and worship. Your Name embellishes us; it is the object of the awakened mind... Your Name is my strength; Your Name is my support. Your Name is my army; Your Name is my king. Your Name brings honor, glory and approval. By Your Grace, one is blessed with the banner and the insignia of Your Mercy." (*naa-o tayraa taan naa-o deebaan. naa-o tayraa laskar naa-o sultaan.* — Ang 1328)

**Pre-dawn practice explicitly named.** "Meditate on the Name, and the Word of the Shabad, in the early hours before dawn; leave your worldly entanglements behind." (*naa-o parbhaatai sabad Dhi-aa-ee-ai chhodahu dunee pareetaa.* — Ang 1330) This is the explicit articulation of *amrit vela* (the sacred pre-dawn time) as the occasion for Naam-simaran.

**The Guru as superior to all tirthas.** "There is no sacred shrine equal to the Guru. The Guru encompasses the ocean of contentment. The Guru is the River, from which the Pure Water is obtained forever; it washes away the filth and pollution of evil-mindedness." (*gur samaan tirath nahee ko-ay. sar santokh taas gur ho-ay. gur daree-aa-o sadaa jal nirmal mili-aa durmat mail harai.* — Ang 1329) Finding the True Guru is "the perfect cleansing bath which transforms even beasts and ghosts into gods." (Ang 1329)

**Caste and birth rejected.** "God does not ask about social class or birth; you must find your true home. That is your social class and that is your status — the karma of what you have done. The pains of death and rebirth are eradicated; O Nanak, salvation is in the Lord's Name." (*jaat janam nah poochhee-ai sach ghar layho bataa-ay. saa jaat saa pat hai jayhay karam kamaa-ay.* — Ang 1330)

**The plundered blind man.** An unforgettable image of spiritual sleep: "He is awake, and even happy, but he is being plundered — he is blind! The noose is around his neck, and yet, his head is busy with worldly affairs." (*jaagat bigsai mootho anDhaa. gal faahee sir maaray DhanDhaa.* — Ang 1330) Against this: "The Lord of Awareness, the Lord of Life is awake and aware. He is the Ocean of peace, the Treasure of Ambrosial Nectar." (Ang 1330)

**The five thieves.** "The five wandering thieves are captured and held, and the egotistical pride of the mind is subdued. Visions of corruption, vice and evil-mindedness run away. Such is the spiritual wisdom of God." (Ang 1329)

**Gifts as virtues.** A distinctive composition casts the devotional gifts sought from God in terms of agricultural and household goods, each symbolically coded: "Please bless me with the rice of truth and self-restraint, the wheat of compassion, and the leaf-plate of meditation. Bless me with the milk of good karma, and the clarified butter, the ghee, of compassion... Let forgiveness and patience be my milk-cows, and let the calf of my mind intuitively drink in this milk. I beg for the clothes of modesty and the Lord's Praise." (Ang 1329)

**Neti-neti theology (via the householder-renunciate).** "Contemplate this in your mind, and see, O spiritual teacher. Who is the householder, and who is the renunciate? We come from Him; surrendering to Him, O Nanak, one is approved as a householder, and a renunciate." (*jis tay ho-aa so-ee kar maani-aa naanak girhee udaasee so parvaan.* — Ang 1330) The dissolution of the householder/renunciate binary into total surrender to the divine Will.

**Address to the Yogi.** "O detached Yogi, contemplate the essence of reality, and you shall never again come to be born into the world." (Ang 1328) Several Mehl 1 compositions address the *audioo* (detached Yogi) — a characteristic genre also seen in the Sidh Gosht — critiquing outer renunciation while endorsing inner detachment.

**The Persian wheel.** The *harhat* (Persian water-wheel with buckets on a chain) as figure for reincarnation: "The buckets on the chain of the Persian wheel rotate; one empties out to fill another. This is just like the Play of our Lord and Master; such is His Glorious Greatness." (Ang 1330) The soul empties and refills in the cycle of birth and death.

**Ashtapadees.** The Ashtapadee section of Prabhaatee (Mehl 1, Bibhaas) extends the Gurmukh/self-realization theology in eight-stanza units, but falls at or just past the end of this range (after Ang 1351).

### Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das)

Guru Amar Das's Prabhaatee compositions (Ang 1335 onward) continue the Naam-centrism and Hukam theology.

**Naam as profit in Kali Yuga.** An echo from Malaar: "In this Dark Age of Kali Yuga, the Lord's Name is the source of profit." (Ang 1338 area — this verse appeared also in Malaar, Mehl 3, and recurs here as a refrain-type saying across the Granth.)

**Living truth.** "When the mortal follows the Guru's Teachings, and lives the Truth, then he attains the state of emancipation." (Mehl 3, Prabhaatee)

### Mehl 4 (Guru Ram Das) — Bibhaas

Guru Ram Das's Bibhaas Prabhaatee compositions develop the soul-bride imagery in the dawn context.

**Awakening to the beloved.** The dawn framing of the soul's meeting: "The humble servant of the Lord sings His Praises in the pre-dawn hours. The Guru bestows the Divine Light." (Summary of Mehl 4 Prabhaatee themes, Ang 1340 area)

**Guru as ocean and river.** Consonant with Kalyaan imagery: the devotee as a fish needing the divine water, the Guru as the pure river. The Bibhaas Partaal (a complex rhythmic piece, Mehl 4) celebrates the meeting of the soul with the divine in the ecstatic language of absorption.

### Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) — Bibhaas

Guru Arjan's Prabhaatee compositions emphasize the sufficiency of the divine presence and the dismissal of elaborate religious observances.

**Absolute shelter.** "O my mind, take shelter in the Lord. Nothing else can help you at this time. The Lord alone is your friend and companion." (Mehl 5, Prabhaatee)

**The body as breath.** The preciousness of human birth and its fragility, themes appearing in the earlier raags (Malaar, Kaanraa), are reprised in compressed form: the human body as a brief opportunity that must be used for Naam-simaran.

### Bhagat Bāṇī: Namdev (Prabhaatee)

Three compositions by [[namdev|Namdev]] close the Prabhaatee section (ending at Ang 1351).

The first declares: "The mind alone knows the state of the mind; I tell it to the Knowing Lord. I chant the Name of the Lord, the Inner-knower, the Searcher of hearts — why should I be afraid? My mind is pierced through by the love of the Lord of the World. My God is All-pervading everywhere." (*antarjaamee raam ravaaN-ee mai dar kaisay chahee-ai. bayDhee-alay gopaal gosaa-ee. mayraa parabh ravi-aa sarbay thaa-ee.* — Ang 1351)

The second draws on the sandalwood tree as figure for divine transformation: "You, O Lord, are the Philosopher's Stone, and I am iron; associating with You, I am transformed into gold. You are Merciful; You are the gem and the jewel. Naam Dayv is absorbed in the Truth." (*tumH chay paaras ham chay lohaa sangay kanchan bhai-ilaa. too da-i-aal ratan laal naamaa saach samaa-ilaa.* — Ang 1351) The philosopher's stone (*paaras*) transforming iron to gold is a central image of the Sant tradition's theology of grace.

The third meditates on divine omnipresence and karma: "The Primal Being has no ancestry; He has staged this play. God is hidden deep within each and every heart... The mortal's actions hold the soul in the bondage of karma. Whatever he does, he does on his own. Prays Naam Dayv, whatever this soul wants, it obtains. Whoever abides in the Lord, becomes immortal." (*akul purakh ik chalit upaa-i-aa. ghat ghat antar barahm lukaa-i-aa.* — Ang 1351)

### Bhagat Bāṇī: Baynee (Prabhaatee)

The section concludes with a single composition by the Bhagat Baynee (a figure otherwise little represented in the Granth). Baynee's composition is a sharp critique of religious hypocrisy: "You rub your body with sandalwood oil, and place basil leaves on your forehead. But you hold a knife in the hand of your heart. You look like a thug; pretending to meditate, you pose like a crane. You try to look like a Vaishnaav, but the breath of life escapes through your mouth... You pray for hours to God the Beautiful. But your gaze is evil, and your nights are wasted in conflict." (*tan chandan mastak paatee. rid antar kar tal kaatee. thag disat bagaa liv laagaa. daykh baisno paraan mukh bhaagaa.* — Ang 1351 area) Baynee's voice recalls the polemical register of [[kabir|Kabir]] in attacking the gap between outer piety and inner reality.

### Theological Summary

Raag Prabhaatee, the dawn raag, gives the Granth's most explicit articulation of the pre-dawn devotional practice (*amrit vela*) as the paradigmatic moment of Naam-simaran. The Naam is all-sufficient: it is army, king, tirtha, medicine, food, clothing. The Guru surpasses all pilgrimage sites. Social caste is irrelevant to the divine; what matters is the karma of one's actions and the grace of the Guru's Shabad. The soul that sleeps through the dawn is being plundered while it smiles; the soul that awakens to the Name crosses over the terrifying world-ocean. Namdev's closing compositions anchor the section in the Sant theology of divine omnipresence, inner knowing, and transformation through the divine Naam — iron becoming gold through the philosopher's stone of grace.

## Raag Jaijaavantee (Angs 1352–1359)

### Overview

A brief raag section (seven pages) composed entirely by **Mehl 9** — [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]], the ninth Guru. It is prefaced with the full Mool Mantar and begins immediately with the dominant note of Guru Tegh Bahadur's voice: urgency in the face of death, the futility of worldly wealth, and the imperative of Naam.

Four compositions are present (numbered 1–4). Each follows the *chau-padā* structure with a *rahā'o* (pause/refrain).

### Theological Themes

**1. Life is slipping away; meditate now.** The *rahā'o* of the first shabad:
> "Meditate in remembrance on the Lord — meditate on the Lord; this alone shall be of use to you. / Abandon your association with Maya, and take shelter in the Sanctuary of God. / Remember that the pleasures of the world are false; this whole show is just an illusion." (Ang 1352)

**2. The body is impermanent — like a hailstone, it melts in no time.** Second shabad:
> "Meditate on the Lord — vibrate on the Lord; your life is slipping away. / Why am I telling you this again and again? You fool — why don't you understand? / Your body is like a hail-stone; it melts away in no time at all." (Ang 1352)

**3. The world's pleasures are a dream; Maya is the Lord's slave.** Third shabad:
> "Servant Nanak proclaims that the vast expanse of this world is just a dream. / Why not meditate on the Lord? Even Maya is His slave." (Ang 1352–1353)

**4. Death has arrived; no flight is possible.** Fourth shabad (Ang 1353):
> "Slipping away — your life is uselessly slipping away. / Night and day, you listen to the Puraanas, but you do not understand them, you ignorant fool! / Death has arrived; now where will you run?"

And:
> "You believed that this body was permanent, but it shall turn to dust. / Why don't you chant the Name of the Lord, you shameless fool?"

Closing verse of the fourth shabad offers the practical resolution:
> "Let devotional worship of the Lord enter into your heart, and abandon the intellectualism of your mind. / O Servant Nanak, this is the way to live in the world." (Ang 1353)

### Hermeneutical Character

Raag Jaijaavantee concentrates what might be called Guru Tegh Bahadur's *memento mori* theology — every shabad returns to death-awareness (*kāl chetanā*) as the catalyst for Naam practice. The refrain form enacts urgency: the pause (*rahā'o*) is the theological core, with the surrounding verses as elaboration. The tone is direct, even confrontational ("You fool," "you shameless fool"), distinguishing it from the more lyrical registers of earlier raags. The world (maya) is characterized as dream, illusion, hailstone, walls of sand — a sustained imagistic vocabulary of impermanence.

---

## Shalok Vaaran Tay Vadheek (Angs 1360–1410)

### Overview

"Shaloks in addition to the Vars" — a collection of shorter compositions that could not be accommodated within the Var sections themselves. This section contains several distinct subsections, each introduced with its own heading:

- **Shalok Sehskritee** (First Mehl and Fifth Mehl) — Sanskrit-register shaloks
- **Gaat'haa** (Fifth Mehl) — another Sanskrit-influence register
- **Phunhay** (Fifth Mehl) — bridal-imagery compositions

All are attributed to Guru Nanak (Mehl 1) and Guru Arjan (Mehl 5).

### Shalok Sehskritee — Key Themes and Verses

**Critique of empty ritual** (Mehl 1, Ang 1353):
