> "You study the scriptures, say your prayers and argue; / you worship stones and sit like a crane, pretending to meditate. / You speak lies and well-ornamented falsehood, / and recite your daily prayers three times a day. / The mala is around your neck, and the sacred tilak mark is on your forehead… / If you know God and the nature of karma, you know that all these rituals and beliefs are useless. / Says Nanak, meditate on the Lord with faith. / Without the True Guru, no one finds the Way."

**The Shabad as universal spiritual path** (Mehl 1, Ang 1353):
> "The Shabad is Yoga, the Shabad is spiritual wisdom; the Shabad is the Vedas for the Brahmin. / The Shabad is heroic bravery for the Kshatriya; the Shabad is service to others for the Soodra. / The Shabad for all is the Shabad, the Word of the One God, for one who knows this secret."

**God as the only permanent reality** (Mehl 5, Ang 1355):
> "Beauty fades away, islands fade away, the sun, moon, stars and sky fade away. / The earth, mountains, forests and lands fade away. / One's spouse, children, siblings and loved friends fade away. / Gold and jewels and the incomparable beauty of Maya fade away. / Only the Eternal, Unchanging Lord does not fade away."

**Ethical instruction** (Mehl 5, Ang 1355):
> "Do not delay in practicing righteousness; delay in committing sins. / Implant the Naam within yourself, and abandon greed."

**The rare treasure** (Mehl 5, Ang 1357):
> "Wealth and beauty are not so difficult to obtain. Paradise and royal power are not so difficult to obtain. / Foods and delicacies, children, friends, siblings; knowledge and wisdom; cleverness and trickery — these are not so difficult to obtain. / Only the Naam, the Name of the Lord, is difficult to obtain. O Nanak, it is only obtained by God's Grace, in the Saadh Sangat."

**The Immanent Lord** (Mehl 5, Ang 1356):
> "The Supreme Lord God, the Transcendent, Luminous Lord, dwells in each and every heart. / Nanak begs for this blessing from the Merciful Lord, that he may never forget Him, never forget Him."

**God as attribute-less and beyond form** (Mehl 5, Ang 1359):
> "God has no conch-shell, no religious mark, no paraphernalia; he does not have blue skin. / His Form is Wondrous and Amazing. He is beyond incarnation. / The Vedas say that He is not this, and not that (*neti neti*). / The Lord of the Universe is Lofty and High, Great and Infinite. / The Imperishable Lord abides in the hearts of the Holy."

**The five vices personified** (Mehl 5, Angs 1358–1359): A remarkable sequence addresses emotional attachment, sexual desire, anger, greed, and egotism each in turn as dangerous enemies:
- Attachment: "O emotional attachment, you are the invincible warrior of the battlefield of life; you totally crush and destroy even the most powerful."
- Sexual desire: "O sexual desire, you lead the mortals to hell; you make them wander in reincarnation through countless species."
- Anger: "O anger, you are the root of conflict; compassion never rises up in you."
- Greed: "O greed, you cling to even the great, assaulting them with countless waves… You have no respect for friends, ideals, relations, mother or father."
- Egotism: "O egotism, you are the root of birth and death and the cycle of reincarnation; you are the very soul of sin."

**Resolution**: "Nanak bows in humble surrender to the Lord; he seeks the Sanctuary of the Lord of the Universe."

### Gaat'haa (Fifth Mehl)

The Gaat'haa is described as "profound and infinite" (Ang 1361):
> "This Gaat'haa is profound and infinite; how rare are those who understand it. / They forsake sexual desire and worldly love, O Nanak, and praise the Lord in the Saadh Sangat."

Key verses include:
- "It is death to forget the Lord of the Universe. / It is life to meditate on the Name of the Lord." (Ang 1361)
- "The lowly nim tree, growing near the sandalwood tree, becomes just like the sandalwood tree. / But the bamboo tree, also growing near it, does not pick up its fragrance; it is too tall and proud." (Ang 1361)
- "When the leaves wither and fall, they cannot be attached to the branch again. / Without the Naam, there is misery and suffering. The mortal wanders in reincarnation day and night." (Ang 1362)

### Phunhay (Fifth Mehl)

The Phunhay uses the extended metaphor of the soul as bride yearning for the divine Husband. Key verse (Ang 1362):
> "O my companion, I have prepared everything: make-up, garlands and betel-leaves. / I have embellished myself with the sixteen decorations… / If my Husband Lord comes to my home, then I obtain everything. / O Lord! Without my Husband, all these adornments are useless."

On Ramdaspur (Amritsar, the city of Guru Ram Das):
> "Ramdaspur is prosperous and thickly populated, and incomparably beautiful. / O Lord! Bathing in the Sacred Pool of Raam Daas, the sins are washed away, O Nanak." (Ang 1362)

The soul's longing as the rainbird (*chaatrik*):
> "The rainbird is very smart; in its consciousness, it longs for the friendly rain. / It longs for that, to which its breath of life is attached. / It wanders depressed, from forest to forest, for the sake of a drop of water. / O Lord! In just the same way, the humble servant of the Lord begs for the Naam." (Ang 1362)

---

## Shalok Farid Jee (Angs 1377–1384)

### Overview

The most concentrated expression of Sufi sensibility within the SGGS. Sheikh Farid's shaloks (75+ verses plus responsive verses by Guru Amar Das [Mehl 3] and Guru Arjan [Mehl 5]) constitute his complete shalok collection as preserved in the Granth. They are introduced as "Shaloks Of Shaykh Fareed Jee" with the Ik Oankaar invocation.

The shaloks are characterized by:
- First-person address in Punjabi dialect, often using "Fareed" as a reflexive self-address
- Nature imagery for spiritual states (trees, birds, water, the body as withered crop)
- Unflinching meditation on death, aging, and bodily decay
- Sufi *biraha* (separation-longing) as the engine of spiritual life
- Critique of religious hypocrisy
- An ethos of radical simplicity and voluntary suffering

### Key Shaloks — Farid

**Death as the pre-ordained wedding** (Shalok 1, Ang 1377):
> "The day of the bride's wedding is pre-ordained. / On that day, the Messenger of Death, of whom she had only heard, comes and shows its face. / It breaks the bones of the body and pulls the helpless soul out. / The soul is the bride, and death is the groom. He will marry her and take her away. / After the body sends her away with its own hands, whose neck will it embrace? / The bridge to hell is narrower than a hair; haven't you heard of it with your ears? / Fareed, the call has come; be careful now — don't let yourself be robbed."

**The difficulty of the spiritual path** (Shalok 2):
> "Fareed, it is so difficult to become a humble Saint at the Lord's Door. / I am so accustomed to walking in the ways of the world. I have tied and picked up the bundle; where can I go to throw it away?"

**Regret at wasted opportunity** (Shalok 4, Ang 1377):
> "Fareed, if I had known that I had so few sesame seeds, I would have been more careful with them in my hands. / If I had known that my Husband Lord was so young and innocent, I would not have been so arrogant."

**Conscience over external appearance** (Shalok 6, Ang 1377):
> "Fareed, if you have a keen understanding, then do not write black marks against anyone else. / Look underneath your own collar instead."

**Non-retaliation** (Shalok 7):
> "Fareed, do not turn around and strike those who strike you with their fists. / Kiss their feet, and return to your own home."

**Procrastination as spiritual ruin** (Shalok 8):
> "Fareed, when there was time for you to earn good karma, you were in love with the world instead. / Now, death has a strong foothold; when the load is full, it is taken away."

**Old age as the body's ripening toward death** (Shalok 9):
> "See, Fareed, what has happened: your beard has become grey. / That which is coming is near, and the past is left far behind."

**When sweetness turns to poison** (Shalok 10):
> "See, Fareed, what has happened: sugar has become poison. / Without my Lord, who can I tell of my sorrow?"

**Aging senses** (Shalok 11):
> "Fareed, my eyes have become weak, and my ears have become hard of hearing. / The body's crop has become ripe and turned color."

**Love the Lord when hair is black; do not wait** (Shalok 12):
> "Fareed, those who did not enjoy their Spouse when their hair was black — hardly any of them enjoy Him when their hair turns grey. / So be in love with the Lord, so that your color may ever be new."

**Response by Mehl 3 (Guru Amar Das)** (Shalok 13):
> "Fareed, whether one's hair is black or grey, our Lord and Master is always here if one remembers Him. / This loving devotion to the Lord does not come by one's own efforts, even though all may long for it. / This cup of loving devotion belongs to our Lord and Master; He gives it to whomever He likes."

**Beauty consumed by death** (Shalok 14, Ang 1378):
> "Fareed, those eyes which have enticed the world — I have seen those eyes. / Once, they could not endure even a bit of mascara; now, the birds hatch their young in them!"

**Grass on the path — radical humility** (Shalok 16):
> "Fareed, become the grass on the path, if you long for the Lord of all. / One will cut you down, and another will trample you underfoot; / then, you shall enter the Court of the Lord."

**Praising the dust** (Shalok 17):
> "Fareed, do not slander the dust; nothing is as great as dust. / When we are alive, it is under our feet, and when we are dead, it is above us."

**Greed destroys love** (Shalok 18):
> "Fareed, when there is greed, what love can there be? When there is greed, love is false."

**God abides within, not in the jungle** (Shalok 19):
> "Fareed, why do you wander from jungle to jungle, crashing through the thorny trees? / The Lord abides in the heart; why are you looking for Him in the jungle?"

**Aged beyond capacity** (Shalok 20, Ang 1378–1379):
> "Fareed, with these small legs, I crossed deserts and mountains. / But today, Fareed, my water jug seems hundreds of miles away."

**Long painful nights** (Shalok 21, Ang 1379):
> "Fareed, the nights are long, and my sides are aching in pain. / Cursed are the lives of those who place their hopes in others."

**Separation as burning** (Shalok 22):
> "Fareed, if I had been there when my friend came, I would have made myself a sacrifice to him. / Now my flesh is burning red on the hot coals."

**Plant what you wish to reap** (Shalok 23):
> "Fareed, the farmer plants acacia trees, and wishes for grapes. / He is spinning wool, but he wishes to wear silk."

**The mud-soaked path to the Beloved** (Shaloks 24–25):
> "Fareed, the path is muddy, and the house of my Beloved is so far away. / If I go out, my blanket will get soaked, but if I remain at home, then my heart will be broken."
> "My blanket is soaked, drenched with the downpour of the Lord's Rain. / I am going out to meet my Friend, so that my heart will not be broken."

**The turban and the head** (Shalok 26):
> "Fareed, I was worried that my turban might become dirty. / My thoughtless self did not realize that one day, dust will consume my head as well."

**Nothing surpasses God in sweetness** (Shalok 27):
> "Fareed: sugar cane, candy, sugar, molasses, honey and buffalo's milk — all these things are sweet, but they are not equal to You."

**Voluntary poverty as spiritual integrity** (Shaloks 28–29):
> "Fareed, my bread is made of wood, and hunger is my appetizer. / Those who eat buttered bread, will suffer in terrible pain."
> "Eat dry bread, and drink cold water. / Fareed, if you see someone else's buttered bread, do not envy him for it."

**The deserted bride** (Shalok 30):
> "This night, I did not sleep with my Husband Lord, and now my body is suffering in pain. / Go and ask the deserted bride, how she passes her night."

**Biraha (separation) as the ruler of all** (Shalok 36):
> "Many talk of the pain and suffering of separation; O pain, you are the ruler of all. / Fareed, that body, within which love of the Lord does not well up — look upon that body as a cremation ground."

**Time wasted** (Shalok 38):
> "Fareed, the hours of the day are lost wandering around, and the hours of the night are lost in sleep. / God will call for your account, and ask you why you came into this world."

**The gong and the body** (Shaloks 39–40):
> "Fareed, you have gone to the Lord's Door. Have you seen the gong there? / This blameless object is being beaten — imagine what is in store for us sinners!"
> "This beautiful body is like the gong; it passes the night in pain."

**Sheikh Farid's old age** (Shalok 41, Ang 1380):
> "Shaykh Fareed has grown old, and his body has begun to tremble. / Even if he could live for hundreds of years, his body will eventually turn to dust."

**Do not make me beg** (Shalok 42):
> "Fareed begs, O Lord, do not make me sit at another's door. / If this is the way you are going to keep me, then go ahead and take the life out of my body."

**I long for You; you long for charcoal** (Shalok 43):
> "Fareed, I long for my Lord; you long only for the charcoal."

**Great wealth and great poverty** (Shalok 44):
> "Fareed, some have lots of flour, while others do not even have salt. / When they go beyond this world, it shall be seen, who will be punished."

**Graves of the mighty** (Shalok 45):
> "Drums were beaten in their honor, there were canopies above their heads, and bugles announced their coming. / They have gone to sleep in the cemetery, buried like poor orphans."

**False commerce, the grave** (Shalok 46):
> "Fareed, those who built houses, mansions and lofty buildings, are also gone. / They made false deals, and were dropped into their graves."

**Religious hypocrisy** (Shalok 50):
> "Fareed, you wear your prayer shawl on your shoulders and the robes of a Sufi; your words are sweet, but there is a dagger in your heart. / Outwardly, you look bright, but your heart is dark as night."

**The body imbued with God has no blood** (Shalok 51):
> "Fareed, not even a drop of blood would issue forth, if someone cut my body. / Those bodies which are imbued with the Lord — those bodies contain no blood."

**Response by Mehl 3** on blood imagery (Shalok 52):
> "This body is all blood; without blood, this body could not exist. / Those who are imbued with their Lord, do not have the blood of greed in their bodies. / When the Fear of God fills the body, it becomes thin; the blood of greed departs from within. / Just as metal is purified by fire, the Fear of God removes the filthy residues of evil-mindedness."

**The genuine sacred pool** (Shalok 53):
> "Fareed, seek that sacred pool, in which the genuine article is found. / Why do you bother to search in the pond? Your hand will only sink into the mud."

**Old age arrives before the soul's union** (Shalok 54):
> "Fareed, when she is young, she does not enjoy her Husband. When she grows up, she dies. / Lying in the grave, the soul-bride cries, 'I did not meet You, my Lord.'"

**Grey hair and the mind's folly** (Shalok 55):
> "Fareed, your hair has turned grey, your beard has turned grey, and your moustache has turned grey. / O my thoughtless and insane mind, why are you indulging in pleasures?"

**The numbered days** (Shalok 56):
> "Fareed, how long can you run on the rooftop? You are asleep to your Husband Lord — give it up! / The days which were allotted to you are numbered, and they are passing, passing away."

**Do not attach to mansions** (Shalok 57):
> "Fareed, houses, mansions and balconies — do not attach your consciousness to these. / When these collapse into heaps of dust, none of them will be your friend."

**Death as the powerful enemy** (Shalok 58):
> "Fareed, do not focus on mansions and wealth; center your consciousness on death, your powerful enemy. / Remember that place where you must go."

**Respond to evil with goodness** (Shalok 78, Ang 1382):
> "Fareed, answer evil with goodness; do not fill your mind with anger. / Your body shall not suffer from any disease, and you shall obtain everything."

**The world-garden and departure** (Shalok 79):
> "Fareed, the bird is a guest in this beautiful world-garden. / The morning drums are beating — get ready to leave!"

**Musk distributed at night; the sleeping do not receive it** (Shalok 80):
> "Fareed, musk is released at night. Those who are sleeping do not receive their share. / Those whose eyes are heavy with sleep — how can they receive it?"

**Universal suffering** (Shalok 81):
> "Fareed, I thought that I was in trouble; the whole world is in trouble!"

**The Creator within the Creation** (Response by Mehl 5, Shalok 75, Ang 1381):
> "Fareed, the Creator is in the Creation, and the Creation abides in God. / Whom can we call bad? There is none without Him."

**The womb at birth** (Shalok 76):
> "Fareed, if on that day when my umbilical cord was cut, my throat had been cut instead, / I would not have fallen into so many troubles, or undergone so many hardships."

**The body's failing faculties** (Shalok 77):
> "My teeth, feet, eyes and ears have stopped working. / My body cries out, 'Those whom I knew have left me!'"

**The stone pillow** (Shalok 67, Ang 1382):
> "Fareed, a stone will be your pillow, and the earth will be your bed. The worms shall eat into your flesh. / Countless ages will pass, and you will still be lying on one side."

**Death personified — in which house today?** (Shalok 68):
> "Fareed, your beautiful body shall break apart, and the subtle thread of the breath shall be snapped. / In which house will the Messenger of Death be a guest today?"

**Namaaz / five daily prayers** (Shaloks 70–72):
> "Fareed: O faithless dog, this is not a good way of life. / You never come to the mosque for your five daily prayers."
> "Rise up, Fareed, and cleanse yourself; chant your morning prayer. / The head which does not bow to the Lord — chop off and remove that head."

**Where are your parents now?** (Shalok 73):
> "Fareed, where are your mother and father, who gave birth to you? / They have left you, but even so, you are not convinced that you shall also have to go."

**Flatten the mind's hills and valleys** (Shalok 74):
> "Fareed, flatten out your mind; smooth out the hills and valleys. / Hereafter, the fires of hell shall not even approach you."

### Hermeneutical Character of the Farid Shaloks

The Farid shaloks operate within the **Sufi tradition of *zuhd*** (asceticism) and ***faqr*** (holy poverty) while being embedded within a Sikh scripture. They are not explicitly monotheistic in the Islamic kalam sense — they address the divine as *Sā'īn* (Lord/Master) and *Rabb* (Lord), and include Islamic *namaaz* references (Shalok 70), but the scriptural context renders them as expressions of universal devotion compatible with Sikh theology. The responsive verses by Mehl 3 and Mehl 5 engage directly with Farid's imagery, sometimes completing his theological move (e.g., on blood and greed in Shalok 52), demonstrating the Sikh practice of inter-traditional dialogue within a single scripture.

The dominant Sufi concepts present: *biraha* (separation-longing), the soul as bride, *faqr* (voluntary poverty), death-consciousness, and critique of *riyā'* (ostentation in religious practice, Shalok 50).

---

## Shalok Mehl 9 (Angs 1426–1429)

### Overview

Fifty-seven shaloks by [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]] (Mehl 9). This is among the most memorized and liturgically used sections of the SGGS outside the Nitnem prayers. The collection is introduced with the Ik Oankaar invocation and the heading *Salok Mehlaa 9* (Shalok, Ninth Mehl).

Each shalok follows a two-verse structure: an observation about the human condition or qualities of the liberated person, followed by "Says Nanak, listen, mind: [the practical implication]." This refrain structure (*Kaho Nanak sun re manā*) functions as both personal address and homiletic urgency — the speaker speaks to his own mind as much as to the listener.

The collection ends (Shaloks 53–57) with a *Dohraa* couplet form, then closes at Shalok 57 with the colophon "||57||1||".

### Theological Themes

**1. Life rendered useless without the Name** (Shalok 1, Ang 1426):
> "If you do not sing the Praises of the Lord, your life is rendered useless. / Says Nanak, meditate, vibrate upon the Lord; immerse your mind in Him, like the fish in the water."

**2. Non-attachment as the mark of the wise** (Shaloks 3–4, Ang 1426):
> "Your youth has passed away like this, and old age has overtaken your body. / Says Nanak, meditate, vibrate upon the Lord; your life is fleeting away!"
> "You have become old, and you do not understand that death is overtaking you. / Says Nanak, you are insane! Why do you not remember and meditate on God?"

**3. Nothing accompanies the soul at death** (Shalok 5):
> "Your wealth, spouse, and all the possessions which you claim as your own — none of these shall go along with you in the end. O Nanak, know this as true."

**4. The Lord is always with you** (Shalok 6):
> "He is the Saving Grace of sinners, the Destroyer of fear, the Master of the masterless. / Says Nanak, realize and know Him, who is always with you."

**5. Ingratitude — given body and wealth but not in love** (Shalok 7):
> "He has given you your body and wealth, but you are not in love with Him. / Says Nanak, you are insane! Why do you now shake and tremble so helplessly?"

**6. The Lord as sole Giver of peace** (Shalok 9, Ang 1426):
> "The Lord is the Giver of all peace and comfort. There is no other at all. / Says Nanak, listen, mind: meditating in remembrance on Him, salvation is attained."

**7. The body as five elements — merge back into the One** (Shalok 11, Ang 1427):
> "Your body is made up of the five elements; you are clever and wise — know this well. / Believe it — you shall merge once again into the One, O Nanak, from whom you originated."

**8. The Lord abides in every heart** (Shalok 12):
> "The Dear Lord abides in each and every heart; the Saints proclaim this as true. / Says Nanak, meditate and vibrate upon Him, and you shall cross over the terrifying world-ocean."

**9. Portrait of the liberated person** (Shaloks 13–19, Ang 1427): An extended description:
- "One who is not touched by pleasure or pain, greed, emotional attachment and egotistical pride — says Nanak, listen, mind: he is the very image of God." (Shalok 13)
- "One who is beyond praise and slander, who looks upon gold and iron alike — says Nanak, listen, mind: know that such a person is liberated." (Shalok 14)
- "One who is not affected by pleasure or pain, who looks upon friend and enemy alike — says Nanak, listen, mind: know that such a person is liberated." (Shalok 15)
- "One who does not frighten anyone, and who is not afraid of anyone else — says Nanak, listen, mind: call him spiritually wise." (Shalok 16)
- "One who has forsaken all sin and corruption, who wears the robes of neutral detachment — says Nanak, listen, mind: good destiny is written on his forehead." (Shalok 17)
- "One who renounces Maya and possessiveness and is detached from everything — says Nanak, listen, mind: God abides in his heart." (Shalok 18)
- "That mortal, who forsakes egotism, and realizes the Creator Lord — says Nanak, that person is liberated." (Shalok 19)

**10. The Naam in Kali Yuga** (Shalok 20):
> "In this Dark Age of Kali Yuga, the Name of the Lord is the Destroyer of fear, the Eradicator of evil-mindedness. / Night and day, O Nanak, whoever vibrates and meditates on the Lord's Name, sees all of his works brought to fruition."

**11. The world as dream and bubble** (Shaloks 23, 25, Ang 1427):
> "Like a dream and a show, so is this world, you must know. / None of this is true, O Nanak, without God."
> "As the bubbles in the water well up and disappear again, so is the universe created; says Nanak, listen, O my friend!"

**12. The rare soul who keeps the Lord in mind** (Shalok 24):
> "Night and day, for the sake of Maya, the mortal wanders constantly. / Among millions, O Nanak, there is scarcely anyone, who keeps the Lord in his consciousness."

**13. The human body as rare opportunity** (Shalok 27):
> "If you yearn for eternal peace, then seek the Sanctuary of the Lord. / Says Nanak, listen, mind: this human body is difficult to obtain."

**14. The Lord-servant identity** (Shalok 29, Ang 1428):
> "That mortal who meditates and vibrates upon the Lord night and day — know him to be the embodiment of the Lord. / There is no difference between the Lord and the humble servant of the Lord; O Nanak, know this as true."

This is a theologically significant claim: the devotee who is immersed in the Lord becomes non-different from the Lord — an assertion of unity through devotion.

**15. Those who save themselves save others** (Shalok 22):
> "That mortal who renounces possessiveness, greed, emotional attachment and egotism — says Nanak, he himself is saved, and he saves many others as well."

**16. Ritual without inner humility is useless** (Shalok 46):
> "Those who make pilgrimages to sacred shrines, observe ritualistic fasts and make donations to charity while still taking pride in their minds — O Nanak, their actions are useless, like the elephant, who takes a bath, and then rolls in the dust."

**17. The final signs of old age; still not savoring the Lord** (Shalok 47):
> "The head shakes, the feet stagger, and the eyes become dull and weak. / Says Nanak, this is your condition. And even now, you have not savored the sublime essence of the Lord."

**18. Only devotional worship is permanent** (Shalok 48, Ang 1429):
> "I had looked upon the world as my own, but no one belongs to anyone else. / O Nanak, only devotional worship of the Lord is permanent; enshrine this in your mind."

**19. The world as a wall of sand** (Shalok 49):
> "The world and its affairs are totally false; know this well, my friend. / Says Nanak, it is like a wall of sand; it shall not endure."

**20. Raam Chand and Raawan, both gone** (Shalok 50):
> "Raam Chand passed away, as did Raawan, even though he had lots of relatives. / Says Nanak, nothing lasts forever; the world is like a dream."

**21. The only real thing** (Shalok 52):
> "Whatever has been created shall be destroyed; everyone shall perish, today or tomorrow. / O Nanak, sing the Glorious Praises of the Lord, and give up all other entanglements."

**22. Dohraa — surrender and rescue** (Shaloks 53–55):
> "My strength is exhausted, and I am in bondage; I cannot do anything at all. / Says Nanak, now, the Lord is my Support; He will help me, as He did the elephant." (Shalok 53)
> "My strength has been restored, and my bonds have been broken; now, I can do everything. / Nanak: everything is in Your hands, Lord; You are my Helper and Support." (Shalok 54)
> "My associates and companions have all deserted me; no one remains with me. / Says Nanak, in this tragedy, the Lord alone is my Support." (Shalok 55)

**23. The Naam, the Holy, and the Guru remain** (Shalok 56):
> "The Naam remains; the Holy Saints remain; the Guru, the Lord of the Universe, remains. / Says Nanak, how rare are those who chant the Guru's Mantra in this world."

**24. Final shalok — the Name enshrined in the heart** (Shalok 57):
> "I have enshrined the Lord's Name within my heart; there is nothing equal to it. / Meditating in remembrance on it, my troubles are taken away; I have received the Blessed Vision of Your Darshan." ||57||1||

### Hermeneutical Character

Shalok Mehl 9 is the fullest expression of what might be called Guru Tegh Bahadur's theological signature: a **philosophy of detachment through awareness of impermanence**, issuing in unconditional reliance on the Name. The shaloks do not develop argument; they present *drishti* (vision) — a way of seeing the world that produces equanimity. The liberation described (Shaloks 13–19) is thoroughly non-dual in character: the liberated person is "the very image of God," and the devoted servant is "non-different from the Lord." This approaches an advaitic soteriology delivered through Bhakti practice.

The recurring call "kaho Nānak sun re manā" ("Says Nanak, listen, mind") signals that the speaker is addressing his own mind — a practice of inner self-address consistent with the SGGS's general practice of the Guru speaking simultaneously as teacher to others and as devotee to God.

---

## Mundaavanee (Ang 1429)

### The Scripture's Formal Seal

*Mundaavanee* means "the seal" — a lock placed on what is complete. This three-verse composition by Mehl 5 (Guru Arjan) functions as the formal closing statement of the SGGS. Introduced as *Mundaavanee Mehlaa 5*, it follows immediately after the final shalok of Shalok Mehl 9.

**Full text** (Ang 1429):

> "Upon this Plate, three things have been placed: Truth, Contentment and Contemplation. / The Ambrosial Nectar of the Naam, the Name of our Lord and Master, has been placed upon it as well; it is the Support of all. / One who eats it and enjoys it shall be saved. / This thing can never be forsaken; keep this always and forever in your mind. / The dark world-ocean is crossed over, by grasping the Feet of the Lord; O Nanak, it is all the extension of God." ||1||

This is followed immediately by a closing *Shalok Mehlaa 5*:

> "I have not appreciated what You have done for me, Lord; only You can make me worthy. / I am unworthy — I have no worth or virtues at all. You have taken pity on me. / You took pity on me, and blessed me with Your Mercy, and I have met the True Guru, my Friend. / O Nanak, if I am blessed with the Naam, I live, and my body and mind blossom forth." ||1||

### Theological Summary Function

The Mundaavanee encapsulates the SGGS's entire teaching in three elements placed on a *thāl* (plate/dish):
1. **Sat** (Truth) — the nature of ultimate reality
2. **Santokh** (Contentment) — the inner disposition required
3. **Vichāro** (Contemplation/Discernment) — the practice of reflection

To these three is added the **Amrit Naam** (Ambrosial Name of the Lord) as the supreme nourishment. The image of eating (*khāvai, bhunchai*) figures the Naam as spiritual food that saves whoever consumes it. The phrase "it is all the extension of God" (*sabh Nānak Braham pasāro*) — literally, "all is the expansion of Brahm/God" — asserts a non-dual ontology as the theological ground of the entire scripture.

The Mundaavanee is significant in the Sikh tradition because it represents Guru Arjan's own declaration of the scripture's completeness. It is understood as the Guru's own seal on the canon.

---

## Raag Maalaa (Angs 1429–1430)

### Structure

The Raag Maalaa ("garland/list of raags") is a versified catalogue of the raag families used throughout the SGGS. It begins with the Ik Oankaar invocation and the heading *Raag Maalaa*. It follows the pattern:

> "Each Raga has five wives, and eight sons, who emit distinctive notes."

Six principal raag families are then listed with their wives (*raagnis*, also called *raagans*) and sons (*putar*/*nandan*):

1. **Bhairao** — wives: Bhairavee, Bilaavalee, Punni-aakee, Bangalee, Asalaykhee; sons: Pancham, Harakh, Disaakh, Bangaalam, Madh, Maadhav, Lalat, Bilaaval (Ang 1429)
2. **Maalakausak** — wives: Gondakaree, Dayv Gandhaaree, Gandhaaree, Seehutee, Dhanaasaree; sons: Maaroo, Masta-ang, Mayvaaraa, Prabal, Chandakausak, Khau, Khat, Bauraanad (Ang 1429–1430)
3. **Hindol** — wives: Taylangee, Darvakaree, Basantee, Sandoor, Aheeree; sons: Surmaanand, Bhaaskar, Chandrabinb, Mangalan, Sarasbaan, Binodaa, Basant, Kamodaa (Ang 1430)
4. **Deepak** — wives: Kachhaylee, Patamanjaree, Todee, Kaamodee, Goojaree; sons: Kaalankaa, Kuntal, Raamaa, Kamalakusam, Champak, Gauraa, Kaanaraa, Kaylaanaa (Ang 1430)
5. **Siree Raag** — wives: Bairaaree, Karnaatee, Gawree, Aasaavaree, Sindhavee; sons: Saaloo, Saarang, Saagaraa, Gond, Gambheer, Gund, Kumb, Hameer (Ang 1430)
6. **Maygh Raag** — wives include Sorat'h, Gond, Malaaree, Aasaa... (text continues to Ang 1430)

### Canonical Status — Disputed

The Raag Maalaa is the **most contested section** of the SGGS from the standpoint of canon. Two positions exist within the Sikh tradition:

**Position 1 (traditional/SGPC)**: The Raag Maalaa is an integral part of the SGGS and must be read in full as part of the Akhand Path (continuous reading). Its inclusion by Guru Gobind Singh in the final recension constitutes canonical status. The SGPC *Sikh Rehat Maryada* affirms this position.

**Position 2 (reform/Tat Khalsa-influenced)**: The Raag Maalaa is a later interpolation, possibly derived from the *Ragamala* of the *Hanuman Nat* or other sources. It is a technical appendix (a musicological list) not composed by any Guru, lacks the spiritual content of the rest of the SGGS, and was not part of the original 1604 compilation. Some Sikh groups (particularly Akhand Kirtani Jatha) do not recite it as part of the scripture's conclusion.

The text itself provides no authorial attribution (no Mehl number, no "Nanak" signature), which is unusual for SGGS compositions and is cited by those questioning its authenticity. Its content is purely enumerative — a taxonomy of raags — with no theological statement.

The SGGS as printed ends on **Ang 1430** with the Raag Maalaa's conclusion. The page count of 1430 is fixed and standardized across all printed editions worldwide; the debate concerns not the page count but the status of this final section within the category of *Gurbani* (the Guru's Word).

## Cross-Links

- [[guru-tegh-bahadur|Guru Tegh Bahadur]] — sole author of Raag Jaijaavantee and Shalok Mehl 9
- [[guru-arjan|Guru Arjan]] — author of Mundaavanee, Shalok Sehskritee (Mehl 5), Gaat'haa, Phunhay; compiled the SGGS
- [[sheikh-farid|Sheikh Farid]] — author of Shalok Farid; Sufi saint whose shaloks are included as Bhagat bāṇī
- [[kabir|Kabir]] — present in Shalok Vaaran Tay Vadheek (referenced in opening Shalok section)
- [[haumai|Haumai]] — egotism; explicitly named as root of birth-death cycle in Shalok Mehl 9
- [[hukam|Hukam]] — the Lord's Will; implicit in "whatever pleases God comes to pass" (Shalok 39)
- [[nam-simaran|Naam/Simran]] — the central practice urged throughout all sections
- [[akal-purakh|Akal Purakh]] — the Undying, Beyond Birth; invoked in Mool Mantar opening each section
- [[guru-granth-sahib|Guru Granth Sahib]] — the text these sections conclude
- [[sikhism|Sikhism]] — tradition to which these texts belong; Raag Maalaa debate is a live intra-tradition controversy (see [[who-is-a-sikh|Who Is a Sikh?]] page for related canon debates)
